mr president, madam president-in-office of the council, mr president of the commission! as a german-speaking belgian and therefore a neighbour, i am pleased to welcome the german presidency of the council, because we know its commitment to europe. as a frontier resident, i can say for myself that i was fortunate to grow up in different languages and cultures, and europe has given us the freedom to live in this diversity. we took advantage of that freedom. we have brought people together in everyday life, in work, in education or even in leisure. we have also used european programmes such as interreg, leonardo and socrates, and so regions have been created on the borders which are in the european constitution. this will also be the recipe for the future: europe is not being built behind closed doors, but together with the people. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we must build bridges between the 27 member states. i believe that you, madam president-in-office, have a particular sensitivity to the old and the new europe. i also hope that we, as pro-europeans, will be able to give an answer to the many questions of the citizens and not leave it to those who preach only disappointment or intolerance. we stand by you and wish you every success in your presidency. (applause from the centre)
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, it is pleasure to see you in the chair, i congratulate you. to demonstrate clearly that this is not a strictly bulgarian issue, the bulgarian alde meps have requested colleagues of other nationalities to speak on it, which, of course, we all gladly do. as you know, five citizens of the european union – and i stress this – are languishing in a libyan jail. since 1999, five bulgarian nurses, a palestinian doctor and nine libyans have been detained on charges that are now well known. on 19 december 2006, the detainees were again sentenced to death. we express our total opposition to the death sentence. we protest strongly against this wrongful conviction. we highlight the disregard for the conclusions of renowned international hiv/aids experts, who stated that the hiv infections were due to an in-hospital infection that began long before the bulgarian nurses arrived. we also express our concern with regard to the allegations of torture. the european union and the european parliament, in particular, must monitor this case very closely. the commission and the council must keep the european parliament informed of any developments at all stages, and next week at their meeting the member states’ ministers of foreign affairs must put this affair very high on their agenda. finally, libya should realise that her standing in the world and her relationship with the union and its member states is at stake. libya should not forsake this opportunity to demonstrate that she abides by the fundamental principles of international law and human rights. most of all, libya must be aware that we are all in full solidarity with the bulgarian nurses and the palestinian doctor.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. one: more than 500,000 people die every year from conventional weapons. that's one human life per minute, 90 human lives during this debate. two , you 're not . one in three countries spends more on defense than on health care. three , please . countries in asia, africa, the middle east and latin america spend an average of 22 billion dollars on weapons. this money will help to achieve the millennium development goals, such as universal primary education and the elimination of child mortality. the report also calls for the eu to be given a role in the fight against terrorism and for the eu to be able to play a more active role in the fight against terrorism. the linking of this endeavour to the lifting of the arms embargo against china is, i think, an immoral position. finally, mr president, the report rightly calls for an international arms trade convention to be concluded within the un. i hope that my country, mr president, as a member of the security council, will also pay special attention to this.
the purpose of the reports before us is to make european railways more attractive to users. one of the things the european union has been encouraging for years is the opening up of the market, bringing more investment to the railways and improving the quality of services. that is the task for which this regulation on passenger rights is also suitable. we have come a long way since the commission's proposal at first reading and also with the council's common position and the vote in our committee. i would like to thank the colleagues who have contributed to it, and especially the shadow rapporteurs, for their contribution to this report. i think we are now putting together a final and feasible set of rights, regulating the liability of railway companies in the event of an accident - i hope there never is one, but if there is one, it should be regulated in the same way throughout the union. in which we arrange for the reception and compensation for delays in the event of a train disruption. in which we regulate the accessibility of trains and stations for people who are less mobile, people who are disabled; where we also deal with luggage or with bigger things like a bike or a stroller; i would like to ask the commissioner to give us a clear answer to this question, and i hope that he will give us a clear answer to it. but we do guarantee that tickets are very widely accessible and available to people who want to take the train. in which we also say that information to passengers must be clear and accessible. in which we also say that there should be a complaints system that is known to everyone and that is also easily accessible to everyone. and - i would ask the president-in-office to persuade her colleagues to add this point - we think that the whole system of rights should also be made clear to the citizens and to the rail passengers in the stations and on the train. i think we have put together a very balanced set of rights. the council has not yet taken a decision on the matter, but it is not yet ready to take a decision on the matter. but there is one point, madam president-in-office, one important point of difference between the council and parliament: we do not understand why all these rights should not apply to all rail passengers. if someone has an accident and is injured, does it matter whether he is on a train that crosses a national border or not? liability should be regulated in a clear and comprehensible way for all rail passengers in the union. if i want to be on a train in a wheelchair, then that train must be accessible or the station must be accessible, and it does not matter whether the train i want to be on is a train running from hanover to amsterdam or from hanover to berlin. so i would ask the council to abandon its position and to ensure that all railway workers get the rights they deserve. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the european union. he also knows that by taking the train he can enjoy the diversity of the european union with peace of mind. and this diversity of the european union, as mrs merkel said this morning, is the soul of europe, and we would therefore like to encourage everyone to get to know this soul of europe by train, if possible.
i would like to begin by thanking the colleagues who, from far and near, have contributed to the result we are discussing today. as you know, it won't end here. we'll probably settle. that's gonna take some work. on the report by mr sterckx, i would like to repeat what i said in the first reading, namely that we have managed to reach consensus across party lines on the main issues relating to passenger rights. the gap with the council, which wants to restrict these rights to international passenger transport, can perhaps be bridged by the transitional measures we want to provide for situations where public service contracts already regulate these passenger rights. at the same time, we must ensure that these exceptions are clearly defined and thus prevent this regulation from becoming an empty box. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. the debate on this subject varies considerably from one country to another, not only between political groups but also internally, given the situation on the railway market and the potential consequences of liberalising the national railway market. and i very much regret, and this is a personal view, that a majority of my colleagues, at least in the committee on transport, have expressed their support for the liberalization of national passenger transport. this is something that is not really being asked for by the industry and the consequences of which have not been properly studied. neither the commission nor the council, trade unions, consumer organizations or the association of european railway undertakings want this liberalisation to be approved today. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. for the simple reason that new competitors will naturally only be interested in the rare profitable lines on the railway market, with a significant economic loss for the public service operator, which will have to tap new revenue to maintain the small unprofitable lines or decide to phase them out. it would therefore have been better to look at the question of liberalisation together with the meijer report, commissioner, as you have already mentioned, so that we have clear and uniform procedures which allow member states or regions to allocate parts of the network to one specific company in the context of public service contracts. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i have also said on many occasions that i do not believe that market liberalization is in itself the panacea for bringing more people on the right track. in belgium, for example, the number of travellers has increased dramatically in recent years. last year it was 6.6%. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the railway package is an interesting trilogy, and each trilogy has a little bit of drama in it. those who know mr jarzembowski know that he always likes to make sure that his proposals are not boring! the first two proposals have found a very broad consensus in parliament. i think it is very good that we are talking about the liberalization of the railways and discussing the accompanying measures at an early stage. it would be a pity if we were to make the same mistake here as we did with road transport, i.e. to liberalize first and then examine where there is still a need for regulation. i think that the same applies to the eu train driving licence. of course, the training should be of equal quality throughout europe, including for the personnel directly responsible for security tasks. it will be difficult to build a european rail network with different criteria and different requirements for train drivers from one country to another. mr savary has largely expressed the views of parliament and i should like to thank him for his report. the same applies to the rights of passengers. in simple terms, it would suffice to say that rail transport should be punctual and of good quality. in many countries, these two requirements would have been welcome. but we went further. my concern is that we may be setting the bar a little too high. the quality of services and the rights of passengers must of course be respected, but we must not set our sights too high when we already know the difficulties that it creates in international transport to meet all these requirements. the same rules should apply to international traffic. as the commissioner has already said, it is not possible that when you cross the border, the rights suddenly change from one country to another. the commission has also proposed a number of measures to help people with reduced mobility. i would remind you, however, that the package originally included a fourth proposal which we lost along the way. we must not forget that a certain quality must also be guaranteed in the transport of goods. the fact that we have imposed sanctions here must not lead to the neglect of freight transport everywhere for fear that sanctions will be imposed in other areas. the liberalization of the market: it goes without saying that international transport must also be liberalized. the same criteria must apply in paris, brussels and cologne. the question now arises of the internal market. i come from a small country, belgium, and we have already lost some of our train of thought on this subject, because the important links are cross-border, such as the link from aachen to brussels or from brussels to antwerp and on to the netherlands. but a question - and mr jarzembowski knows this - still arises in the small countries: who is willing and able to pay for these services and how can they be organised? we must answer this question in parallel with the present package. we can do this in the meijer report before we comment on this issue in general. i am following the commissioner's suggestion that we should include the meijer report in the overall package with this proposal by mr jarzembowski.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the railway companies in the member states are able to comply with the requirements of the directive. to counter these problems without changing the substance of the matter, we have divided the amendment into three parts, retaining the bulk of the text but removing the english word 'existing' by the second split vote, and removing the last sentence by the third split vote, thus preserving the body but removing the practical difficulties without changing the substance of the matter.
the rapporteur. - mr jarzembowski also asked why we asked for a split vote on amendment no 47. the second part of amendment no 47 concerns a person travelling by sea free of charge. i have asked for a split vote because there are many objections to this system. and i would like to know - hence the split vote - how many members support this and how many members do not, because in some member states there are great difficulties with this. that is why i am asking for a split vote.
in writing. it is wrong to impose on all the member states from europe how they should organise their national railways. it is good that competition on the railways in germany results in better service, lower prices and good safety, but this does not have to be good for the railway network in belgium. in many countries there is reason for dissatisfaction with railway services. the commission has therefore proposed an examination of the advantages and disadvantages of competition on the railways: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the railways, and has recently introduced a new system of public service compensation for railway undertakings. no one is asking for this kind of liberalization today: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's railways are not subject to the same restrictions as those imposed by the member states. i would have liked, as planned, to see this report dealt with together with that of erik meijer, whereby good procedures can be agreed to allow member states and regions to allocate parts of the network to one specific company in the context of public service contracts: this means the possibility of granting one concession to one service provider for a given period, but with a clearly defined set of requirements for optimal service provision, safety and environmental care.
in writing. i have decided to vote in favour of rejecting the council's common position endorsed by the jarzembowski report. the common position endorses the timetable for international passenger transport liberalization by 2010. i am firmly opposed to this liberalization which, contrary to what the ardent defenders of the "everything for sale" claim, will not generate a better service at a better cost. it will jeopardise the public service missions provided in the member states: the vast majority of users, who will become customers, will face higher fares and lower quality of service. rural areas will once again be the main victims of this process. moreover, liberalisation will put railway undertakings in small member states at risk and will have adverse effects on the working and employment conditions of railway workers throughout the european union. it is therefore in defence of public services, rail users and railway employees throughout europe that i voted for the rejection of the common position and against the amendments in favour of this liberalisation.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the death penalty is not used in countries which have not yet adopted the death penalty. whether it is islamic countries where adulterers are stoned, whether it is bush's america or india, the world's largest democracy, whether it is communist china, it does not matter. i therefore unhesitatingly join the call for a global moratorium. one miscarriage of justice, one innocent life, is enough to make the death penalty barbaric. but i would like to add two concerns to that. respect for life should not prevent a state of law from giving serious criminals an effective and unrelenting sentence of 30 years or life imprisonment. this condition must and can underpin the consensus against the death penalty among the population. it is only in this consensus that our citizens, who are increasingly confronted with the most brutal forms of crime, will support the worldwide abolition of the death penalty. secondly, some opponents of the death penalty must show some consistency. we have recently seen the socialist candidate for president of france, on a visit to friends in the one-party state of china, praising the efficiency of the chinese judiciary. the efficiency, ladies and gentlemen, lies in the fact that the families of the masses of those condemned to death receive the annual bill for the bullet used to kill the victim. ségolène royal had no say in their affairs. they are not bringing in contracts, because the commercial interests of big companies in china have to be defended, and then suddenly we no longer hear from some european opponents of the death penalty. if europe wants to maintain its credibility, it must also put pressure on countries like china, whatever the political or commercial price.
i would like to oppose the black and white terms in which this debate is too often conducted. it also often seems that the united states is an enemy rather than an ally in the fight against terrorism. it is claimed that it is not, but almost every measure taken is contested here. it is true that the swift case raises some legitimate questions, and colleagues from several groups have already raised these questions here. for example, where is the balance between the tracking down of terrorists and the respect of fundamental rights? it is a question that is also being asked in the united states congress. at the end of the day, the fight against terrorism is largely about risk analysis, so it's about chance. the processing of commercial data in addition to purely personal data can make a useful contribution to this. the only thing that matters is to provide safeguards against abuse. parliamentary control must be provided, for example, over the operation of the system, but also over the algorithms used, which must be of the highest confidentiality. penalties should be provided for in the event of abuse. the information obtained may only be used in the context of the fight against terrorism. there should be a point of contact for complaints from the public, etc. it goes without saying that there is still a great deal of consultation with the united states. a clear legal framework is needed, in which all partners must be open to new ideas and new techniques.
president, ladies and gentlemen, the swift debate is clearly part of the wider debate on the difficult balance between the fight against terrorism on the one hand and the protection of fundamental human rights and privacy on the other. but not only is there a clear dividing line between the european and the american approaches, but there is also no agreement on a common definition of terrorism. the commissioner has indicated here how he intends to develop a european instrument to meet this need in the future, but that is not for tomorrow. we are, of course, dealing with the situation from the past and the present and i have some questions about that. to this day, data is being played back into the swift file as if nothing was going on. the conclusions of the reports of both the belgian privacy commission and the european committee are very clear and unanimous: the commission has not yet taken a decision on the application of the directive. the belgian courts have decided not to prosecute swift and the belgian authorities see no loopholes in the continuation of these illegal practices. i would therefore like to ask what europe's reaction is to this. will the commission still initiate infringement proceedings against belgium? if so, when will the decision be taken? if not, what will the commission do to ensure that european privacy rules are respected hic et nunc in all member states and thus also in belgium? finally, at the council meeting on 15 december, the belgian prime minister also raised the swift issue and called for a european approach. i understand from the presidency that the investigation is still ongoing, but i have a very clear question: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
the rapporteur. mr president, first of all i would like to thank my fictitious rapporteurs, mr bushill-matthews, mrs mcavan, mrs evans, mrs sinnott and mrs belohorská, who are present and with whom i must say that the contacts have been particularly intense and fruitful since i was appointed rapporteur nine months ago. i quote some particularly striking figures in this report: 38% of women, 27% of men, almost 5 million children in the union are affected by obesity, with between 300 000 and 400 000 new cases per year for our youngest. it's simple and it's terrible at the same time. in 2000, who identified obesity as the leading health problem in the western world. how could it be otherwise when, on average, 6% of national health budgets are spent on all the pathologies that are caused by overweight: type 2 diabetes, respiratory problems, heart problems and others? beyond these figures, it is important to add and clarify that the most worrying is the upward curve that this disease is experiencing today. the first thing that we must realize, and we all agree with it, is that we have not reacted in time, and when i say 'we', i am referring to governments, health and education authorities and, of course, to us parents. the second, equally obvious, finding is that the effective fight against obesity and all its health consequences is not easy. this requires a holistic, holistic approach that takes into account factors as varied as heredity, lifestyle, and education in balanced and varied diet, and this from an early age. family and professional background are essential factors. it cannot be ignored that insecurity and poverty are factors that sometimes trigger and, in any case, accelerate obesity. third observation: we do not start from nothing. there are currently some 60 initiatives in the field of obesity prevention and physical activity promotion which benefit from or deserve follow-up at european level, for example fleurbaix and laventie and food dudes, to name just two of many. in this fight, as has been said time and again, europe has not only a say, but also an immense contribution to make. if europe is to bring real added value, it must integrate nutrition and physical activity into other community policies in a transversal way, where, fortunately, the major budgets of the european union are concentrated: the common agricultural policy, the seventh framework programme for research and the structural funds. i am also pleased that, in the reform of the cmo for fruit and vegetables launched last week by commissioner fischer boel, the promotion of the consumption of this type of food with a high nutritional value is one of its seven priorities, its seven main objectives. for my part, i would like to reiterate here, as in the report, that i prefer an incentive policy, price cuts, tax reductions or other types of subsidies, to any system which resembles a tax on calorific products of the "fat tax" type which, in the end, would penalise the most modest households in europe. i welcome all the compromises we have made in our work on important points in this report. i will not mention all of them, but i will mention a few. the compromise on paragraph 4, which recommends that all member states should officially recognise obesity as a chronic disease, as the who does: this is a strong demand, supported by the patient associations, which we had to pass on to each other; it is also, and certainly is, the surest way to avoid a form of stigmatization of a population type in our society and to ensure reasonable care by health care systems. the compromise on paragraphs 16 and 17 follows, which recall the importance of school in the learning of a healthy lifestyle, from the school canteen to the sports field, to sum up. finally, the compromise on paragraph 30 on the urgency of simplified and harmonised food labelling - that is our proposal - for products high in salt, sugar and certain fats. i would also like to make it clear that i agree with the socialist group and the greens when they tackle products and drinks with too many calories. yes, we must put an end to this politically correct, a little tiresome, which leads to inaction. who today can believe and say that there is no such thing as good food and bad food from a nutritional standpoint? that is why it is also important that the european union should take the lead in reducing the consumption of saturated fatty acids - that is our paragraph 28 - which are responsible for bad cholesterol and, therefore, a whole host of cardiovascular diseases. i also regret that our discussions have, in my view, focused too much on the question of banning television advertising for products and drinks high in sugar, salt and fat in children's programmes. i believe that this debate was largely decided by our vote on the revision of the television without frontiers directive in december 2006, so very recently. this is precisely what our compromise amendment 5 expresses. i too find it difficult to explain to a mother the coherence of a policy which would prohibit advertising on television for, say, chocolate bars but which would allow the same advertising on the internet or on the big screen, which is the reason for paragraph 24. in conclusion, mr president, i am convinced that we all have the same will. first, to contain this epidemic, to achieve noticeable progress in four or five years, especially among young people. secondly, we must try to reverse this trend by 2015 at the latest, through ambitious policies at all levels of government, including the european level. that is why we expect a lot from you, commissioner kyprianou, and from the future white paper which you will present to us next spring.
member of the commission. mr president, i would like to thank all the members for the very interesting discussion. i am glad to see that we are thinking along the same lines. first of all i would like to say that of course we all agree that it is an individual’s choice at the end of the day. we will not put the food on the consumer’s table, and we cannot make them eat or not eat something. but at the same time we have a responsibility to make sure that consumers can make an informed choice, which means education, that is true, but it also means making sure that the information they receive is accurate, truthful, correct and science-based with reference to health claims. i note the call for nutritional labelling. that is something we are working on. we need to make sure that consumers get sufficient, understandable information so that they can make their choices. that goes for the citizens and the parents who have to make choices for their children, and they do have a responsibility to say ‘no’ every now and then. this is also an important factor, because we do not want to ban any food. i should like to make that clear. we do not want to exclude any food from the diet of the consumers. i cannot imagine a child growing up and never having an ice cream or a chocolate bar, but they do not have to have ten a day, and this is a question of moderation. this is a challenge for all of us. i also understand that advertising to children is an important issue. i think the compromise reached when we discussed the television without frontiers directive is a prudent one, but we are also working, as you know, on self-regulation within the industry to make sure that it is responsible in this respect. we have always said that it is more practical and much faster to go with self-regulation to a great extent, but we have always said in the commission that if that does not work, then we will not hesitate to come back and take more binding measures. i am sorry mrs breyer is not here, but i have to say that this commission has been very ambitious and courageous on many issues – i am referring to tobacco and alcohol and also to raising these issues. you will remember two years ago, when i first started presenting these issues on behalf of the commission, the reaction that came from various sides. but now we are having this debate. we are now discussing a green paper. it is a strategy that will formulate what the next actions are going to be. product reformulation is a very important factor for us. we are working on and studying the issue of trans fatty acids. i can assure you that i know the situation on that. it is a challenge. we have to make sure that if they are removed, nothing worse replaces them. that is something on which we will be working closely with efsa. the factor that we always have to remember is the social dimension, and the issue of inequality when it comes to health harm arising from obesity, because unfortunately, it does mostly affect lower-income families. that is a reality. so we have an increased responsibility to act in this respect. in conclusion i would like to say that i thank you all again for a very interesting debate. i am looking forward to discussing with you our strategy, once it is presented, and how we can achieve practical results and make it work for the benefit of the european consumers. finally, i would like to thank the rapporteur once again for the excellent work she has done.
the rapporteur. mr president, i am not in the habit of reacting to the words of the far right. i think that it is in tartuffe that molière tells us that not reacting at a given moment is to endorse. i feel obliged to respond here on behalf of the commissioner: mr martinez has nevertheless used the words 'vomit' and 'scandalous' in relation to the green paper and the report. i have been in this parliament for seven years, and i have never heard such terms before. i do not think that this report is particularly controversial or controversial. it seems to me to be quite consensual, as we have heard in the speeches. so i totally deny the accusations, or rather the insults that have been uttered. mr. martinez, very clearly, sees this scourge only through a north-south prism. that lens is his. we must not forget that the european union is a global economy, and that it is not just a world of the rich and the poor, but also a world of the poor and the needy. i would also remind you that in micronesia, in the samoan islands, there are also obese people, and this example is to answer in the same anecdotal tone as that used by mr martinez. i would add in conclusion that i have no lessons to learn, we have no lessons to learn in humanitarian aid and solidarity from someone who, like him, clings to this corner of the political chessboard.
president. - i voted in favour of the resolution on a worldwide moratorium on the death penalty because i am personally an absolute opponent of the death penalty. i do not want to create any misunderstanding. i am against the death penalty, but i am absolutely in favour of a very tough approach to crime. i would like to say that it is because of the laxity and cowardice of many governments in europe, where serious common law criminals, murderers and child rapists, as in my country, are systematically released early, that it is because of that laxity and cowardice that the call for the reintroduction of the death penalty is again being heard in europe. as an opponent of the death penalty, i sympathize with those people and i say that the complaints of those people must be taken into account.
in writing. the list of countries authorising the death penalty is long. the list of victims is even longer: 2,148 people were executed in 2005 alone. what about europe? can we be a european state and at the same time allow the death penalty? no, you can't! the abolition of the death penalty is also mandatory for the countries applying for membership of the european union. we in the european parliament even think that we must go further. that is the meaning of the resolution against the death penalty adopted by parliament today. all the political groups in parliament had already signed the declaration on the moratorium on the death penalty, with a view to universal abolition, with the exception of the far right, which was predictable. the majority required for the adoption of the resolution was reached. an unusual consensus in this parliament! a majority to remind us that joining the eu is not just about agreeing on fishing quotas! it is above all a reminder that europe does not compromise on its fundamental values. europe is at the forefront of the fight to abolish the death penalty. it was worth reminding him of that.
in writing. prolonged poor eating habits and lack of adequate exercise are adding to the burden of obesity on thousands of people in the european union each year. this increases the risk of many serious diseases such as heart disease, high blood pressure and respiratory problems. this not only has a price in terms of public health, but it also costs health insurance in the member states. i therefore advocate an integrated approach to the problem. consumer information and education, the exchange of best practice between member states, clear labelling on foodstuffs or actions in schools to promote healthy lifestyles are all ways of ensuring the necessary behavioural changes. the fight against obesity is not bounded by borders. we can make a positive contribution from europe and learn from each other's approach. cooperation with the member states, industry, the media, education and civil society seems to me to be the appropriate way forward.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i know that it is certainly not the sexiest dossier we have dealt with here and, given the high technical level, everyone assumed that an agreement at first reading was possible. however, this was considered to be beyond the reach of some member states which were absolutely opposed to any harmonisation of spatial environmental information, which were also particularly concerned about fees and permits and saw this initiative as a threat to national monopolies. no less than five council presidencies had inspire on their priority lists, but it was waiting for the finnish presidency, which on the night of 21 november 2006 was able to convince the member states to give up the fight. i would therefore like to take this opportunity to express my sincere thanks to all those who have contributed to this success, starting with the commissioner and his staff. they have already done a lot of preparatory work together with an international team of experts, who are responding with great enthusiasm, so that no time has been lost. but also the shadow rapporteurs and their staff, the legal service of parliament, the secretariat of the committee on the environment and the mediation secretariat, the council presidency and all its staff. this is the procedure, but i should also like to say something about the content. as you know, inspire is setting up an infrastructure for spatial information in europe to support environmental policy at all levels. it will be clear to all that good policy can only be achieved if it is based on reliable and high-quality information. policy makers need to take into account the increasing interconnectedness and complexity of the issues affecting quality of life today when developing new policies. the sixth environmental programme also calls for attention to be paid to this reliable knowledge, but also to informed participation by citizens. the directive, which we hope to adopt tomorrow, sets out the objectives and member states now have two years to complete their national legislation and administrative procedures. in other words, before the next european elections, inspire will be a reality and will be based on the already existing spatial data infrastructure of the various member states. the geoportal provides member states with the possibility of making all geographical data sets available, as a first step towards harmonisation. this idea, which is naturally welcomed by everyone as such, has however triggered heated discussions within some member states which anxiously wanted to protect those fees for the sale of geographical data, but also wanted to impose stricter restrictions on access to information than the aarhus convention provides. it was precisely these two last challenges that prevented parliament from reaching agreement with the council at second reading. i would like to say again to my colleagues that i am very pleased that we were able to obtain during the conciliation procedure that services for searching and consulting spatial data should be made available free of charge in general. only under clearly defined conditions can fees be charged, in order not to jeopardise the financial viability of some public services. as regards intellectual property rights, parliament has chosen to apply strictly the provisions of the aarhus convention on access to information on environmental matters and thus not to allow stricter restrictions on the basis of confidentiality, as requested by the council. i think we have found an honourable compromise and i hope that parliament will also give its full support to this agreement tomorrow so that implementation can really start in the member states. the commission has already made a number of proposals. these are almost inexhaustible if gaps and inconsistencies between european geographical data sets are eliminated, which now often make planning impossible and in some cases even cause chaos. the system is thus essential for improving and managing water quality in europe. the information collected can also contribute to the prevention and control of floods. all in all, i would say that inspire is an important complement to the existing environmental legislation in the european union and we will be following its implementation in the member states with great interest.
i would also like to thank the two rapporteurs for their very open cooperation. i think that the interest of so many people and organisations in this first reading, both in the strategy and in the revision of the directive, shows that waste offers many opportunities. waste is increasingly being seen as a valuable source of raw materials. but waste is also a problem. we 've heard it all before: the commission has also proposed that the member states should be able to take the necessary measures to ensure that the waste is not reused or recycled. i think that the revision presented here certainly contains positive steps towards reducing the harmful effects of waste treatment on the environment and health. i am thinking of the five-step hierarchy, the waste prevention programmes for the member states, a better definition of a number of concepts, criteria for distinguishing products from waste, the introduction of the proximity principle, also the prohibition on mixing waste in order to dilute it and, of course, a procedure to clarify when waste ceases to be waste. however, there are still many bottlenecks. i am thinking, for example, commissioner, of amendment no 85, which states that landfills with gas extraction may be considered as a useful application. i think this is contrary to the policy of stopping the dumping of biodegradable waste. i also think that amendments nos 81 and 82 should not provide for deletion, precisely because amendment no 39 refers to the prohibition of incineration at sea. amendment no 26, which deals with the definition of 'energy recovery', should also be deleted, otherwise many incinerators would be covered by recovery and a free market for waste incineration would be created. this would be completely contrary to the principle of proximity, which is also very important in order to achieve a high recycling rate. finally, i think that the introduction of energy efficiency criteria is not the place to be here either, but in a review of the waste incineration directive. there was also no thorough study of the effects. finally, we must also ensure that certain provisions do not have the effect of reducing the achievements of some member states or of negating the current policy efforts. i am thinking here of my region, flanders, which is at the forefront of reuse and recycling in europe. these high figures are only possible thanks to the involvement of local authorities, but also to a number of accompanying measures such as levies and landfill bans. the flemish land-use planning system, for example, could also be jeopardised by excluding uncontaminated excavated land from the directive. different member states will have different interpretations of this when it comes to cross-border transport. i would have liked to hear the commissioner's opinion on this.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, the rapporteur may not have intended it, but the bozkurt report is in fact the most undeniable illustration of the fact that turkey simply does not belong in europe. it is wrong to think that turkey is just a country where a few things are still wrong and that everything can be solved with some new laws and some new rules or with the absorption of a few tens of thousands of pages of community acquis, after which a new european member state would be created. that is absurd. europeans are first and foremost people who live within a very specific geographical european framework. turkey is not. but europeans are also people who belong to a very distinct community of law and values, including equality between men and women. and yes, it is a matter of culture, mentality and even religion when that religion, as in the case of islam, does not limit itself to inner experience but wants to impose its outdated and usually totally reprehensible rules on the whole community. to remain on the subject of this report, turkey is undoubtedly the most modern islamic country, but it remains a backward country when it comes to women's rights. because it is an islamic country, where women are considered inferior and that will not improve with the progressive islamization, on the contrary. so for the love of god, let's stop running am symptom. turkey is a neighbouring country, but it can never become a european country.
the commission's vice-president mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am pleased to take this opportunity to answer your questions and to explain, perhaps even better than has been done so far, the whole genesis of these provisions. i cannot allow it to be said that these measures were adopted hastily and secretly, mr president. i shall demonstrate this, although i always find the remarks of parliament very useful and have listened to the speakers very carefully. but i would really like to explain first why new rules restricting the presence of liquids in hand luggage on board airplanes were introduced in the community on 6 november 2006. first, why these restrictions? the measures introduced by regulation (ec) no 1546/2006 are intended to address the new threats posed by homemade liquid explosives. we have relied on information from member states, in particular the united kingdom, that liquid explosives are a real threat to civil aviation. last summer, on august 10, 2006, british intelligence revealed a terrorist plot to blow up several aircraft using liquid explosives. the investigation found that the explosives were homemade, made from chemicals that were relatively easy to obtain. police discovered liquid explosives hidden in bottles of soft drinks, seemingly perfectly harmless. the old security measures would have been impossible to detect these explosives. given that information on the manufacture of such bombs may be in circulation, and that the necessary ingredients are easily acquired, it was concluded that the threat posed by these devices was real. the honourable members should know that it is possible to mask the colour and smell of these explosives. they may also be in the form of a gel or cream, as well as a liquid. a solution that was both feasible and effective had to be found. the commission wanted a consistent approach to be applied throughout the european union and a clear understanding of the situation by passengers, so that a uniform level of security could be ensured at all eu airports and on every flight. let us imagine, ladies and gentlemen, that very different rules were adopted in the european union without coordination. think of the confusion that this would have created, especially in the minds of the passengers. this would have made airport security management very problematic. could we avoid these difficult questions? could the commission afford to wait for a real attack to occur on board an aircraft to demonstrate that action was necessary? could the commission have been content to see member states implement a patchwork of disparate security rules which would have made transit through the main european airports difficult and chaotic? the commission has therefore examined this issue in cooperation with aviation safety experts from the member states and with the technical task force of the european civil aviation conference, ceac, which brings together experts from across europe. these experts advised him to limit the permissible capacity of bottles containing liquids to 100 millimetres and the total volume of liquid to that which can fit in a one-litre bag. they considered this sufficient to prevent the entry of a homemade liquid- explosive bomb large enough to cause catastrophic damage to an aircraft. we have had contacts with the american authorities who have reached a similar conclusion, and the approaches adopted on both sides of the atlantic are in every respect compatible. the commission has therefore decided to take the necessary steps to implement this consistent standard throughout the european union, in application of the framework legislation enshrined in community law. i now turn to procedural matters, because i cannot allow it to be said that we have not followed the procedures. a number of honourable members have expressed concern about the way in which the security rules applicable at airports have been revised. i would like to mention the european legal framework. regulation (ec) no 2320/2002 on the establishment of common rules in the field of civil aviation security constitutes the framework legislation. this regulation was adopted by both the council and the european parliament, under the codecision procedure, in the months following the attacks on the world trade center. this regulation establishes the principle that member states must define common basic standards for aviation security measures in order to ensure systematic compliance with these measures throughout the european union. however, as is often the case when a particularly sensitive subject is involved, this framework legislation does not cover all the details of the common standards and the measures envisaged for their implementation. this information is set out in annexes which may be adapted in accordance with comitology procedures. in the case of aviation safety, this use of comitology is necessary if certain details are to be changed in good time to take account of new information. many passages in these technical annexes contain sensitive data, since they specify the detailed rules for the application of ground safety measures at airports. and you can't just release certain specific information about airport security. public access to these detailed enforcement measures could help criminals and potential terrorists circumvent our security measures. for this reason, regulation (ec) no 2320/2002 adopted by the european parliament and the council specifically states that this information is secret and must not be published. the rules on liquids in cabin baggage are laid down in commission regulation (ec) no 1546/2006 and not, mr president, in regulation (ec) no 1448/2006, as stated in the questions. regulation (ec) no 448/2006 also deals with aviation safety but sets standards for explosive detection systems used at airports. at this stage of the debate, may i remind you that the other nine regulations have been adopted by the european union, that these regulations are in force, and that we have followed the same procedures for the tenth regulation under discussion today. the regulation (ec) no 1546/2006 in question has an annex classified as 'european union restricted', which means that it cannot be published in the official journal. however, there is no reason to conclude that the adoption of that regulation was vitiated by irregularities. it has received the approval of the member states of the european union within the air safety regulatory committee. it was following the positive opinion of this committee that parliament, through your committee on transport and tourism, received the draft regulation, in accordance with the comitology procedures laid down in council decision 1999/468/ec and in the framework agreement on relations between the commission and the european parliament. by letter of 4 october 2006, the committee on transport informed me that parliament had exercised its right of scrutiny and that no comments had been made on the draft measures. it should also be noted that the airline and airport representative bodies have fully supported the legislative proposal and actively participated in the development of the rules. we cannot say that this regulation was adopted in secret and undemocratically. frankly, if there was a procedural defect somewhere, i'd be the first to admit it. the next question concerns the transparency of the rules. as i have already said, the rules on aviation security, in particular those established by regulation (ec) no 1546/2006, are not published because potential terrorists could use them as a source of information. in this respect, community rules do not differ from national rules on aviation safety. it should be noted, however, that this regulation is addressed to the competent authority of each member state and that the authorities of the member states are required, pursuant to article 8 (1) of regulation (ec) no 2320/2002 on the establishment of common rules in the field of civil aviation security, to communicate information to interested parties according to their need to know in accordance with the national rules applicable to the dissemination of sensitive information. i must say that considerable efforts have been and must continue to be made to inform air passengers of what they may or may not carry. they are told what they need to know to prepare for their journey and if it appears that this information is not clearly communicated to passengers, the commission will raise this issue with the competent national authority. ladies and gentlemen, i have really become a champion of passenger rights. i have shown this, particularly in relation to airlines, and we are currently carrying out an inquiry - i have reported to parliament on the initiation of this inquiry - to find out whether passengers' rights are really being respected. therefore, i am not, it seems to me, a priori suspected of not being interested in the passengers. i fully understand the message of the questions you have asked me. these questions highlight the difficulty of the problem, but what i would first like to say is that the procedures have been followed to the letter, in accordance with our regulations, and that if there are any difficulties in the application, we ensure and insist that the member states warn us of them and allow us - i will remind you of this in reply to the other speakers - to evaluate this device, which i hope will be temporary, as the development of technology and equipment will make it possible to detect better, in the liquids, the risks i have mentioned. in any case, what i am saying is that safety is still a major element of air transport in particular and that we have had to take these measures not out of a sense of humour; we have had to take these measures because we have been unable to find a solution to the problems of safety in the air. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken the necessary steps to ensure that the measures are implemented in a uniform manner throughout the european union. i have just been to washington last week and i can tell you that we are doing everything in our power to ensure that passengers on both sides of the atlantic are subject to the same rules, otherwise, of course, we will only be postponing the problems of transatlantic flights and transport. i would like to say that the commission has taken the necessary steps to ensure that the commission's proposals are implemented in a way that is consistent with the principles of the treaty.
the problem we are discussing today is complex. it is, after all, a question of finding a good balance between two things which are very close to our hearts and which at times may seem contradictory, namely, on the one hand, the pursuit of the greatest possible degree of effective security and a sense of security and, on the other hand, the protection of fundamental human rights, civil rights, including the right to privacy, freedom of movement, to which we are so accustomed. the consequences of the measures taken were quite spectacular: the commission has also been asked to provide information on the implementation of the directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. it could be said that the rapid reaction of the european security agencies can be interpreted as a sign of efficiency. but on the other hand, we're also faced with two kinds of questions. first of all, the question of the effectiveness of the measures. we all know that there are inevitably a number of exceptions, for example, to medical personnel. we have therefore to ask ourselves whether these measures are actually deterring well-prepared, well-informed terrorists, while the masses are being harmed. i understand that the commission is planning a first evaluation this spring. that is good, this should be done regularly and we should also be aware of the side effects, such as the infringement of freedoms. that balance is in danger of being lost and a democratic debate is necessary, as we are having it tonight. this brings me to a second fundamental question, namely the democratic control of such decisions and therefore the involvement of the european parliament. i am convinced, commissioner, that the commission has formally followed all the rules. i have no doubt about that. in fact, a number of members of the committee on transport - and i was there - were able to examine the legality of the proposal for a regulation individually, alone, without staff, without legal advice, without consultation, a few days before the regulation was formally adopted. we have therefore been able to establish that the committee of experts has taken a decision in accordance with the regulation applicable to this matter. i would like to say, commissioner, that it has become clear from this question that the comitology procedure applied here is inadequate. we must therefore seek together ways of having a genuine democratic debate on these sensitive issues which have a major impact on the way our citizens live. just so we 're clear , i am not making a plea here to decide as a parliament what kind of scanners should be used at airports or when a threat is alarming. it is important, however, to be more involved than today in thorough evaluations and impact assessments, for example. at the same time, i would also like to make a plea for sufficient funds to be provided in the meantime through research and development to be able to detect, as you have just said, this type of liquid explosives so that we can avoid it. i hope, commissioner, that after tonight we can draw some conclusions, and i am counting on your support and experience.
in writing. prevention is better than cure! this saying, which sounds like a desire to act at the source, is perfectly applicable to european waste policy. indeed, what do we see? between 1995 and 2003, the total waste generation increased by 19%, with an average of 3.5 tonnes per person per year. if we add that less than 20% of waste is recycled, that half of the materials at the end of their life are not processed and end up scattered in nature and that, in addition, the proliferation of incinerators and landfills dangerous for the environment and health is not a sustainable policy, the whole philosophy of waste management must be rethought, and this, around 3 strong axes: eco-design as a priority of waste prevention policy since 80% of the environmental impact occurs at the time of manufacture and treatment of the product; the introduction of a dissuasive and unfavourable green tax on packaging and energy products; the closure of the 10 000 landfills on the territory of the european union or their compliance with the 2001 european landfill directive.
mr president, i voted against the bozkurt report. on the one hand, some necessary findings are made, but as a parliament we are leaving out an opportunity to put real pressure on the turkish government. the accession negotiations with turkey could be used as a lever to make progress on the human rights situation, in this case women's rights. but the european parliament seems to be deliberately choosing not to do so. there is a majority in this house who believe that turkey must be able to join the european union at any cost. officially, of course, it is stated that negotiations with turkey can be suspended at any time if it is found that this country is manifestly violating the human rights conditions. however, we have had to establish on several occasions that this suspension of negotiations will never take place. it is a provision which serves only to appease the majority of voters in europe who are against turkey's accession.
in writing. forced marriages, domestic violence, honor killings, illiteracy, etc.: the situation of women in turkey is not rosy! according to unicef, every year 700,000 girls are denied education by their families. the employment rate for women is less than 25%, far from the eu's 55%. these are worrying figures, as turkey's eu membership is a matter of controversy. this is why parliament is proposing constructive measures: an assessment of turkey's progress on the basis of criteria; the creation of shelters for women victims of violence; the compulsory registration of all girls at birth, in order to combat forced marriage; the training of the police and the judiciary in the fight against honour crimes, in order to encourage the systematic opening of investigations and the necessary sentences; the commission has also proposed a system of mandatory quotas for women's participation in political life. i am not a big fan of affirmative action. but i am convinced that temporary measures in turkey are useful. this country is in dire need of women in positions of power. they should serve as models, so that beyond the codes, it is the mindsets that evolve.

mr president, for the greens it is quite clear, as my colleague mr harms has said, that the european council in march will be mainly about energy, and we have demands in this respect. i would like to mention another point which relates to the evolution of the labour market and taxation in europe. two examples. in belgium, instead of losing 4,000 jobs, the volkswagen-forest plant will lose 3,000 and workers will go from a 35-hour week to a 38-hour week for the same pay and greater flexibility. under these conditions, the group's management believes that the plant will be one of the most efficient in europe and will meet the criteria for a successful lisbon strategy. secondly, in france, half of the private sector employees earn less than 1 400 euros a month, mrs royal reminded us this sunday. these facts throw a brilliant light on the european social model. many economists see this change in working conditions and pay as structural, linked to technological innovation and increased globalisation of activities. the majority of european employees will thus be subjected to increasing pressure in the years ahead. how can we cope with this situation? madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would first like to recall the overwhelming vote in the plenary on 14 november: 559 votes against, 64 in favour and 16 abstentions. the european parliament has rejected, by such a large majority, the proposal for a council regulation on the voluntary modulation of direct payments. i regret that the commission has not therefore opted to abandon the text. while i understand and share the need for better financing for the second pillar of rural development, i cannot accept that this should be done so abruptly at the expense of the first pillar and certainly not in a voluntary way, because the effects of such a measure would undoubtedly be devastating. the commission's proposal would endanger the livelihood of many farms and distort competition. moreover, the proposal could lead to the abandonment or renationalisation of the cap. finally, it does not comply with the community's objectives in the field of rural areas. these reasons, which led to the rejection of the commission's proposal last november, are still very much alive today. on the basis of these considerations, which are amply detailed in mr goepel's report, i therefore fully support his position.
the report is a first step towards a year of intensive work in the temporary committee on the cia, and i would like to thank both rapporteur fava and our chairman, mr coelho, for their efforts and, above all, for their patience. the commission's proposal is not a good one, because it is not a genuine committee of inquiry with judicial powers and because the council of europe has already started to investigate possible human rights violations. but today both supporters and opponents will have to admit that the committee of inquiry did do some sensible work. of course, hard evidence was not to be expected, nor has it always been forthcoming, but there is a lot of factual evidence. many testimonies and working visits have revealed a system which allows one to conclude that several european member states were indeed aware of illegal practices and that others were unaware of what was happening on their territory. both findings are equally painful to me. the differences in views between europe and the united states on how to strike a balance between the fight against terrorism and respect for fundamental and human rights were very clearly shown. it is no coincidence that this discussion coincides with and is consistent with the conclusions reached in the swift case and the eu-us discussion on passenger data transfers. i'd like to make one observation here. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i hope, however, that the european parliament will be united in its vote this afternoon and send a clear signal, because the protection of fundamental and human rights is a matter of life and death.
mr president, i support this report, which is a courageous report, condemning practices which are an insult to human rights. few european countries have been spared. what about belgium? have cia planes flown over his airspace with detainees on board? the belgian investigation reached negative conclusions but acknowledged that the authorities did not have a mandate to check passengers on airplanes in transit. so there will always be doubt, as in other countries. the belgian senate committee described in detail the incidents which initially intervened in its investigation, including the reluctance of the secret services. the president of the senate, anne-marie lizin, did not hide it when she came to testify in the european parliament. in june 2006, the findings of the inquiry were voted on in the senate and made public. so we can say everything about belgium, that it is a complicated country, that flemish and walloons would do well to get along and that we make stupid jokes on television. but not that it's not a great democracy playing transparency. we swept the front door and i hope the report at least acknowledges that.
in writing. the european parliament has just delivered its opinion on the work of its temporary committee of inquiry into the use of european airspace by the cia for the purpose of illegal transport of prisoners. the verdict is not surprising: condemnation of the many member states which have de facto supported the cia. the report by mr fava is of a high quality: it recalls a common foundation of fundamental values: respect for human rights, condemnation of torture and respect for the legal framework in the war against terrorism. it also has a major flaw: it is totally unaware of the fact that our governments have no other limits to their efforts than those of reason, the one that allows them to reject the impossible: the secret services will never be welcoming and transparent! let us not confuse humanism with angelicism! the philosopher alan aldaws said simply: "freedom is not possible without security, and security is not worth anything without freedom". this concern for safety in freedom justified my abstention! i am convinced that my friend antoine duquesne, former minister of the interior of the kingdom of belgium, would have delivered the same message if he had been able to vote today!
in writing. i voted against the joint resolution tabled by the epp, the pse and alde presenting ideas for the spring 2007 council in relation to the lisbon strategy. two paragraphs which i regarded as fundamental have been accepted by my colleagues, whereas i find them contrary to my european convictions. firstly, the european parliament has agreed to support the policy of allowing private funding for universities and to encourage the private sector to speak out about the needs of the market in education and training. this may lead to a form of competition between universities which could undermine equality in university education. in addition, the european parliament decided to ask member states to review inefficient social models in the light of their long-term financial viability, changing global dynamics and demographic trends, with the aim of making these social models more sustainable. in my view, this amounts to nothing less than an open threat to social security systems. it is only on these two grounds, contrary to my convictions, that i have decided to vote against this resolution.
the committee on development and cooperation has tabled a number of amendments. madam president, commissioner, mr president of the council, ladies and gentlemen, i have been asked, on behalf of the committee on development, to draw up an opinion on this report on the external dimension of the fight against terrorism, drawn up by my colleague, mr luis yañez, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs. when we talk about this subject, we can only do so in an approach which includes our development policies, for the benefit of the countries of the south in particular. this opinion, which is uncompromising, does indeed touch on a number of essential points, which have already been discussed here, concerning the very definition of terrorism, respect for human rights and relations with the various diasporas in europe, in particular. the opinion also addresses the difficulty of combatting terrorism in conjunction with the need to uphold human rights in all circumstances, so that no one can justify the denial of one in the name of the other. our report then touches on the issue of the fight against terrorism in terms of the impact that its financing may have on the quality and effectiveness of european development cooperation. we should point out, for example, that at present a large number of measures adopted by the member states, which do not directly relate to a policy of reducing poverty in the southern countries, are unfortunately included in official development aid, such as financing for security investments, the repatriation of illegal immigrants, a number of expenditure related to immigration policies and so on. the committee on development would also like to draw the attention of the house to the fact that the inclusion in official development assistance of financial support for a range of activities related to the fight against terrorism will only further undermine its primary objective, namely the eradication of poverty. this opinion was voted almost unanimously in the committee on development on 6 november. unfortunately, however, a majority of the members of the committee on foreign affairs did not consider it necessary to adopt a single paragraph of that opinion. that is why we have decided to reintroduce the main lines of this text in the form of amendments to the report, and i therefore call on all the democratic groups to support them so that we can, alongside the necessary fight against terrorism, effectively pursue development policies aimed at achieving the millennium development goals, which are the real long-term answer to terrorism and all the suffering it brings with it.
the european parliament has been very active in the fight against terrorism, and i am sure that the european parliament will be very active in the fight against terrorism. as the report rightly states, international terrorism is the greatest threat to the peace and stability of open societies, the most direct threat to freedom, security and human dignity. international terrorism has declared war on the entire western world, including europe. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we clearly identify that threat. when i read in recital a of the report terms such as 'international terrorism and its ideology', i fear we are not on the right track. terrorism is not an ideology in itself, nor is it an end in itself. as the director of the arab television station al-arabiya stated some years ago, it is a painful reality that today not all muslims are terrorists, but all terrorists are muslims. it's a story that, like all stories, contains a kernel of truth. it is fashionable in europe to say that we do not want a clash of civilizations, but whether one likes it or not, that clash of civilizations is in full swing. the fight against poverty must be continued and supported, of course, but we must not be naive and think that this means that we are also fighting terrorism. the perpetrators of the attacks in new york, london and madrid were not poor people, but, on the contrary, highly educated people of the middle class. the european union therefore benefits from the introduction of democratic ideas, including freedom of expression, into the islamic world. so there should be a kind of radio freedom on a large scale. is this a plea to stigmatize all muslims? no, not at all. but it is important that within islam, things like self-criticism and introspection are promoted. a few weeks ago it came to light that the chairman of the muslim executive in belgium - which is an official government agency, funded and recognized by the government - had given money to a convicted terrorist who was in a prison in belgium. this fact is in itself very bad. what i find even worse is that there has been no reaction from the muslim community in belgium to this incident. on the contrary, they have remained silent, and that at least gave the impression that they were not at all shocked by the fact that the president of the muslim executive, who claims to speak on behalf of all the muslims in belgium, is indirectly supporting terrorism. that is a disturbing situation and we must take action against it.

in writing. fifty years! it is almost 50 years since several european countries embarked on development aid and north-south cooperation. half a century, with mixed results. why would you do that? for a number of reasons. one of them is the lack of ownership of development strategies by the countries receiving aid. that is why i voted in favour of the report on budgetary aid to developing countries, aid which is directly managed by the government of the recipient country. it is therefore this government, after a dialogue with the union and the elaboration of a strategy, which assumes responsibility for its own development. i know this instrument is controversial. especially when it comes to using it in fragile countries, with unstable finances. this is obvious when it comes to undemocratic or corrupt countries. it cannot therefore be used everywhere. the success of budget support, however, is the breaking of a vicious circle. the conditions are clear: no budget support without respect for governance standards, the fight against corruption. no budget support either without measures to clean up the economy. the more points a government scores in good governance, the more it receives. it's the creation of a genuine virtuous circle!

in writing. corporate social responsibility should be taken for granted, but it is far from being. companies that claim to be socially responsible have an obligation to society to minimise the negative social and environmental impact of their activities. meanwhile, many initiatives are demanding the label "socially responsible". this means that consumers cannot see the forest through the trees and any checks for the ordinary layman become a non-starter. the effects of free markets and the search for the lowest price are also destructive and are becoming widely known. in order to support the power of the consumer and the support of a single well-intentioned company, sound regulation is necessary. europe can take the lead in social responsibility by developing policies that create standards, maintain standards and ensure a level playing field. this is precisely what the report on 'corporate social responsibility' says: the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: it calls for mandatory reporting, chain responsibility and greater transparency on lobbyists. it also recommends that the social and environmental impact be given greater consideration in public procurement. the report is very much in favour of sustainability and i therefore support it fully.

in writing. the european parliament rightly argues that the commission's recommendation of 18 october 2005 on the 'cross-border collective management of copyright and related rights for the benefit of lawful online music services' went too far. the music industry, parliament and the council have not been consulted and the 'soft legislative approach' proposed by the commission has already influenced market decisions, making it more than a recommendation. companies wishing to offer online music services in europe still have to negotiate rights with the copyright organisations and record companies in each country. the commission is right to say that this can be done more simply. however, the recommendation opens the way to a total free market, which may lead to the loss of cultural diversity and local repertoires. it is more in the interest of collective rights managers to attract the most profitable rights holders. the commission is putting commercial interests ahead of cultural diversity. parliament called for controlled competition by setting clear conditions such as equal treatment of authors, a fair and transparent system of competition which avoids downward pressure on authors' incomes and fair representation of all stakeholders in the management structures. this report is about unity in diversity and i therefore support it.
