on behalf of the pse group. mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, allow me to start by congratulating my colleague bernadette bourzai, an alternate member of the parliament's committee on agriculture, whose commitment and involvement are to be commended. i would like to continue by congratulating the commission on its excellent proposal which is likely to bring more clarity to the trade in veal, which will make it possible to inform the consumer better. the proposal is the result of a long negotiation process in the member states and the trade associations concerned to provide consumers with more information and to allow fairer competition between the various producers. most of these were penalized by a name covering a product, the calf, whose physiological evolution caused great differences in a few months because of its speed. in a few weeks, the calf changes from a monogastric mammal, when it feeds almost exclusively on milk, to a four-stomached ruminant mammal when it starts eating fiber-rich foods such as grass, hay, and grains. it is easy to imagine the obvious consequences of this change on the dietary and nutritional composition of meat, including: different vitamin content, white, pink or red colour of meat due, in particular, to the fact that iron is absent from milk but present in plants. but to put on an equal footing and call veal meat from an animal less than six to eight months old that has been raised on milk and meat from an animal 14 or 16 months old that eats, ruminates, does not allow either the consumer or the producer to find out the reality of what they are eating. within the european area, this harmonization, which makes it possible to differentiate between veal and young bovine meat, and this without prejudicing the quality of either, is an important step towards respect for the consumer and producers and expresses our concern for a healthier and more balanced diet for more responsible consumers.
on behalf of the pse group. - mr commissioner, as a belgian socialist member of the european parliament, i think it is really important to take part in this debate tonight, also because this debate is being held in response to a petition by the royal belgian society for the protection of birds which collected over 200,000 signatures on the subject of bird hunting in malta. this is an international problem, ladies and gentlemen. in malta, thousands of migratory birds are shot or captured each year. this is all the more so since malta is one of the few resting places for migratory birds when they cross the mediterranean. what good is it to europe's countries to invest a lot of money and energy in protective measures under the habitats and birds directives if the same birds are shot on their way across borders? i would like to say to my maltese colleagues: malta has indeed obtained a number of transitional measures in the accession treaty until 2008, but these relate only to the capture of birds for breeding purposes in order to maintain the species. so, animal welfare measures. under no circumstances does this transitional measure allow the hunting of migratory birds in spring. the european commission has made it clear in its notice of failure that malta is in breach of the birds directive on this point and no exceptions can be made. the report of the committee on petitions' fact-finding mission by mr libicki in june 2006 also denounces the large-scale hunting of migratory birds and concludes that the derogation from the birds directive that malta has decided on its own should be ended. i therefore think it is extremely important that the european parliament supports the commission in the procedure and convinces malta to respect the birds directive and thus not to allow hunting of migratory birds this spring. this case, ladies and gentlemen, may be of the utmost importance for breaches of the birds directive which are also being found elsewhere in the european union.

threats. international studies are documenting the cost of not acting now, the cost we are imposing on our children and grandchildren if we remain passive. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of the european union. with their decisions, the heads of state or government have opened the door to an ambitious and, in my view, responsible climate and energy policy in europe, a policy which no longer glosses over the scale of the problems we face but seeks effective strategies to tackle them. we have taken a big step towards an integrated climate and energy policy integrated because one cannot exist without the other; because by far the biggest climate risk today is the production, the consumption of energy by humans. this is of course particularly true of greenhouse gas emissions. the eu remains a leader in international climate action with the decisions taken in brussels. they give us a credible start on the forthcoming negotiations on the successor agreement to the kyoto protocol. the eu unilaterally and independently commits to reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 20% by 2020 compared to 1990. this is an ambitious goal and it will require a great effort from all member states to achieve it. the heads of state or government went further: we have even promised a 30% reduction over the same period, provided that other industrialized countries and economically advanced developing countries follow suit. we can only achieve these objectives if we also set ourselves on the road to the future in terms of energy policy. this is why a comprehensive energy action plan was agreed in brussels, alongside the climate targets. the action plan has two objectives at its heart: the aim is to reduce energy consumption within the european union by 20% by 2020 as a result of increased energy efficiency and, crucially, to increase the share of renewable energy in total energy consumption to at least 20%. you remember we were just having a big fight about the binding of the last target. some member states expressed reservations that this requirement might be too ambitious. i am very glad that we were able to agree on the commitment at the end, because it is precisely in these three objectives that the link between climate and energy policy is clear. without the efforts made in renewable energy and energy efficiency, the eu would clearly not be able to achieve its climate target. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster is a good one. we will have to take account of the different starting points and the possibilities of the member states, and the commission has been asked - and has accepted - to submit a proposal for this division later this year. this is not the place to present the energy action plan in all its parts. i think that an overall assessment here in this house is more important, and it is here that, in addition to the objectives i have just set out, we must also note, above all, the strategic guidelines adopted in the areas of the internal energy market, security of supply, international energy policy, energy research and new energy technologies. i would like to draw attention to one aspect of these five examples: security of supply in the medium and long term will only be achieved if we succeed in diversifying energy sources and transport routes. in concrete terms, this means: intensifying relations with the major producing countries, developing sustainable external energy relations, including with the countries of central asia and the countries bordering the black and caspian seas, and of course also maintaining our energy relations with the gulf states and north africa. but it also means reliable and transparent energy relations, including with russia. the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome. the results of climate change and energy policy are of particular importance. the european council has not, however, - as you will see in the conclusions - confined itself to this. the spring summit is traditionally a review of the lisbon strategy for growth and jobs, and it is important to start by: the reformed lisbon strategy is taking effect. there are visible successes across europe in rising growth and falling unemployment, but the message is that this is not the time to sit on our hands. quite the opposite! we want to take advantage of the positive momentum: i see no alternative to continuing structural reforms and consolidating public budgets. the european internal market must be developed and completed in important areas - i will mention here again electricity and gas, postal services, but also financial markets. this includes - as one of the conclusions of last friday - the reduction of the deficit in the implementation of community law. we have made good progress in this area in recent years. i would remind you that the implementation deficit was 3% in 2000, today it is only 1.2% and we know that efforts must continue. the european council therefore decided last friday to reduce this deficit further to 1% by 2009. the lisbon strategy will only be successful in the eyes of citizens if it succeeds in developing the social dimension too - this is particularly true in the light of the positive developments on the labour markets. in this context, the heads of state or government stressed the importance of fair working conditions, workers' rights and opportunities for participation, safety and health at work and family-friendly work organisation. i would like to emphasize another element of the decisions: better regulation and reduction of bureaucratic burdens. we have made some progress in this area in the past, but we must not, of course, let our efforts in the future be slack. in particular, we want to reduce the administrative burden of eu rules, the so-called red tape, by 25% by 2012, and member states are invited to set similarly ambitious national targets over the next year. in the final press conference on friday, president barroso said what i think was a very friendly, almost flattering word about the council meeting which had just ended: in terms of the results and the objectives formulated, this was the most important summit he had attended during his term of office. i say that in front of this house. this success, mr commissioner, would not have been possible without the excellent preparatory work of the commission, but also without the support of the european parliament, for which you, mr pöttering, as its newly elected president, attended a council meeting for the first time. the european union is on the way to a modern and future-oriented climate and energy policy. the heads of state or government have shown that europe can take a leading role in important global issues. the message of the summit is, in my view, that if we europeans join forces, if we act together, we can successfully shape the future. the german presidency's motto, which we also adopted as a guiding principle before the conclusions and which i mentioned in the debate, is: europe succeeds together! (applause from the centre)
vice-president of the commission. mr president of the european parliament, mr president of the council, ladies and gentlemen! the commission thanks the european council and the german presidency for the far-reaching and courageous objectives set by the european council last week, and we thank you for the unmistakable signal you have given to the rest of the world, which has been registered with great attention throughout the world: the signal that we in europe are serious about climate protection, about developing a common energy policy and about defending our competitiveness. and last but not least, an important outcome of this council was that those who claimed that a union of 27 member states was no longer capable of acting were wrong. this was the first major challenge for the union of 27, and i believe it has met it. it was a good summit for our european policy of the partnership for growth and jobs in the framework of the lisbon strategy. the heads of state or government acknowledged that the new strategy was delivering the expected results and had made an important contribution to the economic recovery, which was reflected in an upward trend in gross domestic product growth of 2.9% in 2006. we now expect seven million new jobs to be created in the european union in the next two years alone, and we will indeed come very close to achieving the original lisbon targets of the year 2000. the heads of state or government have made it clear that we cannot be satisfied with the first signs of a stronger economic dynamic. i agree with what mr steinmeier has just said: europe now has a great opportunity to increase the pace of reform once again. we haven't made the turn yet. the negative trend has slowed down, it is true, but we have not yet succeeded. all we can say is that we're on the right track. this is also reflected in the european council's support for the country-specific recommendations, which the commission has adopted for the first time. please do not underestimate that in a field of their exclusive competence, member states have accepted that the commission has put forward country-specific recommendations for their national agendas and that these recommendations have been adopted unanimously by the european council without debate. this shows that for the first time we have a functioning mechanism of economic policy coordination in europe. this year we are already preparing for the next three-year cycle of growth and employment policy, and the commission already sees three clear needs. firstly, we will have to fully integrate climate and energy policy into european growth and employment policy. it must be a one-time policy. secondly, it will be absolutely necessary to strengthen the third pillar of this strategy, the employment pillar, the social dimension, with particular emphasis on improving the employability of people in europe. what we are seeing is a new development. we will increasingly lack adequately trained workers in certain regions and sectors. we must increase employability, above all by strengthening education and training. the third major task in the context of the lisbon review will be to increase its visibility. it's called "do good and talk about it". perhaps we talk a little too little about the fact that we have a european answer to the global economic challenge, and it should be much more embedded in the national political debate that european integration, common european economic action is the answer we need to keep our competitiveness in global competition. on simplification of legislation and reduction of red tape - a subject which is particularly close to my heart - just a quick word: it is important that parliament and the council have been asked to make even greater use of impact assessments in the future. the quality of legislation is the key to the whole project. the prerequisite for this is good impact cost estimates. the whole project of simplifying legislation and cutting red tape has now really taken off in all its parts. i am very grateful to the council for supporting the commission's objective of reducing by 2012 25% of the bureaucratic costs on european businesses induced by european legislation. but it is at least as important that the member states have committed themselves to do the same in their own area of responsibility. that was the argument that was at issue. it is a great success that this has been achieved. the objective of achieving an overall reduction of 25% in bureaucratic costs for enterprises by 2012 now seems to be within reach. i would like to make it clear once again, here in the european parliament: when we talk about cutting red tape costs, we mean nothing less than paperwork for the companies. the report is concerned with reporting obligations, statistical obligations, information obligations and documentation obligations. in no case are consumer protection standards, quality standards, environmental standards, safety standards or social standards even touched upon. it's not about changing the substance, it's about making it so that it doesn't put unnecessary strain on the companies, but releases energy. (applause from the centre) when tony blair said a while ago that if the european union did not exist, it would have to be invented, he was probably referring to decisions such as those taken by the european council just a few weeks after the alarming facts of the latest un climate report became known. with the support of the european parliament and on the basis of the commission's january proposals, it succeeded in making climate change and the energy transition a priority of european policy and in adopting an action plan for the next three years. the strength of the decisions lies in the fact that they have succeeded in closely linking climate and energy policy. reducing greenhouse gas emissions goes hand in hand with a competitive, secure and sustainable energy policy. the strength of the decisions lies in their credibility, because they are not suspended in a vacuum, but are linked to a concrete package of measures. mandatory renewable energy targets, as well as the targeted promotion of energy savings and new carbon capture and storage technology, will set the european economy up for the global challenges of the 21st century in terms of climate policy. we will continue to reduce the existing barriers to renewable energy and energy efficiency in all industrial sectors, with a view to achieving a 20% share of renewable energy in general and a biofuel share of at least 10% by 2020. i think i have heard what foreign minister steinmeier has just said as president of the council in relation to the commission's mandate to submit a proposal by the end of the year. i can assure you that the commission will put forward a fair and balanced proposal on how to distribute the burden within the european union, taking into account what individual member states have already done, what their starting positions are and what their conditions are for meeting this objective at all. i'm sure we can do this. the important thing is that we finally achieve a functioning internal market for electricity and gas for the benefit of consumers, which will stimulate investment and create a genuine european interconnected network. with these steps, new global markets will open up for us. the energy bill of every citizen and every business will be relieved. we are paying too much for energy in europe today, not only because of the global situation but because of the mismanagement of our own energy supply. therefore, the consistent implementation of the new eu climate policy is also part of the fulfilment of the lisbon agenda. we propose that the advanced countries agree to reduce co2 emissions by 30% by 2020 compared to 1990. in addition, the eu is already committing itself to reducing co2 emissions by at least 20% by 2020. this puts us in a very good position for the upcoming climate negotiations. i believe that this climate double-cross will lead to a new dynamic globally that we also urgently need. we can no longer stand by and watch the us and china point fingers at each other and demand that the us take the lead. somebody's got to start now! this would certainly be an important point for the eu-us summit on 30 april this year, which the heads of state or government as well as the other foreign policy issues also addressed. let me say a word to european industry: the council's guidelines provide a clear framework for our industry and give it investment security for years to come. they can now plan their investments, they know what the policy requires of them and they can develop their strategies. we want europe to export the best and cleanest products, but not its jobs. i do not want to hear from european industry what it cannot do and what it does not think it can do. i would like to hear from european industry what it can and can do. and it turns out there's a lot more to it than she thinks. we should not forget that this is only a starting signal. this strategy must be further developed during this parliamentary term by means of concrete community projects and individual legislative projects. we have the support of the citizens of europe. we know from the recent eurobarometer survey that europe's citizens are acutely aware that a change of direction is inevitable. you are also aware that decisive action will not be in vain. let me summarize . the lisbon strategy is an open and dynamic process. we need a marketplace of ideas that can then be translated into concrete political decisions. in this connection, let me quote a report by the centre for european reform: there is hardly a european country that does not examine the danish model of flexicurity, the finnish path to higher education, or the british liberalisation strategy. i can add more examples, such as the french "pôles de compétitivité", the dutch standard cost model, tax reforms in some of the new member states. mutual learning is a key element of this reform process. the european partnership for growth and jobs is the european answer to the two great questions of our time: the great social question of how to provide good and sufficient jobs in the age of globalisation, and the great ecological question of how to keep our planet habitable. we know that people are asking these very questions: will i keep my job? will i still receive benefits if i am sick? will i be able to educate my children? will i be cared for in my old age? we also know that they are asking the question: what will the future be like for my children and grandchildren? here is the answer to these questions, but it is important that you also show the citizens of europe that this answer can only be a european answer! if ever there was a need to demonstrate why we need european integration, here are the two themes that show that european integration is an indispensable necessity in the 21st century too! thank you, thank you. (applause from the centre)

the european parliament has already expressed its views on the commission's proposal for a regulation on the european union's external relations. the euromed countries supply most of the immigrants in most of the european member states and it should therefore be obvious that there is also talk within euromed and more talk about a number of specific problems. there is the problem of illegal immigration, of asylum seekers who have been processed and are allowed back into their country by some of the euromed partners, but with great difficulty. the same applies to the cooperation we should expect from the other side of the mediterranean to make it more difficult and discourage illegal immigration. we must also put the problem of the rise of muslim fundamentalism on the agenda, both with our partners in euromed and in europe itself. another issue is the lack of integration of many immigrants in europe, and here too there should be a positive impulse from the countries of origin. for example, it should be possible to speak of immigrants who take on the nationality of their host country but who at the same time want or, rather, must retain their original nationality. commissioner mandelson has just spoken about managing and controlling legal immigration. well, i think it is high time we dared to say that we do not need any new immigration and that the re-migration of people with adaptation problems should no longer be a taboo.
in writing. the fact that a resolution like this is being tabled in the plenary of our parliament could easily have been avoided. malta could simply have abolished the spring hunt and thereby complied with the provisions of the birds directive. but after countless parliamentary initiatives and legal action by the european commission, the country still acts as if its nose is bleeding. it looks as if it will again allow hunters to take down numerous protected migratory birds during the spring. this price shooting serves no higher purpose than the out-of-control hobby of the local hunters. it is a threat to the diversity and conservation of bird species throughout the european union. the representatives of the hunters categorically call those who oppose the spring hunt 'fascists'. they are aggressively threatening their own government, their representatives and their police apparatus. we cannot allow that in this union. the maltese government may be offended by this resolution. but it could also feel empowered by the support of the whole of europe in taking measures to abolish the spring hunt. for that last reason, i fully support this resolution.
mr president, i voted against the voggenhuber report because once again this parliament is sweeping the floor with referendums in the netherlands and france on the european constitution. how else can you describe it? despite the fact that the charter of fundamental rights has never been a legally binding text and that it can never be after the rejection of the constitution in these two countries, this politically correct catalogue is being re-introduced through numerous back doors. this is unacceptable both legally and politically. the official europe is clearly united in its contempt for national democracies. the dutch, for example, were recently told by commission president barroso that they should finally adopt the constitution. the citizens may continue to vote, but in the end it is the european mandarins who decide. anyone who thinks that such arrogance will reduce the euroscepticism of our citizens is gravely mistaken.
author. ladies and gentlemen, two years ago i spoke about the human rights situation in cambodia. two points in this joint resolution by six groups in parliament particularly attract my attention. the first, which i would like to stress today, is the assassination on 24 february of hy vuthy, the president of the free trade union of cambodian workers. this murder is in addition to the 2004 murders of chea vichea and ros sovannareth, who had been presidents of the same union, as well as the acts of intimidation and physical attacks that other union members have been subjected to in recent years. the right to form a trade union is recognized in cambodia but recent incidents suggest that this right is being violated. it is inconceivable that people cannot freely join a trade union without suffering the consequences. the murder of chea vichea remains unsolved to this day. born samnang and sok sam oeun were arrested, found guilty of the murder of the trade unionist and sentenced to twenty years' imprisonment without any proof of their guilt being produced. we want the real perpetrators of these murders to be found and brought to justice and for born samnang and sok sam oeun to receive a fair trial. the second point i wanted to stress today is the khmer rouge tribunal. during my mission to cambodia in april 2006, i was able to see the expectations of the cambodian people in relation to this court. today, one year after our visit, the court is still not set up. it is essential that it be put in place without delay so that all the unpunished crimes of the khmer rouge are finally brought to justice. finally, we welcome the efforts to stabilise the situation and the smooth running of the electoral process. it seems essential that the kingdom of cambodia continue its efforts and place particular emphasis on the functioning of the judiciary, which we found, after our mission last year, to be the weak link in the system.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i should like to say that i am very pleased. this report on organic protection and labelling of organic products is finally being debated in plenary at an important moment, since the vote in the committee on agriculture on 27 february coincided with a demonstration by the agro-biologists who were rightly complaining about the new specifications for organic farming which aimed and is aiming to tolerate 0.9% contamination, as in conventional farming. this report, the fruit of the hard work of mrs aubert, which i welcome, is therefore extremely important for the whole sector and offers a unique opportunity for parliament to distance itself from the council and the commission. it is essential, especially now, to send a strong signal to ensure that organic production is properly protected. to this end, i tabled amendment no 170 on behalf of the pse group, which reads as follows: 'member states should have an adequate legislative framework, based on the precautionary principle and the polluter-pays principle, to eliminate any risk of contamination of organic products by gmos. operators are responsible for taking all necessary precautions to avoid any risk of accidental or technically unavoidable contamination by gmos. the presence of gmos in organic products shall be limited exclusively to unforeseeable and technically unavoidable volumes up to a maximum value of 0,1%.' in short, it is just as important not to distort the very essence of organic production by imposing too high a tolerance for accidental contamination as it is to maintain a minimum level that is acceptable and accepted by the sector, so as not to penalise the organic farmer who is accidentally contaminated and whose production would be totally downgraded if zero tolerance were to be applied. in addition, we support the use of natural nitrogen fertilisers and any other natural mineral fertilisers and therefore propose, by amendments nos 168 and 169, to delete the passage in article 8 (1) (d) which prohibits the use of nitrogen fertilisers. finally, i fully share the positive view of the legal affairs committee of the parliament to apply the dual legal basis, articles 37 and 95 of the treaty, because there is a double advantage in also referring to the internal market jurisdiction. firstly, this report, which was voted on in the committee on agriculture, would be extended to the whole of the catering sector - caterers, community kitchens, canteens, restaurants - and to certain products such as food supplements. secondly, thanks to the internal market, we would move from a consultation procedure to a codecision procedure, which would give us a vital say in the drafting of this regulation which will directly affect the quality of food for the citizens.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to agree with the main points of the excellent report by our colleague, alain lamassoure, which was very well represented by our colleague, mr böge. first, we agree on the method. our rapporteur has clearly understood that the reform of the union's financing would not take place against the agreement of the member states, that is to say without the consent of the national parliaments. we have to keep in touch with them because we have to convince them. secondly, and this is the main point, we agree on the structure of the report which proposes a comprehensive reform, but in two phases. the first and most urgent phase is to clean up the present system of all the pathologies it has accumulated over the years. no more of these little friend-friend gifts, no more of the discounts, the discounts on discounts, the exemptions, the caps, the pitiful bargaining. cleanup is the priority. as for the second phase, we shall have occasion to discuss it later. one last word, madam president. the priority for us is the constitutional treaty. if this budget debate were to make it more difficult, we should have the courage to postpone it.
madam president, mr böge, i think that the work done by alain lamassoure and the committee on budgets is useful. we share his criticisms and have long shared the central idea of the need for a new system of own resources. i would like to express my deep disappointment, particularly with regard to paragraphs 28 et seq., which, in my view, attempt to reassure the people, but on the basis of a false realism. it is a false realism to say that the tax sovereignty of the member states must be maintained when in fact there is no such tax sovereignty because of the tax competition within the union. the commission's proposal for a directive on the taxation of savings income is a very good one. and finally, false realism about the order of magnitude of the budget. on this point, i would like to take the opportunity to say that i am totally wrong about the argument put forward by mr mulder, that we do not have enough money. no, we don't have enough money for life+. no, we don't have enough money for foreign policy. no, we don't have enough money for education and research policy. and no, we do not have enough money for transeuropean networks. that was one of the positions of parliament and i cannot understand why we should go backwards in an own-initiative report.
member of the commission. mr president, honourable members, dear friends of football and sport, i am very pleased to represent the commission here tonight for the debate on football. i think this constitutes further proof that parliament is committed to sport. the support you give to our initiatives for sport is of course both welcome and needed. i would like to start by congratulating both rapporteurs, mr belet and mr catania, on the quality of their work. the two reports treat football from different angles, but they both illustrate the nature of sport, its values, its potential for education, for society and for the economy. before speaking on the more detailed aspects of the reports, let me say a few words on the white paper. this is going to be an essential piece of work for the future of european sport. the white paper on sport is due to be adopted in july this year. this will be the culmination of a long process and should be seen in the light of wider political considerations. plans for a white paper are driven by the expectations of sport stakeholders. they wish to see their concerns addressed by eu policymakers, including the need to better promote sport and to achieve more legal certainty. the white paper will cover all sports, and there will be no football-specific approach. the ultimate goal of this initiative is first to mainstream sport into other active policies of the union in order to improve its use as a tool for eu policy. secondly, we are aiming to set conditions for improved governance in european sport. the main topics of the white paper will be the social and economic role of sport, the organisation of sport and governance issues. we will pay great attention to parliament’s reports when preparing the white paper. the commission has been following the committee’s work very closely and this has already given us very useful input. on mr belet’s report, the commission welcomes parliament’s initiative on the future of professional football. we share many of the concerns expressed in the report. the white paper will address many of the issues which mr belet raises, such as social cohesion, protection of young workers, social dialogue and the free movement of workers. as your draft report acknowledges, it is extremely difficult to establish a comprehensive european legal framework recognising the specificity of sport, but eu case law does recognise the specificity of sport and the social and educational role played by football in europe. concerning the free movement of workers, for example, the court held that sport is only subject to community law when it constitutes an economic activity. this covers both professional and amateur athletes, and the court made an exception to the general rule of non-discrimination for matches which are of purely sporting rather than economic interest, for example, games between national teams. on the issue of home-grown players, the commission is very sensitive to the measures proposed by uefa. we could share the idea of promoting the training of young people, as well as sending a signal to the clubs that they should invest in the training of young people and not only in transfers of players. however, we are still considering the question of quotas of locally-trained players, including from the angle of proportionality. the commission welcomes parliament’s call for intensified social dialogue in the football sector. this is a good mechanism for addressing issues such as mobility, work contracts and working conditions. we have supported the social partners’ efforts to develop a more structured dialogue where football has taken the lead at european level. the commission will continue to support employers’ and employees’ organisations in the whole sport sector and it will continue its open dialogue with all sport organisations on this issue. in conclusion, the commission will give due and realistic consideration to your recommendations in line with current eu areas of responsibility. the request for the commission to draw up an action plan to define the issues to be tackled deserves careful consideration. on mr catania’s report, i want to underline first that sport can be a positive force for education, culture and social integration. but in recent years we have seen unfortunate and growing signs of violence and hooliganism during sporting events. two weeks ago sport ministers discussed the issue in stuttgart. they underlined the need for improved prevention measures, in particular encouraging cooperation between all those involved, including the supporters. the commission has concentrated on promoting exchanges of experience and good practice among member states so as to develop better police and judicial cooperation. we have established good working contacts with uefa and other sporting authorities. in terms of public order and police control, i think everyone was pleased with the excellent results of football matches during the world cup in germany last year. it shows that good preparation and coordination with other member states are very effective in preventing crime and especially hooliganism. the first statistics show that the crime rate did not increase at all during that period. council decision 2002/348/ec obliges member states to establish national football information points. this is a positive step in improving cooperation between police forces and other bodies that combat football-related violence. the austrian initiative that mr catania’s report addresses aims at replacing the existing network of information points with a specific network of national football information points. these would have access to the personal data of hooligans or ‘risk supporters’ identified by the different member states. the commission welcomes the report’s support for this initiative and takes due note of the concerns expressed on human rights and data protection, to which, as you know, the commission attaches great importance. in conclusion, it is a positive result that sport is now truly on the agenda at european level. the 50th anniversary of the rome treaties is - i'm the reporter. president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, some people are quite surprised when you associate europe, the european union, with sport. and then, predictably, they always say, the eu has no competence in sport and should not try to do anything about it. that statement, ladies and gentlemen - we all know it - is wrong, and the people involved know it very well. sport, and especially professional sport, and the commissioner has already said it, and that is what we are talking about here, is in all respects in contact with european regulations anyway. in particular, the economic aspects of professional football are subject to it. the commission is involved and the european court of justice is involved. that has been conclusively proven in recent years. professional football, my fellow players, is obviously big business. no one's denying that. but it's so much more. it has important social and educational functions and that is why we emphasize in this report the specific approach that is necessary. the specific nature of sport is enshrined in the declaration of nice and the protocol to the amsterdam treaty. no one can get around that. it is therefore our duty to take this into account when applying eu rules. no one is asking for exceptional measures or so-called group exemptions. what we do ask is this: i think that the commission should issue guidelines. commitment to transparency in their policies. many members of this parliament, many colleagues, also want more solidarity and redistribution of resources, of the money in football. i don't think it's is contained in this report and what will hopefully be adopted tomorrow when it draws up its white paper on sport. we have taken the utmost account of the eu's powers in this area. there is no point document from the commission and i think, i assume, and you can be sure, that in return you can count on the loyal support of this house.

in writing. the new regulation will replace regulation 2092/91/eec on organic farming. the main concern remains the presence of gmos in products that will be labelled 'organic': the commission had expected that the tolerance would be 0.9% of the presence of gmos, whereas quantities from 0.1% are already detectable. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union is able to meet the requirements of the treaty. it is therefore essential that the expectations of consumers who choose an organic product be met, that is to say, that they can be sure that their product does not contain gmos. that is why i am glad that parliament has introduced this 0.1% threshold in the aubert report. i call on the agriculture ministers in the council to review this regulation in depth in the light of the results of this vote. i also support parliament's request that this regulation be adopted by codecision between the council and parliament and i expect the commission to follow parliament in this respect, because the opinion of elected officials must prevail over that of civil servants.
in writing. a majority of european citizens (58%) continue to show their distrust of genetically modified food. the european scepticism about the presence of gmos in conventional agriculture is all the more true for organic farming. it is therefore essential that the european union promotes organic farming which meets an increasing consumer demand and which also corresponds to a move towards a drastic reform of the cap in the direction of truly sustainable development! the report by mrs aubert stresses that there are a few rules of common sense that must prevail in order to promote organic products. firstly, we must oppose the european commission's proposal for a regulation which aims to set the same accidental contamination rate at 0.9% for the various sectors. secondly, and on the contrary, as the organic sector demands and as has already been implemented by several european states or regions, a rate of incidental presence of gmos should be set as low as possible: 0.1%, which corresponds to the scientifically possible detection threshold. finally, the polluter pays principle must be applied, because there is no question of making organic farmers pay the bill for the risk of crop coexistence!

member of the commission. mr president, i have listened carefully to the discussion on the proposal for a directive on payment services in the internal market and i would like to thank you all for your comments. we cannot establish a single market for payments without a harmonised legal framework at eu level instead of the patchwork of 27 sets of different national rules. at the same time we need more competition and innovation in payments markets. this will provide substantial benefits to our citizens, our businesses, our public administrations and our economy as a whole. quite simply, payments are the lifeblood of our economy and without an efficient payments system we cannot have an efficient single market. the issue regarding data protection was raised by the following members: mr gauzès, mrs bowles and mrs berès. i would like to say two things on this issue. firstly, the commission has already responded to parliament’s resolution of 14 february 2007 on the swift case. article 19 of the proposed directive allows the exchange of information between competent authorities responsible for protecting personal data and the authority responsible for supervising payment institutions. this corresponds to the conclusions of the article 29 working party opinion of 22 november 2006 on the processing of data by swift. secondly, the limitation of data processing to eu countries, as proposed by amendment 287, would in fact be a contradiction of the data protection directive. that directive has a specific regime for authorising the export of data to third countries if those countries provide the necessary guarantees. this regime aims at ensuring that european data protection standards are not undermined by the export of data to countries with more lenient standards. the swift case has demonstrated that we must remain vigilant on the protection of personal data. combating terrorism and terrorist financing are important. so is the protection of privacy, however, which is a fundamental principle for all eu member states. the commission will use all means at its disposal to ensure that all member states respect data protection, including the export of data to third countries. i will bring the points made here to the attention of my colleague, commissioner frattini, who is directly responsible for this area. tomorrow parliament has the opportunity to endorse the proposal which reflects the views already expressed in its lead committee’s report, which provides a legal foundation for an efficient and modern payments market. the commission fully supports a set of compromise amendments aimed at achieving this. i would once again like to thank the rapporteur, mr gauzès, and parliament for your patience in deferring this vote. i stress his conviction that your vote is a historic occasion for payments in europe.
vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. i am very pleased that we can speak today about the long-awaited regulation on medicines for novel therapies. this is a regulation of enormous importance to countless sick people in europe. these new therapies offer hope for many sick people in the hope of new life-saving treatments. i'm not talking about the common cold, i'm talking about diseases like cancer, parkinson's, aids and other terrible scourges. the commission has not yet adopted a harmonised legal framework. this means that many patients are not receiving the treatments that are available. or they're prescribed drugs that aren't safe. with this regulation, we are paving the way for easier and safer access to life-saving medicines. the proposal is also of great importance for the european research area. in the current situation, european researchers and manufacturers - often small and medium-sized enterprises - are unable to grow or compete internationally. let me say a few words about the legislative procedure: it is, of course, a matter for parliament to decide how it will handle the procedure. however, in view of the need to create clear conditions for these long-awaited innovative therapies, i would ask you to allow me to make a strong appeal to you not to delay the procedure any further and to reach a decision here. i therefore welcome all the more the fact that three groups have put forward a comprehensive compromise package to enable such a rapid agreement to be reached. i can assure the commission that we can support this whole package without any change. i think the presidency is also convinced - we shall hear about that - that the package will be adopted by the council. i am well aware that we are dealing with a particularly sensitive subject here, and we must be particularly sensitive when it comes to ethical issues of such importance. i would therefore like to make it clear once again that the sole purpose of this regulation is to ensure that patients receive safe and effective medicines. that's all we're after. the regulation does not specify whether medicinal products can be accepted from an ethical point of view. the principle of subsidiarity is strictly observed: ethical issues fall within the competence of the member states. we cannot allow the european union to impose its ethical convictions on others. you can't do that! the only way to do this is to leave ethical questions to the member states. that's the way we've always done it. this is nothing new. there has been a clear policy for years, with many examples. the proposal therefore neither imposes national authorization of ethically sensitive technologies nor does it prohibit them throughout europe. the proposal is limited to respecting national decisions. the important principles that organ and tissue donation must be voluntary and unpaid also apply. they are already firmly anchored in other legal acts. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. in any case, we should establish common safety standards for such products. i do not see any reason why we should accept different levels of protection in different member states. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. contrary to the amendments proposed by the legal affairs committee, we should not exclude ethically sensitive products, such as those derived from embryonic stem cells, from the scope of the regulation. let me say again, in all seriousness, that today we have a unique political opportunity to adopt a regulation that sick people in europe and the european pharmaceutical industry have been waiting for for a long time. we can't afford to miss this opportunity. i therefore ask all members of the european parliament to support the compromise package put forward by mrs roth-berendt, mrs ries and mr adamo on behalf of their groups and to reject the other motions. if adopted, this regulation will promote innovation, strengthen the competitiveness of our research and industry and, above all, help save lives and relieve people of serious suffering. by supporting this package, you can achieve all of this.

the rapporteur. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, my report is on the discharge of parliament's budget. i want to deal with four points. first of all, we have been saying for years in various discharge resolutions that parliament's discharge does not only concern the management of the secretary-general or the administration, but also the policies and decisions of the administrative bodies of our house, the decisions of the president, the bureau and also the conference of presidents. we are not giving discharge to the secretary-general, but to the president of the european parliament. what we approved last year with the ferber report. second point: the building policy. building policy is important. it is a lot of money and i would like to draw attention to the dispute between the the land and the preparation of the land for construction is reimbursed. there is now a dispute in brussels on this point and it is about a sum of around 15 million euros. that's a lot of money. the to our negotiators, the president of the european parliament and the vice-president responsible for building policy. i also ask in the resolution for special attention to be paid to the impact of the european parliament on the neighbourhood in which we are staying in brussels. the residents of these areas face a great deal of traffic, working and living conditions, and i think that consultation between parliament and the residents of these areas is of the utmost importance. i think we should be good neighbours and that's why consultation is important. i therefore hope that a report will be drawn up on this before next year's budgetary allocation. the third point, a very delicate one, is the voluntary pension fund. the voluntary pension fund has an actuarial deficit of eur 28.8 million today. that's a lot of money. i admit the deficit is falling: last year, it was 43.7 million. the total amount of eur 202 million is currently held in that voluntary pension fund and that sum is invested on the stock exchange. now we all know that the stock market can be a dangerous thing and that the results on a stock market are not always positive. a stock market can be fickle. so there may be a lot of losses with that money. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in the 2005 budget, parliament increased this voluntary pension fund by eur 11.4 million. that is also a lot of money and i think it is normal that the list of final beneficiaries should be made public. we ask the commission to publish the list of final beneficiaries, as this is a very important item for the agricultural sector, which receives a lot of money from the european budget. everyone is for it! that's what happens! you can read that on a website. so why are we putting the brakes on when it comes to our own members of parliament? i can't do this anymore. i see from the voting list that the epp-ed group has tabled out. i have asked for a majority vote in the interests of transparency, and i am announcing this now, so that those who vote against can also answer to their constituents. i think that the european parliament cannot be left to pick up the pieces at the end of the day. this is not possible, this is not a good use of money and public resources. finally, i would like to draw attention to the kyoto vice-president of the commission. madam president, honourable members, i just have some brief comments to make. first of all, thank you all for your comments. concerning some smaller issues, such as the secondment of agents, we will investigate or study the case raised about gender mainstreaming. a pilot study is under way and it is difficult to collect information about policies linked to gender issues. i would also like to stress that interinstitutional dialogue concerning the agencies is extremely valuable, if parliament is in favour of this idea. the commission proposed an interinstitutional agreement concerning agencies in 2005 and this was blocked in the council. now, during the german presidency, you have tried to revitalise interest in this agreement. this is vital if we are to really straighten out this issue. as regards some small details, one member asked about the size of the losses from the european budget. i just want to repeat that, in exact accounting terms, the losses are those amounts of money which are written off from our balance sheet. in the year 2005, the amount was eur 90 805 147.47, which is 0.09% of our budget. this is the exact figure corresponding to the definition of losses. also, i must say to mr seppänen that the accounts for 2006 have already been signed by our chief accounting officer. those agencies are an important issue. secondly, national declarations were the main issue on which many participants touched, saying that there are problems with the shared management of eu funds. the wording from the interinstitutional agreement was included in the revised financial regulation. article 53b says that member states should produce an annual summary at the appropriate national level of the available audits and declarations. it has been agreed with the council that on 15 february 2008 a summary of audits in agriculture will be made available. the technical aspects of doing this in other areas, in particular in relation to the structural funds, are still under discussion, but there will definitely be firm results. the commission raised this issue on 27 march in ecofin and yesterday it was discussed in the council’s financial committee. we have been in contact with the german presidency and the german ministry of finance with a view to discussing again in june how we can reach a definite outcome on the issue of shared management. the main problem here, visible throughout all these discussions, is that this must be done in such a way as to provide evidence for the court of auditors. a great deal of tension could arise if the court of auditors says, ‘you can provide us with your national declaration but this does not mean anything. we will still make our checks and audits and evaluate the situation’. there must be a workable solution. from the beginning, the commission has supported the idea of shared management. we are working with national audit institutions to involve them in this process, but there must also be clear evidence for the court of auditors. it is going ahead. five countries have already declared their readiness to issue national declarations. when i compare the council meetings i attended at the beginning of this commission with the situation today, which is completely different, i see a great step forward. i would like to make one last point on an issue raised by some members. yes, there will be an intergovernmental conference. as we understand it, quite intensive preparations are being made for this intergovernmental conference. this is a situation the commission's vice-president mr president, mr costa, it is true that this proposal is important. the fight against the terrorist threat is of course a priority for all the institutions of the european union. aviation safety requires constant adaptation. we must continue to look for ways of providing greater and more effective protection for european citizens. lessons must be learned from experience and the safety needs must be reconciled with the operational needs of the parties involved: the administration responsible for implementing the rules, airport managers, airlines and, of course, passengers. the existing regulatory framework for aviation security was defined after the attacks of 11 september 2001, and was developed in an emergency, which probably explains the excessive detail of the annex to regulation (ec) no 2320/2002. the normative and detailed nature of this annex is a handicap which prevents us from introducing new, more efficient technologies or from providing for safety systems which are more modulated according to the specific risks to be taken into account. finally, it does not allow us to fill the regulatory gaps in air cargo, in the handling of flights from third countries. so this new proposal to replace regulation (ec) no 2320/2002 with a new regulation, better because it is simpler and clearer, is obviously very desirable. the reorientation of aviation safety rules will improve the protection of citizens; it will allow the legitimate interest of stakeholders to be satisfied by a better balance between the risks to be taken into account and the scope of controls to be carried out. i would have preferred, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, without any hesitation, that this proposal had been adopted at first reading. this delay is all the more regrettable when we see, as i do today, that mr costa's draft report absolutely validates the need for this initiative and we agree, mr president costa, with most of the practical operational suggestions. mr president, i now come to the amendments tabled. more than half of the amendments are adopted by parliament at first reading. the commission's position is, of course, the same. i should like to make a few remarks on some of the new amendments tabled. first of all, amendment no 46 seems to me to be appropriate. it will allow the commission to cooperate more closely with icao for the purpose of exchanging information and mutual support with regard to audits and inspections. so, mr president, we welcome the approach set out in this amendment. amendment no 33 inserted a lapse clause for all implementing measures which should be reviewed after six months. but in a joint statement annexed to the new decision on comitology, parliament and the council nevertheless recognised that good legislation requires that the implementing powers conferred on the commission be without limitation in time. i understand the argument that a text which has entered into force in an emergency must be reviewed at a later date, but i am nonetheless concerned. systematic application of this principle may lead to legal uncertainty and the aviation industry may then be forced to change its procedures or to develop new ones with adverse consequences for the efficiency of procedures, passenger information and costs. i would therefore prefer to reject amendment no 33 as it stands. i am prepared to accept the inclusion of a commitment to revise the text. i take this opportunity to emphasize that the commission is constantly monitoring the proper functioning of the measures adopted and does not hesitate to amend them if necessary. last week, as part of the semiannual review, the commission postponed the introduction of a limit on the size of hand luggage for one year in order to review the merits of the measure and, if necessary, to ensure better preparation for the implementation of the measure by operators. amendment no 37 gives member states the option of not applying measures which they consider disproportionate. this provision, if adopted, would risk undermining the system of harmonised eu-wide basic levels of aviation safety. we risk going backwards, to a patchwork of 27 uncoordinated national regulatory systems, and we risk undoing five years of harmonization efforts. the amendment would also risk calling into question the 'one-stop-shop' system for intra-community flights. a series of amendments - nos 10, 67, 77, 79 and 81 - are intended to facilitate the conclusion of security agreements with third countries. the intention of these amendments is good, but unfortunately they are not linked to a mandate. they only make the task more difficult, contrary to their purpose. therefore, chapters 4 and 5 of the annex to the proposed regulation should be reviewed on the basis of the council's common position, in order to determine the aspects on which the commission could reach agreements. but i do not think that these amendments are necessary to achieve the objectives set. that is why the commission prefers to reject them. mr president, mr costa, you have raised, for the council in particular, the main stumbling block: the financing of aviation security. obviously this is an important debate. i am content to approach this question in a more pragmatic way. europe needs an instrument to ensure that the standards adopted in the field of aviation safety are applied in a manner that ensures a level playing field. and these fair play rules must apply both within the european union and to global competitors. but it's true: who pays the bill? i would like to take one of the amendments proposed as an example. it considers that member states which introduce more extensive and more burdensome security procedures than those required by eu legislation should compensate operators for the additional costs incurred by these stricter measures. i understand the logic behind this point of view. it is also true that the commission has a tool in this area, namely the treaty rules on state aid. so we shall be considering the appropriate use of these rules in the context of financial support measures for providers in the field of aviation safety. i fully understand what president costa said to the council. but i would like to draw parliament's attention to the inconvenience of delay in the introduction of an improved technical regulation on aviation safety. the danger is that this draft will be held hostage to community regulations, in return for financial decisions to be taken at member state level. i in writing. the proposal for a directive on criminal measures to ensure the enforcement of intellectual property rights requires member states to punish any intentional infringement of intellectual property rights when these acts are carried out on a commercial scale. counterfeiting and piracy are clear crimes. so far, no problem. i'm not sure i can do that. i cannot, however, support the zingaretti report for a number of reasons. the limited list of property rights increases legal uncertainty. it is not possible to discourage innovation, creativity and investment by companies if they are found to be unwittingly violating these rights and are immediately prosecuted for this. the concept of 'commercial scale' is also not sufficiently clear. does this include the street musician? does it exclude personal use? i also have serious doubts about subsidiarity and proportionality. it is not for the eu to determine the nature and level of penalties, especially where personal freedoms are concerned. nor can criminal prosecution be privatised, as the report suggests in article 7 (investigation teams under the collective rights management). people are entitled to clear legislation, and this report has failed to do that.

member of the commission. mr president, the commission strongly welcomes the excellent compromise reached by the european parliament and the council on the rapid border intervention teams regulation. as you know, over the last few years the european union has experienced an increasing need for providing operational assistance to the member states which, due to their geographical location and the complexity of their external borders, have to shoulder the heaviest burdens in terms of border control. in response, the european union created the frontex agency in 2004 as a measure for channelling the solidarity between member states and the community in terms of operational cooperation. moreover, a new external borders fund will be used from next year for ensuring financial solidarity, by boosting the capacity of all member states to deal with the challenges posed by their different external borders. the establishment of a mechanism for the creation and deployment of rapid border intervention teams is a further measure of solidarity. it is an important step forward in the cooperation between member states and the community, by controlling the external borders of the european union and performing checks on people at these borders. the rapid border intervention teams will become a highly trained and specialised reserve of border guards, which will be deployed at short notice, by the frontex agency, to a member state in need of such assistance. as something new and ground-breaking in this regard, the rapid teams will be enabled to carry out all necessary functions related to the checks on people at external borders, in the same way as checks are performed by the national border guards of the host member state. in this context, the commission would like to make the following oral statement on the international law of the sea and international protection obligations. each member state participating in operations coordinated by the frontex agency on the high seas remains fully bound by its individual obligation to respect the principle of non-refoulement as enshrined, in particular, in the geneva convention relating to the status of refugees of 28 july 1951 and the united nations convention against torture and other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment vis-à-vis all people under its jurisdiction. when an interception or rescue operation is carried out in the territorial waters of a member state, the community acquis on asylum is applicable. this includes the dublin regulation. consequently, in the absence of any other relevant criteria, the member state on whose waters the interception or rescue operation is carried out, would be responsible for examining any asylum claims. these principles remain fully valid in cases of future deployments of rapid border intervention teams following the adoption of this regulation. as the commission underlined in its communication of 30 november 2006 on reinforcing the management of the southern external maritime borders, it is not clear under what circumstances a state may be obliged to assume responsibility for the examination of an asylum claim when the interception or rescue operation takes place on the high seas or in the territorial waters of a third country. similarly, it is unclear under what circumstances the member state hosting an operation coordinated by the frontex agency could be considered as ultimately responsible for compliance with this principle. evidently, the further development of an integrated system for managing the external maritime borders should be based on a clear common understanding of the member states’ protection obligations. to this effect, the commission suggested that member states should address these issues collectively and pragmatically, either in the context of broader bilateral or regional agreements or through the development of practical guidelines in close cooperation with the international maritime organisation, the united nations high commissioner for refugees and other relevant stakeholders. in order to support this process, the commission will soon publish a study on the law of the sea addressing these and other relevant issues. the publication of the study will be followed by an expert meeting with member states to identify the practical follow-up, having regard to the limits of the community’s responsibility in this area as well as the oral statement. finally, i would like to stress once again that the commission is very pleased with the good cooperation between the three institutions involved in reaching agreement on this extremely important piece of new community legislation and would like to warmly thank the rapporteur, mr deprez, the shadow rapporteurs and the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs for their excellent contributions to achieving success on this file.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i am glad to say that the fact that the draft regulation known as rabit can be put to the vote in this house tomorrow with a view to an agreement at first reading is due to the fact that our three institutions have cooperated in what i would call an exemplary way. firstly, the commission, whose initial proposal was of a high quality and which has not ceased, throughout the process of discussion, to show a great capacity to round off corners and to facilitate compromise. the council, especially during the finnish presidency and now the german presidency, has repeatedly stated its determination to achieve and has spared no effort to convince all the member states. i would like to thank in particular the last presidency, mrs monika schmitt-vockenhausen. and i, on behalf of parliament, have been able to count from the outset, and i want to thank them publicly, on the strong support and confidence of a majority of the non-attached rapporteurs from the other political groups in the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs. i want them to find here the expression of my gratitude. the substance of the problem to be dealt with, mr president, is both simple and urgent. the aim is to organise the assistance of the member states of the union to those of them who have to cope with the sudden and massive arrival of illegal immigrants wishing to cross the external borders of the union. we know that this problem is now affecting the southern countries of the union - and we are constantly reminded of this by dramatic images. but no one can exclude that in the future other frontiers, particularly in the south-east or east, may be subjected to the same sudden and recurrent pressures. the draft regulation before us confirms four main principles which i have always vigorously defended on behalf of parliament. first principle: solidarity in the field of external border control is not an option, it is an obligation. the draft regulation provides for the member states to contribute to the rapid reaction pool and to make border guards available to frontex at its request, unless they themselves are faced with an emergency situation which they have to deal with. second principle: border guards in reserve, when deployed in the territory of another member state as part of rapid reaction teams, are not substitutes or second-rank officers to the member state's border guards. of course, the members of the teams will receive their instructions from the host member state, but otherwise they are on an equal footing with the national border guards. the tasks they can perform are the same. they are entitled to wear their own uniforms, with a european badge attached. they may carry their service weapons in accordance with the national legislation of their home member state, except in the event of disagreement between the two states concerned. they can be allowed to consult national and european databases and their accreditation document, which was foreseen in the original draft, has been cleared of the vexatious elements, which in my opinion it contained. the third principle, and i say this particularly to mr catania, is that respect for fundamental rights applies in all circumstances. the draft regulation provides that: first, members of the teams, such as national border guards, must refrain from any discriminatory behaviour; two, they must act in accordance with the obligations of member states on international protection and non-refoulement; thirdly, that the members of the teams must act in full compliance with their obligations under the international law of the sea, in particular with regard to search and rescue. and that is what the commissioner has just reiterated in our presence. so, mr catania, the amendment you are proposing is not necessary. i would even say that it is insulting because it assumes that border guards, including spanish, italian or maltese border guards now, would not have as their concern when they discover boats in distress, to save people. the draft regulation also provides for the full application of the european directives on the protection of personal data. fourth principle: when there is an emergency, it is an emergency for everyone. since the aim is to deal with emergency situations, the regulation provides for very short time limits for the implementation of the measures. the director of frontex shall have a maximum of five working days to decide on the intervention. once the operational plan has been established, the effective deployment of the rapid reaction teams shall take place within five working days at the latest. in the light of this, it was therefore only natural that, in cases where intervention was justified but where the budgetary resources of frontex were not sufficient to cover the cost, the budgetary authority should undertake, in compliance with the provisions of the financial regulation, to find a budgetary solution as a matter of urgency. this is the sense of the amendment which has been added to the text of the draft regulation and which gives substance to the agreement reached in this respect between the commission, parliament and the council. that is the proposal which our assembly will have to vote on tomorrow. i have no doubt and i hope that it will be widely accepted.
mr president, 31 000 illegal refugees tried to escape to the mainland via the canary islands in 2006. six times more than the year before. similar conditions are also found in southern italy and lampedusa. the human tragedy of refugees must be addressed by addressing the desperate situation in the countries of origin, not by importing transient social tensions. our group therefore welcomes frontex and the establishment of intervention brigades in countries facing the problem of mass immigration. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the control of the external borders is a matter for the member states, but the flows of refugees we have today show that these borders have become too porous. the exponential influx of illegals can therefore only be addressed by additional, but not substitute, resources to support member states. i hope that frontex can answer this question.
mr president, i voted in favour of the deprez report because the amendments to the commission's text generally contain technical improvements. the rapid intervention teams of frontex are a bitter necessity, especially in the light of the serious shortcomings of several member states in protecting the external borders. it remains to be seen how effective these shifts will be in practice. the whole concept is based on the willingness of all member states to fulfil their schengen obligations. there is now too much laxity, too much tolerance, the mass regularisation of illegal immigrants is putting the whole concept of schengen at risk. even if the frontex rapid intervention teams do a good and efficient job, all this will be a waste of time if some member states refuse to take up their political responsibility.
