in writing. the current european legislation on mandatory packaging sizes is 30 years old and no longer meets the needs of today's consumers. the new rules which we have adopted today in the second and final reading - in full agreement with the council, a big congratulatory to the rapporteur - are in line with consumer demands for a wider variety of packaging formats. throughout europe, barriers to competition will be removed and innovation encouraged. european producers will be able to decide for themselves which packaging formats best suit their customers' wishes. as regards the request in this report to extend the scope of the unit pricing directive to certain small businesses, i would like to make the following reservation. i am absolutely in favour of good consumer information. i am not opposed to the large distribution chains, which account for the lion's share of the market, imposing on their products the obligation to price by unit of measurement. however, extending this obligation to smes or neighbourhood shops is not a good thing. i shall therefore oppose this with all my might when this parliament is about to consider the revision of the current consumer legislation.
in writing. president. - ladies and gentlemen, i think the harbour report, which was voted on today at second reading, deserves the full support of parliament. today, the green light is given to a type-approval which will apply not only to passenger cars but also to buses, coaches and lorries. the mutual recognition of approvals, and this for a very wide range of vehicles, is another important step in the completion of the internal market. the introduction of this framework directive will also have a positive effect on safety, as a number of safety measures for buses and coaches will be put into effect more quickly. the simplification brought about by this framework directive, ladies and gentlemen, is a good thing for the consumer and the producer, because it guarantees a more internal market, ensures greater safety and has a positive effect on the environment. for all these reasons, i support the rapporteur in his entirety.
i voted against the rocard report, mr president, because it is yet another proof that the european union is not prepared to face up to the realities of the arab world. whether one likes to hear it or not, the fact is that there is no interest whatsoever in political reform in the arab world, much less in the cultural or intercultural dialogue that is so much praised here. the rocard report should have been a major indictment of the deplorable state of the rule of law, freedom of expression and freedom of religion in the arab countries. but instead this parliament and this report are elevating the so-called cultural determination of human rights to a new kind of norm. the situation of religious minorities in the muslim countries leaves european mandarins with a siberian cold, as we see, by the way, also and repeatedly in the attitude of the european union towards turkey. despite the hate campaigns waged by the turkish and state-sponsored imams against christians, with all the deadly consequences that this has brought, the accession process is proceeding smoothly. it is this cowardly political apology against the aggressive effects of islam that my party will continue to fight.
in writing. in voting on the rocard report on reforms in the arab world, we had to vote on amendment 21, which calls on arab countries that have not yet done so to commit themselves more to freedom of worship or the right of individuals and communities to freely profess their convictions and faith while guaranteeing the independence and separation of institutions and political power from religious authority. an oral amendment was then proposed, which sought to extend this fundamental rule to all democracies; which i totally share. some members of the epp opposed this extension. i considered myself to be in full agreement with the original text of amendment no 21. i therefore decided to vote in favour of this amendment, and i am glad that it was adopted by 382 votes to 222 with 33 abstentions.
in writing. if we want to make the quality of life in europe truly sustainable, housing is an important lever in this. the andria report on housing policy and regional policy rightly places the subject in the context of the european social model, energy policy, employment, urban development and the internal market. all these areas have their intersections and their twists and turns. after all, poor housing and poverty go hand in hand, and poverty is still increasing. the housing market has been undergoing major changes in recent years, due to the interplay of supply and demand, but also to social and demographic changes in our society. the shortage of social housing is great. housing is a basic right. it is also a fundamental aspect of regional development, both urban and rural. the report explicitly refers to the social component of housing, the energy poverty that often accompanies it, the employment that can be generated by decent, environmentally friendly housing. it also goes into more detail on the need for an integrated approach and the support of local authorities. housing is and remains a national matter, but the preconditions can be guaranteed at european level. this is what the report calls for and why it has my full support.
in writing. today, in this house, a resolution was adopted by a large majority calling for new legislation on information and consultation of employees. unfortunately, ladies and gentlemen, in my country the current directive has not even been transposed. the belgian government has long been in default and has recently been condemned by the european court of justice. when the commission put forward this proposal in 1999, it was quickly apparent that the matter was politically sensitive. yet, in essence, it is simple. all employees must be informed and consulted about the company event by means of appropriate representation and an appropriate institution. this is in the interests of both the employees and the company itself. the instruments used to give shape to this economic democracy and participation may of course differ according to the size of the enterprise. it goes without saying that we should not treat an sme in the same way as a multinational. the precise form of the dialogue between employers and employees is a matter for the social partners. the resolution we have voted today also places a major responsibility on the social partners. i'm very interested in that.

the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. the exclusion of health services from the scope of the services directive. i would like to remind you that this exclusion was the result of a decision by a large majority in this house and that we were fully supported in this decision by both the commission and the council. i think it was also a right decision, firstly because health services cannot be equated with traditional commercial services, secondly because a patient is something different from a consumer and thirdly because the member states have the primary responsibility for the organisation and financing of health care in their territory. i therefore expect that we will reach a resolution on wednesday which is consistent in this area. in the meantime, of course, health services are still services within the meaning of the treaty and are subject to the rights and freedoms of the treaty. and, as with the services directive at the time, we do not want to leave everything to the court. and here too we must reconcile different objectives. the optimum functioning of the internal market on the one hand, and the scope for a fully responsible health policy on the other. what we have to keep in mind is balance and legal certainty. a codification of existing case law on the rights and obligations of both mobile patients and mobile service providers is certainly necessary, but not sufficient. the creation of added value for people and the quality of care and the preservation of the margin of manoeuvre for member states to continue to take commissioner, it is particularly good that the commission is going to actively support the ilo strategy for decent work. decent work is more than just respect for the fundamental labour standards of the ilo, important as they are. it also means that people have the right to a living wage, the right to social protection and the right to organise. today, 1.5 billion people in the world earn less than enough to meet their basic needs. however, 90% of all countries in the world have a legal minimum wage, but often homeworkers, agricultural workers or people with precarious contracts are not covered or the law is not applied. social consultation and trade union rights are therefore crucial to guaranteeing the right to decent work for all. i am therefore particularly pleased with the joint statement by the european trade union confederation and the american unions, which together want to make decent work a point of contention. europe must put decent work at the heart of its foreign policy in order to share more equally the benefits of globalisation and to avoid social dumping in relation to wages and working conditions in more developed economies. trade agreements currently being negotiated by the european union with countries such as india or korea should support decent work and trade preferences should be withdrawn if countries systematically violate fundamental social rights. europe should also support its partners in developing countries to give decent work a prominent role in their country or regional strategy plans and in the european partnership agreements. not only governments but also multinationals have a crushing responsibility in ensuring fair wages and working conditions. multinationals based in the european union, but also their subsidiaries and subcontractors elsewhere in the world, which continue to violate fundamental rights, should be blacklisted and excluded from all possible public procurement in europe. europe will thus take the lead in international efforts to give a social dimension to globalisation.
in writing. by approving this afternoon the regulation on mobile phone charges abroad, the european parliament has adopted legislation awaited by millions of consumers: workers or tourists, who are exasperated by the exorbitant costs of making or receiving crossborder calls on gsm and who have received the support of the european commission, which has authored this legislative proposal. and although my liberal philosophy is not very well suited to interventionism in market prices, i am opposed above all to laissez-faire, to laissez-faire and to cartels in a given economic sector. this european law is a major step forward in protecting consumers, who will see their bill reduced by up to 70%. another improvement was the freedom of choice for the operator to offer the customer a choice between the regulated tariff and a flat-rate tariff which would also cover sms and mms. it is a pity that this reduction in roaming costs will not be operational for the summer season. citizens will take comfort in the clear willingness of the three european institutions to go out and reclaim their rights and to fulfil the first of their missions: to make laws that will change their daily lives for the better.
in writing. european health policy cannot be limited to regulating patient mobility or creating a single market. no two-speed policy: those who can afford it, they move to get the best care. this is inappropriate because it undermines social and territorial cohesion and solidarity. health care - like other social services of general interest - is often part of the national social protection system which guarantees citizens that their fundamental rights are respected. for these reasons, health services were excluded from the services directive by this parliament last year. we can't undo this today! the newly adopted text, which examines the impact and consequences of such exclusion, calls on the commission to develop an appropriate instrument to codify the case law on the rights and obligations of mobile patients and carers. i don't think this goes far enough. the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council at the next meeting of the european council in brussels. health is a basic right. everyone has the right to move to another country for better care. each healthcare provider and the member states concerned have a duty to treat all patients equally.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the public authorities. the epp-ed group has broken an earlier compromise with the pse group which protected health services by keeping them outside the scope of the services directive. fortunately, the epp group members decided in the plenary vote to respect this compromise, and to respect health care, by refusing to make health a commodity. the vote was a victory for the socialists' will to defend accessible, high-quality and affordable health services for the citizens of the union.
in writing. until now, the generalised system of preferences (gsp) has guaranteed acp countries privileged access to the eu market, with lower import tariffs at eu borders and better market access. this made it easier for developing countries to export their products to the richer european countries. this wto agreement is a formal exception to the most favoured nation principle of non-discrimination. the 2000 cotonou agreement aims to abolish this exception by the end of 2007. individually negotiated economic partnership agreements (epas) will replace them. otherwise, any wto member can report discrimination. the report before us is rightly critical. it is essential that the european commission negotiates the epa taking into account the development of the acp countries. the principle of total market liberalisation must not be allowed to play its part, because this is a real threat to them, both socially and economically. the report does not take into account the realities on the ground. whether the epo will have a positive or a negative impact remains a question. it is not surprising that the countries concerned do not want to sign these agreements before the end of 2007. if parliament does not take the time pressure off and is not prepared to allow the sap+ system to continue, i cannot support the report.
president, prime minister, the dutch electorate has voted massively against the european constitution. i can only hope that your government will continue to strive for a new treaty and not a constitution. i also hope that the voters will have the opportunity to express their views on this, not only in the netherlands, but in all the member states. but this debate is not just about the institutional question. it is about the future of europe, and therefore also about the fundamental question of the eu's borders, about whether a non-european country like turkey can join. when you took over the eu presidency in 2004 and came to the european parliament for the first time, you said that turkey would be strictly monitored to ensure that it met all the copenhagen criteria. can you, mr prime minister, three years later, hand on heart, declare that this arrangement has been respected? turkish-armenian journalist hrant dink was murdered after being convicted by a turkish court as a criminal. orhan pamuk, nobel prize winner for literature, has fled abroad because he too is threatened. for the turkish penal code, it is a crime to allegedly insult turkish identity. so there is no freedom of speech. not to mention the murder of three christian publishers and a catholic priest. i could also talk about the fact that turkey still still refuses refuses to recognize a member state of the european union and to meet its obligations in relation to the opening of its ports and airports. the current showdown between islamists on the one hand and the supporters of the secular state on the other should also be cause for concern in europe, but the european union continues to negotiate as if there were no dirt in the air. the vast majority of the population who are against accession are ignored. and whether one likes to hear it or not, that arrogance and that alienation from the world is the number one threat to the survival of the european union. member of the commission. madam president, i would like to begin by saying that we acknowledge, understand and appreciate the seriousness of this disease. not only is it a serious disease, but it also affects young men and women in europe and all over the world in the most productive period of their lives; people who have usually already formed their own families, have young children, have all their life ahead of them and then have to go through these difficulties. also, we appreciate the importance not only of the european parliament’s resolution but also of the code of best practice, which was drafted by the platform. as i have already said, the commission also participated in the first european conference on multiple sclerosis last month, and that conference was organised in the context of our public health programme. it was a project funded by the programme ‘multiple sclerosis – the information dividend’ and led by the european multiple sclerosis platform. of course, many of the aspects relating to this disease have to be dealt with by the member states, so it is a gradual process that will take some time, but the commission is there to help, support and encourage. at the conference, many essential points were presented concerning the follow-up to parliament’s resolution of 2003, especially regarding the discriminatory treatment afforded to people with multiple sclerosis. the commission is taking action in the main fields covered by the proposal regarding a code of good practice on the right of people affected by multiple sclerosis to quality of life. some actions target multiple sclerosis directly; others are of a more general or, let us say, horizontal or generic nature but, again, they are of benefit to the issue of multiple sclerosis as well, for example, the issue of equal rights and access to treatment and therapies for multiple sclerosis. the commission, within its more general framework of safe, high-quality and efficient health services, is reinforcing cooperation between member states. it is helping create reference networks for diseases, exchange of best practice and centres of reference, and the objective of these will be to ensure equal rights to patients and equal level of treatment all over the european union. so, once these networks are established, they can cover multiple sclerosis as well, especially with centres of reference and exchange of best practices. on research, on the other hand, we have a more directly targeted agenda and initiatives. the seventh framework programme includes collaborative activities on research into brain-related diseases, human development and ageing, with particular emphasis on how to turn basic discoveries into clinical applications. one first call proposal for this topic was neuro-glia interaction in health and disease, which is of direct interest to the multiple sclerosis research community. also, under the preceding programmes (the fifth and the sixth), the commission funded 18 projects on multiple sclerosis, the largest of which, in the sixth programme, totalled eur 11.4 million and related to neuro-protective strategies for multiple sclerosis. on the issue of employment and discrimination, which is important for us, directive 2000/78/ec already prohibits discrimination based on disability in employment, occupation and vocational training, and requires employers to put in place reasonable accommodation tailored to the individual case to enable a person with a disability to work or undertake training. the commission is committed to encouraging actions and promoting strategies for the adaptation, and reintegration into working life, of affected persons. on the issue of participation and empowerment, the project i mentioned earlier, ‘mutliple sclerosis – the information dividend’, will facilitate consensus on agreed european quality standards on information about multiple sclerosis, which has to be accessible, delivered in a timely manner by appropriately skilled and experienced professionals and provided in an equitable and efficient way. finally, i would like to inform parliament that the commission intends to discuss with member states a proposal for a large european consensus conference on neuro-degenerative diseases, including multiple sclerosis, to be held in 2009 or 2010, and the commission will be able (on the basis of conclusions endorsed both by public and private stakeholders at that conference) to propose a communication or other relevant policy initiative, including specific actions in all the fields of disease prevention, research and management of this group of diseases. we will, of course, keep parliament informed of all the next steps.

in writing. directive 98/49/eec guarantees the rights to supplementary pensions of employees and selfemployed persons moving within the eu, but does not provide any guarantees as regards the transferability of supplementary pensions and the flexibility of the acquisition conditions. the commission's proposal for a new directive addresses this. it lays down time limits and proposes three minimum rules on the portability of rights: whether they remain in the company (dormant rights), whether they are taken away within a certain period, or whether they are paid out in cash (up to a certain amount, threshold). the new directive should apply to all supplementary pensions based on an employment relationship. i am particularly sorry that mrs oomen-ruijten's report excludes the proposal for the portability of pension rights from the directive. now it is only about the dormant rights. i believe that the new directive should do everything possible to support workers who invest in additional pension rights. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. i can only support the report if the directive retains the broad scope proposed by the commission. otherwise, i'm against it.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: mr president, the excellent report by mrs kratsa, whom i thank for her work, contains two important findings. first, aid to palestine since 2003 has been well managed and free of fraud. this is a crucial element that will silence the persistent and baseless rumor of corruption in the leadership team. secondly, the temporary aid mechanism set up after the 2006 sanctions against the hamas government has unfortunately not succeeded, despite the money invested, in preventing a humanitarian disaster with increased infant mortality, the emergence of new diseases and extreme poverty in the occupied territories. however, since the emergency government was established after the dissolution of the national unity government, aid seems to have miraculously been restored and even the taxes withheld by the israelis could be returned to the new government. i'm glad you did. but as the country, engulfed in civil war, is torn in two, this dramatic reversal of position calls for a new kind of reflection. the european union has been responsible for its policy in the middle east since january 2006. this policy was aimed at isolating hamas, at depriving it of its electoral victory. it ignored the advances of the political platform, on which the government of national unity had been built; i am sure that we have had our fair share of reports in this parliament, but i have the impression that the pirker report is actually the best we have had on refugee policy. what do we read in the report, literally? we read there that a forward-looking common asylum policy is based, and i quote, 'on the obligation to admit asylum seekers and on the principle that return cannot be an option'. this is not only pure legal and international nonsense, but also a completely wrong political signal. i believe that a truly forward-looking asylum policy must first of all be based on the reception of genuine asylum seekers in the region and on the continent of the asylum seeker himself, that this reception can only take place in strictly controlled centres and that only those who are recognised as genuine refugees after a rigorous investigation can possibly be temporarily received in a country of the european union. the list of safe countries is of course crucial in such a policy. i regret that the pirker report is going in the wrong direction in this respect, as we are unfortunately accustomed to in this european construction.
author. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i have spoken in this house many times about the situation in burma and the words repression, military junta in power, human rights violated were already all over my speech. the resolution we are voting on today is a call to burma to stop certain actions. i share the view of my colleagues that the main cause of the situation is the house arrest imposed since 30 may 2003 on aung san suu kyi, the burmese opposition leader who turned 62 on tuesday and who has spent 11 of the last 17 years in house arrest. she has only been allowed to leave her home once, for health reasons involving urgent care, and has little contact with the outside world. aung san suu kyi is the emblem of burma's opposition to the military dictatorship; she actually won the nobel prize in 1991. it is absolutely unacceptable that she should be placed under house arrest. we therefore call for his immediate and unconditional release. burma must absolutely stop persecuting and imprisoning pro-democracy activists and must release those already in prison. i am thinking in particular of u win tin, a journalist who is now 77 years old and has been imprisoned for almost 20 years for writing a letter to the united nations denouncing the poor conditions of detention of political prisoners. as vice-chairman of the asean delegation in this parliament, i see the growing role that asean can play in the region. may it have a positive influence on the burmese government in the near future.

president. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we have been working for more than ten years to open up the european market for postal services, as the commissioner said, and today we are deciding on the final stage, on what is to be done with the reserved part of the 50 grammes. if this part of the old monopoly is also removed, we shall have in a few years' time a postal market which has every chance of becoming more dynamic, which will be fully open and which will be able to operate in a competitive and transparent manner. the commission proposal itself did not seem to offer sufficient guarantees for a cautious liberalisation, but thanks to the negotiating skills of rapporteur markus ferber and the good cooperation of other colleagues, a broad political agreement has been reached in the committee on transport, which gives postal operators in the first place extra time to prepare properly. as not all member states were equally advanced on the post-liberalisation track, this is a wise move. and, mr president, in other areas too, the ferber report is a giant step forward. for example, postal workers do not necessarily have to fear negative social consequences, although we must of course remain vigilant in this area. equally crucial is our commitment to guaranteeing universal service to users. of course, the mail still needs to be delivered daily and users need to be able to send mail from their local area. of course, the national plan that the member states have to submit to the commission in connection with the financing of their universal service is a key element. colleagues, after the vote tomorrow, we expect a balanced text. it is then up to the council, and certainly also to the member states, to deal wisely with our voting result.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. the postal directive is one of the most important dossiers. it affects not only over a million workers but almost all citizens. that is why we have not had an easy time in the committee on transport and tourism. i would like to thank the rapporteur because with the amendments and the compromises we have taken important steps forward and have considerably improved the commission's proposal. what was important to me was this: first, service should be the focus. secondly, what will be the role of the member states in the future? this should be clearly defined. service should be the focus this does not mean for me that tomorrow will mean cheaper wages, worse service and possibly even higher prices! unlike some of my colleagues who are a little more pessimistic, i believe that the current draft has given the member states an important role. they can also fulfil this role, because they can receive the service in terms of distribution and collection. the design takes account of citizens' expectations of the direct service. social dumping can be avoided if the member states so wish. we have also received petitions in our country on the subject of the postman, with the tenor 'keep our postman in his role'. we have not only taken this important task into account in our draft, but we have also made it clear that the member states can retain this profession. there are different models of financing. i am of the opinion - but this is not feasible at the moment - that perhaps some part of the reserved area would have offered a secure financing opportunity. but we are in the first reading, and perhaps the second reading will bring some changes. monopolies must be broken, but not at any price. i have already explained our conditions for this prize: first, service, second, preservation of the profession and third, guarantee of financing. and lastly, for me, the elimination of monopolies means not creating new monopolies. overall, parliament would do well to discuss the whole principle of liberalisation in greater depth again, particularly with regard to services for the public.
i support the compromise reached by the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs. i think that an open european market for postal companies is the only way to compete with electronics. they have to modernize, they have to adapt to new circumstances, and if you have the cushion of a monopoly, you're not going to look for new customers. then you'll continue the way you've always worked. we also have a clear timetable: it's 2011. everybody knows what we're up against. we emphasize the importance of public service, not just for the citizens, by the way. this public service is also of particular importance to small businesses. and, a tricky one, how do we compensate for any loss of that public service? the member states have a major role to play in this, as mathieu grosch has already said, but i would still like to say that this compensation should not be used to enable postal companies which have always done poorly to continue to do so. we need to make the postal sector more efficient and i think that will be one of the important consequences of the proposal we are voting on tomorrow.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to make two points. the first concerns the question of practical experiences that go beyond speeches. the question is, first, whether the widespread competition of postal operators leads to a relative fall in prices and, if so, who benefits. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of the services provided is not compromised. we as members of parliament have been informed by trade union organizations and by the postal operators themselves that the experience of germany and sweden does not provide positive answers to these questions. in germany we have recorded a net loss of 29 000 jobs at deutsche post, of which 15 000 have not been compensated by job creation by new operators, and we are also seeing a rise in precarious employment. in sweden, price reductions have benefited only large enterprises, at the expense of individual consumers and small and medium-sized enterprises. so, contrary to what mr simpson has said, it is not for theoretical reasons that we are advocating the retention of the reserved area; it is for the sake of the environment that we are advocating it. it is not because we decided in 1997 that the mass was necessarily said. i think we have to look at the facts, and the facts show that in many countries that have anticipated competition, the results are not what they should be. this should make us think. my second point concerns the responsibility of the member states. as mr grosch has said, the proposal before us leaves a great deal of autonomy to the member states in terms of both working conditions and the provision of services. that said, i do not think it is our responsibility as elected members of the european parliament to draw up a european law and leave it to the member states to decide whether to do so or not. that is why our amendments introduce, in the body of the directive, in its articles, requirements for compliance with working conditions and wages and for the provision of universal service. and that, in my view, is the big difference between the green amendments and the compromise amendments which the epp-ed and alde groups and a section of the socialists support.
the reform of the postal market is a very sensitive subject, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, because the impact of the opening up of the market will of course vary from country to country, depending on geography, the degree of urbanisation or the amount of mail people usually send. it's also, as brian simpson said, a network of people that everyone is confronted with. the compromise which will be put to the vote tomorrow is one which i welcome with mixed feelings. on the one hand, it is certainly true that it is far removed from the original commission proposal and the rapporteur's position on crucial points. thank you for your flexibility, mr. ferber. the definition of universal service remains unchanged. two years of extra time have been taken. the member states will have to indicate before that date what financing model they envisage, and in the social field we have obtained that the licensing system will allow all operators to be subject to the same wage and working conditions. that is essential. on the other hand, there are still many unanswered questions. is sustainable financing of universal service possible in a fully liberalised market? and this in all circumstances? unless, of course, you assume that the taxpayer is footing the bill. we have not yet received sufficient guarantees in this respect. i think it would have been better if, instead of focusing on a date and deciding to move towards liberalisation, we had first checked country by country whether the opening of the market is the best way to modernise the system further and to have a good level of service. the compromise is a step forward, but it is not enough. there's still work to be done. the ball is too much in the member states' court and therefore i will certainly support some of the amendments which did not make it through the committee but were tabled again - including by the greens/efa group.

mr president, i'm in the middle here. a small side note in curtailing the use of mercury. that's what everyone's for. this is a good thing, because in many sectors the use of mercury has been phased out and indeed needs to be replaced by other materials. but a total ban on handicrafts, such as the traditional mercury barometer, is too far-reaching in our view. i recently visited the company dingens in the belgian municipality of leopoldsburg, and that company has been proving for decades that mercury barometers can be produced in a sustainable, environmentally responsible way. moreover, such a traditional barometer has an unlimited life span, unlike its successor, the digital barometer, which runs on batteries and thus consumes energy. the manufacturers of traditional barometers have also become adept at the sustainable maintenance of these instruments. no mercury from barometers is leaking into the waste stream. i therefore call on the commission, madam president - and i will stop here - and on all those concerned to send a signal to the sector concerned and to take account of the mercury barometer producers' justified specific situation in the evaluation, which is now two years in the making, so that they may, i hope, retain the right to an exceptional system.

member of the commission. on 13 june 2007, the commission adopted a final communication, which follows on from the 2006 communication. in this communication, the commission fully supports the member states' objective of combating illegal labour market practices. the commission recognises that national authorities must carry out appropriate checks to ensure that service providers comply with national working conditions. however, an assessment of the progress made since 2006, carried out by the commission on the basis of information provided by member states and the social partners at eu level, shows that some of the measures applied in some member states are disproportionate and go beyond what is necessary to ensure the effective protection of posted workers. as indicated in the communication of june 2007, this appears to be related to a lack of effective cooperation between the competent authorities in the member states and a lack of access to information. in its communications, the commission recalls that the free movement of services, which is one of the fundamental principles of the ec treaty, can only be restricted by rules which can be justified by overriding reasons of general interest, such as the protection of workers, and these reasons must be proportionate. whether the national measures in question, such as the requirement to have a representative resident in the host country or the obligation to keep certain documents relating to social and working conditions on its territory for inspection purposes, can be considered legitimate and proportionate, must be assessed on a case-by-case basis. the commission will now thoroughly assess the situation in each member state to determine which control measures are to be considered unjustified and disproportionate. if necessary, the commission will initiate infringement proceedings to ensure compliance with community legislation. i would like to stress that the commission is also examining whether the measures taken by individual member states are sufficient and effective and, if they are not, will follow the same procedure, since the objective is to protect workers effectively without restricting the internal market. the commission does not intend to publish further guidance at this stage. the final decision on the compatibility of the national measures in question is to be taken by the court of justice, which has already given judgment, and not by the commission or the parliament. as regards cooperation on information, the checks carried out by the commission show that encouraging improvements have been made. however, further improvements remain necessary. the commission therefore proposes to improve and strengthen administrative cooperation with the member states, including the involvement of other stakeholders, in particular the social partners, who play an important role in the enforcement of the rules. the evaluation of enforcement actions shows that although the application of the directive has not led to many formal complaints or legal proceedings, some issues, such as the effectiveness of cross-border sanction enforcement or the protection of workers in the case of multiple subcontracting, merit further consideration. the commission intends to involve the social partners and the member states in this reflection. in conclusion, i would like to emphasise that the june 2007 communication states that the commission does not, in principle, wish to call into question the different social models of the member states, nor the way in which individual states organise their systems of industrial relations and collective bargaining. it is, of course, the commission's task to ensure that european community law is strictly applied and, in such cases, where disproportionate measures are taken which are not aimed at protecting workers but which have the effect of restricting the freedom to provide services in the internal market, the commission must of course act as required by the treaty.
i am glad to say that the commission has taken the initiative in this area. on the one hand, it is of course good that the commission is continuing to work on the implementation of the posting of workers directive, because almost every week we hear of new cases of social exploitation of posted workers, and this situation also put a great deal of pressure on working conditions in our countries. but on the other hand, we have the strong impression that the commission still does not strike the right balance between guaranteeing freedom to provide services on the one hand and protecting workers on the other. it is clear, commissioner, that a number of control measures introduced by member states, such as the compulsory retention of documents at the workplace, the imposition of posting certificates or the presence of an authorized representative, are essential elements in order to protect the working conditions of posted workers. it is also clear, commissioner, that member states can only rely on their own national measures, because the much needed transnational cooperation between administrations and access to the necessary information are virtually lacking, and international enforcement mechanisms simply do not work. but the communication suggests that a package of these measures is not compatible with community law and even threatens infringement proceedings before the much needed steps have been taken to ensure the flow of information, to make cooperation between member states really work and to set up an effective system of sanctions. of course, my group welcomes the commission's intention to set up a high-level standing committee to help the member states to develop their cooperation. but administrative cooperation, commissioner, is not enough. what we are also asking for is a european platform for crossborder cooperation between the competent inspection services of the member states. as long as this aspect is not regulated, a crucial instrument for enforcing the posting of workers directive is lacking.

we have voted with the flemish socialists in favour of all the amendments, including the compromise amendments which represent progress compared with the original commission proposal. but we ultimately voted against it to send a strong signal that we're still concerned. in fact, a lot of homework was given to the member states to ensure that liberalisation was brought to a successful conclusion. they will have to ensure that their social legislation applies to all operators. they will also have to ensure that by 2010 it is clear how they will finance universal service in a competitive market. we thought it would have been better to wait a while before making a final decision.
in writing. i voted, as did a large majority of my colleagues, for the ferber report, namely a total and controlled liberalisation of postal services by 1 january 2011. the compromise adopted today is in fact the result of two dynamics which are dear to me as a liberal: the dynamic of an open market economy which is incompatible with monopolies established in law or in fact, and the dynamic of the special interests of consumers who must benefit from real and fair competition, mainly in terms of prices, but not only! because another factor is paramount: the human dimension. and i am glad that the vote this afternoon took account of this by introducing a social regulation clause, by recalling the essential role of postal services in terms of social and territorial cohesion and by giving the member states the final say on the financing of the universal service. these are all positive signs which should reassure european postal workers and also help to break the confusion which some left-wing members of parliament have cleverly cultivated: the liberalisation of a sector does not mean its privatisation, this european directive does not mean the closure of post offices!
in writing. the ferber report is the next in a series of reports on the liberalization of universal services, which still holds out serious opportunities to guarantee the principle of equality in a social europe, alongside the ideal of freedom. postal services may compete with each other, but whether this will improve quality and lower prices remains to be seen. i think it is irresponsible that the new directive does not provide clear guarantees for the quality of the so-called reserved service, that its financing remains unclear and that the social agreements laid down in collective agreements should not be compulsorily respected. if the market opens fully in 2009, it is uncertain whether more remote areas will receive the same service at the same price as densely populated areas. the ability to send and receive letters at a reasonable price is a basic right! in addition, postal service providers will do their utmost to keep their costs as low as possible in order to compete with each other. fixed jobs will turn into insecure part-time jobs. the postmen will be underpaid, there will be many redundancies, more post offices will be threatened with closure, and soon we will have blue, yellow and green mailboxes in addition to the red ones! i do not support the report.
in writing. the vote on the liberalization of the postal services today is the result of a compromise between the epp group and the socialist group. however, this compromise clearly implies the definitive liberalisation of the last part of postal activities managed by the public authorities, and this without any guarantee as to the financing of the universal service and the maintenance of employment for the 1.6 million european postal workers. as a socialist and shadow rapporteur for the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, i could only vote against this green light for liberalisation, despite the improvement compared to the commission's original proposal.
in writing. president. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, when you see the advertising campaigns for air tickets, you get the impression that you can fly around europe for a few euros. when the payment is made, the sober up. suddenly, the price of the ticket is being increased by all sorts of additional costs in the form of tolls, surcharges and taxes. parliament is putting an end to these evil practices today. that is a good thing and i have therefore strongly supported the report by the committee on transport. the airline consumer has the right to a fair and transparent price without unpleasant surprises. the european parliament is once again taking the lead in protecting air passengers, following the introduction of additional rights to protect passengers in the event of their flight being rescheduled, cancelled or delayed. it is important that member states provide for swift and effective penalties in the event of non-application of these new provisions. trust is good, but control is better.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but we are totally opposed to the idea of extending social protection at european level. the references in the report to the principle of subsidiarity and the method of open coordination are mere appearances. we therefore say very clearly that any attempt to introduce social protection at european level is contrary to the principle of subsidiarity and that this is pushing the european union further down the path of a centralistic superstate. the subsidiarity principle means that the european union can only act to promote crossborder labour mobility. the intervention of this parliament is further illustrated by its call on the member states to no longer close off their labour markets but to open them fully to workers from the new member states. we therefore say 'yes' to flexibility and 'yes' to social protection, but 'no' to a european superstate which is trying to impose itself in this area too.
in writing. europe is not a social europe until it succeeds in establishing a system of minimum social standards for each type of worker in a single market with free movement. the commission's green paper on the modernisation of labour law already addresses this. the protasiewicz report calls for any reform of labour law to take into account principles such as gender equality and non-discrimination, decent work, the protection of all workers (regardless of the type of contract) and the necessary role of the social partners and other representative organisations of civil society in the development of labour legislation. improved labour law is able to adapt to change, protect workers and reduce uncertainty. if labour law is to meet the challenges of the 21st century, it also needs strong instruments to curb the growing informal economy and exploitation. the report also calls for greater convergence between member states to increase legal certainty, for example on crossborder work. i will support the report, although i miss a reference to equal pay for equal work and it lacks some paragraphs of force, for example on working hours, training (access rather than right to) and the link between labour law and lifelong learning and education.

the commission's vice-president mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank mrs ayala sender in particular for her report on the logistics of freight transport. i should also like to say to mrs ayala sender that i remember our visit to zaragoza, where we were able to see the value of all the efforts currently being made in europe to develop logistics. your report, madam president, gives us great hope for the commission's approach in the communication it adopted in 2006 on logistics. this resolution is an important step in the joint efforts of parliament and the commission to improve the functioning of freight transport in europe. i feel very encouraged as i will be presenting the action plan for logistics in a few weeks' time. madam president, you have spelled out the issues very clearly: the economic dimension, the performance of logistics, which is a key factor in the competitiveness of our economies. logistics facilitates the flow of goods and helps to make our products competitive by giving them access to more distant markets. logistics has thus become an important, dynamic, job-creating industry and europe has the best performing european logistics companies in this field. in addition, logistics contributes to environmental performance. as you said, it helps to reduce congestion. freight transport and logistics must also meet the challenges of reducing road accidents and emissions of pollutants and noise. efforts in the logistics sector are not aimed at regulating this growing sector, but at securing a sustainable future for it by enabling it to mobilise the potential for efficiency that still exists in the transport sector. this is why we have developed the concept of co-modality, the efficient use of different modes of transport in combination with each other. in this respect, logistics offers us the opportunity to reconcile economic and environmental objectives. however, a number of obstacles to the development of logistics remain. firstly, the lack of harmonisation and the absence of common standards which prevent the adoption of new solutions, in particular the development of information and communication technologies. we could plan freight transport better, we could improve the operation and safety of transport systems, we should improve the tracking of goods and ensure that customers have the information they need. the supply chain must function as a coherent whole, even if it includes several partners and combines several modes of transport. secondly, we must strengthen the importance of quality in freight transport with indicators of service quality, with the sharing of good practice, which implies improving the training of professionals, the recognition of their skills. thirdly, we must tackle the problem of bottlenecks, obstacles to the transport of goods. we consulted all the logistics actors and received five hundred responses which allow us to better prepare the logistics action plan. the report by mrs ayala sender also rightly points to the simplification of administrative procedures, the need for investment in infrastructure and the difficulties of logistics in an urban context. we need concrete answers to all these questions. i am glad that the committee on transport and tourism has really attached importance to the action plan for logistics which i will present in october. this action plan should propose concrete measures with clearly identified objectives and a precise timetable. this logistics action plan will be part of a package of initiatives including a freight-priority railway network, a port policy and two commission staff documents on the maritime space without barriers and on motorways of the sea. the adoption of these various measures will be an important signal and it is in this way that we can come up with efficient, integrated, environmentally friendly and, of course, user-friendly transport alternatives. the action plan will be accompanied by measures to make rail, sea and inland waterway transport more attractive. the commission alone does not claim to solve the freight transport problems facing europe, but on this point too, mrs ayala sender's report sends a message to the member states to encourage them to develop their own initiatives in favour of logistics, in particular by investing in infrastructure, in close coordination with the european union. we will only succeed if the member states and the european union act together. the logistical problem, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, is a major problem. it is by working on this that we will achieve the modal shift we are calling for in the fight against global warming. i believe that it is through good logistics and good transport infrastructure that we will make european mobility sustainable, that is to say compatible with our environmental requirements for the good of the economy and for the good of the european citizens. mrs ayala sender, let me sincerely thank you for your contribution to these advances in logistics, which is a key to the development of sustainable transport.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i think that, on the whole, we have reached an honourable compromise in the negotiations with the council. i would like to thank the members, in particular the shadow rapporteurs, mr el khadraoui, mr rack, mr zile and others, and the chairman of the conciliation committee, mr vidal-quadras roca, who i think has conducted the negotiations very well, and of course the german presidency, as mr savary has already said; i think that mr tiefensee and his people have worked very hard on this and have shown a great deal of persuasiveness in the council. i would also like to thank the people of the commission, the commissioners and their services, because i think we have also received a lot of help from that angle. achieved what? i said an honorable compromise. i think that parliament has got a number of important things out of the way here. firstly, there are basic rights for all rail passengers, which was not the original intention. basic rights include: availability of picket lines, liability of the railway undertaking for its passengers, for baggage, for what it offers in services, insurance; it also means the right to transport for disabled people, no discrimination. i think that is a very important point that we have reached for all train travellers. ensure personal safety for all train passengers. these are things that railway companies will have to guarantee for everyone from the end of 2009. secondly, i think it is important that we have made the distinction, where it was necessary, not between cross-border or national but between long distance and short distance and that we have therefore said, on the basis of objective data within one european market, look for certain things for those types of train travellers, for others we cannot; so we make a different distinction than national or international. i think that's not unimportant. for long distance, we have established compensation and assistance for delays, including for people who are regularly late, for example commuters and subscribers; we have assistance for disabled people and people with reduced mobility at stations and on trains, especially for long distance travel, and hopefully soon for all travellers. we've got complaints handling in the directive. we have made a distinction between local, urban and regional transport where we allow exceptions, but where we allow those exceptions only after they have been defined by the member states and under the supervision of the commission. i think it is not unimportant that member states can now say: look, we make distinctions between regional lines based on criteria that we set ourselves. we put some very precise things on paper there. i think that is the merit of the european parliament. i have had to do my best to convince the commission to abandon its original distinction between international and national and then together we have succeeded in convincing the council to do the same. the first was, i have to be honest, easier than the second. we have had to overcome a great deal of resistance in the council and i think we have been able to make a great deal of progress there. but we have to admit that things are not going as fast as we would have liked. that there's a three-year, five-year exemption for long distance. but again, this exception is only possible after the member states and railway companies have given the european commission the reasons for it. it is not unimportant that one should not just get an exception, but that one should also give reasons for it. i think that together, by the end of 2009, we will have taken a major step forward. the basic rights. we are entering a debate which railway companies and member states, regulators in the member states, can no longer avoid. they must start to do so, they must start to discuss with the passengers, with the train travellers, what their rights are, how they are going to guarantee them; i also realise that it is a debate which will take a very long time. the railway companies are not the fastest companies for a number of objective reasons, in some cases not only because of their history and business culture, but also because they are very heavy investments that require time. i thought that we as a parliament should take this into account. in any case, railway companies and member states can no longer avoid discussing higher quality, better service to the customer. i think that the third package in general is a step forward towards a european railway market. an open market, a dynamic market, where different players bid for the hand of the customer, in such a way that they are also forced to take care of that customer. my report on the rights of workers, i think, is a contribution to that; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i think that the report by gilles savary on driving licences is an important step forward. in an open market, you need things like that to ensure that trains can go anywhere and that the border is not really a barrier. i regret that we did not take the necessary steps in the georg jarzembowski report on the market. that we continue to distinguish between crossborder and non-crossborder and that we do not do for national rail transport what we would do for international rail transport, namely open up the market and ensure that as many players as possible have a chance in that infrastructure. i think we as a parliament have missed an opportunity there. everyone knows the reason why that happened. i do not know whether we would have made a success of our discussions with the council, but i am sorry that we did not go all the way, as the british so nicely say. i think, mr president, that parliament has done what it could do, that we have urged the council to go far ahead and go as far ahead as possible. did we want more? of course we would have liked more, but i hope that a large majority of our colleagues will accept that parliament has weighed in on the decision-making here, that we have taken all our chances in the mediation and that we have taken a step forward in the european railway market which is important both for the citizens and for the union's economy.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we have come to the end of a very long process and the result is, of course, a compromise that makes no one completely happy, but also no one completely unhappy. we all want a bright future for rail transport, but we have a slightly different view on how to achieve this. i repeat what i have said before, namely that i do not believe that liberalisation alone is a miracle cure in all circumstances, in all situations. on the contrary, in many cases it will cause additional problems, especially when it interferes with the provision of public services at local or regional level. what certainly works - and railway companies in a number of member states have proved this - is additional investment in comfort, punctuality, an attractive pricing policy and that of course also requires political will. i am therefore personally very pleased that we have succeeded in adopting a balanced proposal on the liberalization of rail passenger transport. we want to open up the market for international passenger transport, to make it more attractive without completely opening up domestic passenger transport. the opening of the market is accompanied by cabotage, but this can be somewhat restricted if the economic balance of public service contracts is jeopardized by this cabotage right. i know that a majority of my colleagues, although not a qualified majority, were in favour of this, of liberalising national passenger transport, but although that might not be a problem in some countries, i am convinced that it would certainly be in a number of other situations, if only because of the great differences in the density of the networks and their structure; a tailor-made approach by each member state is therefore appropriate. this directive, together with the public service regulation, will provide a solid legal framework to enable this tailor-made approach. as regards passenger rights, it is clear that we in this parliament, across all party lines, wanted to be a lot more ambitious and aimed at a high level of passenger rights for all rail passengers throughout europe. it is regrettable that this is not the case for urban and regional transport. it is also regrettable that long distance transport on domestic lines can remain exempt from the application of this regulation for up to 15 years, but what is certain is that within two years all passengers on international trains will be able to enjoy a number of basic rights and that some member states will be able to be more trendsetters by not making use of the long transitional period. that is what we should all be calling for here, the european mill is grinding slowly but surely. gradually we will strengthen and improve passenger rights. in view of the results of a regulation on passenger rights in the air transport sector, i would call on the commission to carry out the necessary evaluations relatively quickly and, if necessary, to take new initiatives to close loopholes so that this regulation does not turn out to be an empty box. finally, commissioner, i would ask you to ensure that the content of the regulation is communicated to all rail passengers in a timely and widespread manner so that they are fully aware of the rights conferred on them.
president, commissioners, the recent recall campaign by a toy manufacturer has clearly and rightly served as a wake-up call. it is now up to us to deal with this problem rationally and thoughtfully. if, after years of efforts to ensure a high level of consumer protection, including product safety, we are still sometimes confronted with situations where non-compliant goods are produced, imported and placed on the market, then this is of course not a pleasant situation, but perhaps we cannot avoid it completely. but despite this, the european citizens, the european consumers, can expect us to continue to make every effort to put a stop to this. the case which is the immediate cause of this debate concerns toys from china. i'd like to make a call anyway so we don't get stuck on that. not on china, because there are other unsafe goods coming into the market. we sometimes produce them ourselves. it is better to make good agreements than to start protectionist actions. we should not focus on toys alone, even though one in four products found to be defective is a toy. we must ensure that the safety of products in general is guaranteed. we do not need to devise yet another new approach, but we do need to close the gaps in the existing system. we are working on this with the proposals in the single market package for goods. i would like to make this clear to the people; the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. we always aim for a high level of consumer protection and this time it appears that this is indeed the case. the internal market and consumer protection are not contradictory issues, but are absolutely compatible and we must continue to pursue these paths. i have one question to put to the commission, madam president, we are calling - it is in our joint resolution - for an assessment to be made as to whether a voluntary safety label is appropriate in addition to the ce marking. i would like to ask the commissioners what their reaction is to this.
madam president, what is the lesson to be drawn from this mattel case? the first point is necessary, and it is a parenthesis, but it is important, and it has already been said. the successful conclusion of this business saga is partly due to the sense of responsibility of the company's managers, who took the necessary decisions: a massive withdrawal and effective communication. my second point is to congratulate commissioner kuneva, who has met our expectations perfectly: the efficient use of the rapex system, which could certainly be further refined, and the message of firmness she has sent to the chinese authorities. a third comment, which expresses frustration. in reality, the mattel case is almost a chronicle of a failure that has already been announced: the failure to supervise ce marked products in the union, in this case toys, but they are not the only ones concerned. in 2004, during the debate on the eco-design directive, i denounced, together with others who are here this morning - i see karin scheele, peter liese, claude turmes - the presence on our european market of other dangerous products bearing the ce mark. there are certainly interesting ideas in our joint resolution: the revision of existing directives, increased cooperation, in this case with china, more resources for customs services and national supervisory authorities. the two commissioners present stressed this point, and it is essential. i take this opportunity to regret the absence of a council official this morning, whose presence would have been essential. in conclusion, if europe wants to protect the safety and health of consumers, it must, of course, as it has done, hunt down and prosecute cheaters, but also ban the marketing of consumer products containing dangerous substances, such as cmrs and endocrine disruptors.

in writing. the liberalisation of the railways is not an end in itself, it must serve the users and efficiency. in international transport, liberalization is a matter of course, since no country should be able to close itself off from the other. in national transport it cannot be the purpose of liberalization to leave the profitable lines to competition while leaving the unprofitable lines to the public service without compensation. in this context, i look forward to the application of the recently adopted regulation on public passenger transport. the european parliament has been able to win the battle for passenger rights against the member states and the railways, which is a great success for the citizens of the eu. it is particularly in transit countries such as belgium that a distinction between international and national passengers would be incomprehensible and unfair. in this sense, the third railway package is a step in the right direction, although it is certainly not to be the last.
in writing. the report on the road map for renewable energy is a good one. it clearly indicates that the coming years are crucial and that a legislative framework under the co-decision procedure is necessary to increase the current 7% share of renewable energy in the energy mix to 20% by 2020. the report rightly highlights the enormous potential of offshore wind energy: a potential share of 15% of total eu energy demand by 2020, with the potential to create 368 000 jobs. the development of biofuels is rightly called into question, particularly as regards the need for sustainable production methods and the fair balance between food and energy production. the greens would like to see it put more forcefully and therefore call for a serious analysis of the impact of biofuels on the climate, the environment, the social fabric and food security. it is a pity that the report leaves the 10% target for biofuels unchanged. another shortcoming of the report is recital e, where the nuclear lobby proposes nuclear energy as a 'bridge technology'. the greens disagree and therefore tabled amendment no 2. overall, this is a good report and deserves our support.
president, ladies and gentlemen, do the south americans themselves still believe in an interregional agreement between the eu and mercosur? i think that's the question we should be asking ourselves. the mercosur countries are pursuing a two-track trade strategy. on the one hand, to develop mercosur further, but on the other, like brazil, to conclude as many bilateral trade agreements as possible in order to secure its own market position, if necessary at the expense of other mercosur members. it's like combining an argentine tango with a brazilian samba. and yet, as long as we on both sides, the eu and mercosur, continue to believe in the usefulness of broad cooperation between the two continents, there is hope, despite the pressure of us president bush to establish a free trade area of the americas. with 34 countries, the enthusiasm of the mercosur members is not very high. they are disappointed in the lack of support from the us during the recent severe economic crisis. europe is already mercosur's most important trading partner, but there is still a lot of growth potential between the two trade blocs. if we can now do something about the divisions among the mercosur countries, for example by cooperating in multilateral trade negotiations such as the wto, where the eu and mercosur often have common interests, then we may be a little closer to an interregional agreement again.

i would like to explain, mr president, why i voted against the duff report, even though the duff report is not a controversial report as such. i am opposed to the european federalist philosophy which underlies the report in any case. in short, i am against a european federal state and therefore i am automatically against european citizenship, which includes the right to vote and stand as a candidate for eu citizens in member states other than their own. i believe that the union must remain a community of national democracies, with the right to vote and stand as a candidate in any elections reserved for the citizens of the states concerned. it is indeed a sign of the evolution of the european union that european citizenship is being systematically confirmed and extended, including by making the charter of fundamental rights now binding.
i have of course not approved the gruber report on the legal migration policy plan for two main reasons. firstly, i do not think it would be wise to encourage a brain drain from the poorer countries towards europe now, even more than it is already the case. i think that no one benefits from this, especially the poorer countries. secondly, i am continually struck by the naivety with which immigration is seen in this parliament as a kind of solution to europe's undeniable demographic problem. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this has an economic cost, among other problems. today, in our european capital, brussels, some 53% of the inhabitants are of what is known as non-belgian origin. by 2050, that's going to be 75%. we do not need a new mass immigration, but the exact opposite.
mr president, in the gruber report this parliament is once again taking the one-sided quantitative economic approach to the immigration problem. i strongly reject the view that only a new wave of immigration will guarantee europe's economic future and would point out that immigration is about much more than just figures and tables. the proposals in this report will only aggravate the already serious problems of integration. one must have read it to believe it. while some immigrants do not want to participate in the labour market and do not want to be re-educated, there is a desire to set up an information and management centre for migration in mali. it is undeniable that many so-called temporary workers go into the illegal business after their permits have expired, and this is already the case. experience in switzerland, for example, clearly shows that the family members of seasonal workers also continue to enter illegally. last but not least, more legal immigration of highly skilled people will undeniably lead to further brain drain from developing countries with all its consequences.
the report on the political priorities for combating illegal immigration which we have just adopted is certainly not the worst report that has ever been presented here and even contains some very interesting recommendations, but i still think that the report fails to mention the man and the horse in a clear way and that is why i did not approve it myself. the problem of immigration in europe is threatening to become the problem of the next century, is perhaps already the problem of the next century today, and illegal immigration is an important part of that. so we should have expected a much more coherent position, especially in the face of successive waves of regularization in the various european countries. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. it is actually quite simple and we should have been bold enough to formulate it. illegal immigration cannot be tolerated. illegal immigrants must be traced and returned to their countries of origin in a coordinated and humane manner. those who do not dare to say so, and therefore certainly do not dare to act, will cause enormous problems which will ultimately cost our prosperity and our civilization.
mr president, when you read the moreno sánchez report, you automatically remember the saying: gentle healers make stinking wounds. as my colleague mr vanhecke has already said, although some of the proposals in the report are certainly a step in the right direction, parliament is still generally taking a halfhearted approach, which is not good for an efficient and coherent european policy to combat illegal immigration. for example, parliament fails to make it clear that the regularizations introduced in many european countries are one of the main, if not the main, causes of illegal immigration because of the suction effect they create in the countries of origin. illegals should not be regulated, but they should be actively tracked down and expelled in a timely but humane manner. contrary to what this parliament insinuates, the unequivocal proclamation of this political message has nothing to do with so-called xenophobia. in addition to a coherent policy on expulsion, close checks at the external borders and readmission agreements, there should also be reception centres in the region of origin, but this too is rejected by this parliament. i therefore voted against this report.
in writing. i fully support this excellent report and would like to add three comments. one: we must dispel the illusion that an active policy of legal immigration will have the effect of suppressing illegal immigration into our countries. an active policy of legal immigration, by definition, will stem from our own needs, while illegal immigration corresponds to the needs of those - and they are currently tens of millions, especially in africa - who want to come to our country to escape from war, persecution, poverty, or simply the lack of prospects for themselves or their children. secondly, the control of the eu's external borders and the fight against illegal immigration channels will unfortunately remain one of our political priorities for many years to come, because in reality the industry of poverty has fallen into criminal hands. the fight against illegal immigration is therefore not "against" illegal immigrants: it is part of the fight against organised crime that we must win collectively. thirdly and lastly, we must of course also fight against poverty, and this must be done in parallel and without delay, and this must be done through joint development in africa.
