i am afraid that my opinion of this report is somewhat different from what i have heard in this chamber, because i consider it to be a strange report, at least for an institution which calls itself democratic, because it makes recommendations on the basis of a highly controversial directive which, in my view, runs counter to fundamental rights and freedoms and which, in a number of respects, is contrary to the fundamental principles of the rule of law. the truth is that genuine discrimination against minority groups in europe or genuine racism are - and fortunately are - very marginal phenomena which are already being adequately and rightly combated. what we are dealing with in this report is something quite different. in reality, this is yet another attempt to introduce muzzle-loading regulations to further stifle free speech, to create a climate of genuine progressive opinion terror, whereby in the end indigenous people are actually obliged to give priority to immigrants. this is the most discriminatory report ever written against indigenous people. in particular, i consider the reversal of the burden of proof in discrimination and legal proceedings, especially in the field of employment, to be absolutely reprehensible in a democratic state of law where everyone is presumed innocent until proven guilty. in short, this report is not about discrimination, it is about a kind of legalisation of political correctness.
the problem with a report like mrs buitenweg's is that it is based on pious principles and good intentions, but unfortunately it is far removed from reality. of course, discrimination on the basis of race or origin is reprehensible. the question is only whether this discrimination is as widespread as it is always intended to be. the latest annual report of the eumc (european monitoring centre on racism and xenophobia) speaks of an increase in the phenomenon, while the eumc is not able to support this statement with concrete figures. the eumc is not even able to give a generally accepted definition of what discrimination is. all too often it is assumed that almost all the problems facing immigrants are caused by the so-called discrimination they are supposed to suffer. this discourse has disastrous consequences, because it excludes whole groups of the population from the concept of personal responsibility. the hugging policy and the victim culture have only made the problems worse and i therefore welcome the minority view expressed by koenraad dillen.
mr president, with this report, buitenweg, the european parliament is taking a very dangerous path and that is why i voted against it, together with my group. the combination of the right to bring proceedings against so-called equality bodies, the reversal of the burden of proof in court cases and even more prohibited grounds of discrimination will undoubtedly lead, in our view, to a witch hunt, a hunt for people with a different and critical opinion. because that's what it's about. europe wants to set up a new inquisition against anyone who refuses to accept the dogmas and the multicultural society. this has nothing to do with eliminating discrimination against minority groups, but with the legalisation of a certain political correctness. if we want to tackle real discrimination, we must also finally address the situation of women within the muslim community in europe, or put pressure on the applicant country turkey in connection with the open discrimination against religious minorities, but that does not fit the european parliament's image.
president. - apart from the previous speaker's reservations about some kind of new treaty for a european constitution, which is being pushed down our throats in a totally undemocratic way, the european presidency has apparently also set itself the main objective of achieving a breakthrough in the dossier of a common european immigration policy. the least that can be said about this is that such a policy at european level, decided in the pre-bedroom scenarios with particularly obscure decisionmaking by the commission and council, as we unfortunately already know, would be totally undemocratic. the problem of immigration affects our citizens personally and in their private environment, and to shift decision-making on this to a european level where virtually no one has a say is not only undemocratic, but also downright dangerous. the portuguese proposal to 'steer' illegal immigration as it is called through legal immigration is too stupid to be allowed to go unchecked. illegal immigration must be tackled by a policy of 'peek-a-boo', by a concerted policy of detection and expulsion, by close checks at the external borders and by european detention and reception centres in the countries, or at least the continent of origin. as for the so-called absolute necessity of legal immigration, i would point out that we already have some 20 to 25 million unemployed in europe, including millions of unemployed immigrants with all the consequences of that. a new wave of legal immigration with all its attendant so-called family reunification will only increase the particularly dramatic problems of integration and assimilation exponentially. and if it were really only the highly educated - which i dispute - then we would be organising a brain drain from the poor countries which is simply outrageous. or is the aim - the council will have to say - to keep countries poor and underdeveloped?
first of all, mr president, i should like to express my doubts about the timing of the resolution we are discussing. it is true that the european council in june asked parliament to submit a draft on the future composition of parliament, but now we find that the whole proposal is based on the reform treaty, a text which has not even been ratified yet, let alone entered into force. in other words, we are putting the cart before the horse. it's not the first time this has happened, by the way, and it's starting to get really shocking. i am not speaking here as the spokesman for the its group, but as the representative of a smaller member state. the question is no longer whether belgium will be split up and flanders will become an independent state. the question is when it will happen. but this aside. i think that the principle of degressive proportionality advocated in this report is the most workable and also the most just starting point, if one at least considers that the smaller member states and their representatives should be able to continue to play a significant role in this parliament. i am in favour of a broad interpretation of this principle of degressive proportionality and therefore intend to support the amendment in mr bonde's case. i think it is in the interest of the european union that the smaller member states are represented in the european parliament in the best possible way. if not, then some of the support for the european institutions among the people will disappear.
mr president, it has been said here many times, but ultimately this debate is about the credibility of parliament. the question is whether parliament is able to fulfil the task entrusted to it of submitting a proposal by a large majority, so that it is difficult for the council to dismiss this proposal. the merit of the lamassoure/severin report is that it presents a pragmatic proposal which meets all the requirements of the treaty. there are not four alternatives, there are actually only two alternatives, namely the lamassoure/severin report or the nice report. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the negotiations. now i do not understand some of the amendments tabled. it is clear that the fourth amendment does indeed strongly defend the smaller countries, but is actually a caricature of what proportionality should be. but i also do not understand the amendment which was tabled mainly on the basis of german incentives, because it does not take account of the position of the smaller countries, while it asks that they should give greater opportunities to the large member states in the council. or should i understand that this is a vicious way of returning to nice and returning germany to 99 seats? if so, i think that is very undermining for this parliament. i hope that tomorrow parliament will see that its credibility is at stake, and that it can only maintain it by supporting the lamassoure/severin report by a large majority, because that is the only realistic amendment.

- i'm the reporter. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, it is often the case that the reports of the committee on budgetary control, which is the european parliament's anti-fraud committee, do not bring good news. we are talking about weaknesses, irregularities, fraud, and sometimes even fraud. i am pleased, ladies and gentlemen, to be able to bring you a success story today, to bring you a positive story, a story that shows how cooperation between the european commission, the european parliament and the member states can solve problems. what's the context? cigarette smuggling was discovered in the early 1990s as a mega-fraud. hundreds of millions of ecu - we were still talking about ecus and not euros - disappeared into the pockets of criminals at the expense of the budgets of the european union and the member states. as early as 1994, the then uclaf - the predecessor of olaf - set up a cigarette task force. in 1996 and 1997, a european parliament committee of inquiry - the first committee of inquiry to be set up under the provisions of the maastricht treaty - investigated the whole of transit fraud. the committee of inquiry found that the transit control system was an archaic system. the commission also found that the exporting producer had not provided any evidence that the exporting producer had been unable to obtain the necessary information from the exporting producer. the document had to be returned to the customs office of importation. there were a lot of problems with that. those papers came back much later, sometimes three, four months. there was the issue of forged papers. there was also the problem of counterfeit stamps. one of the main recommendations of the committee was to replace this archaic system based on paper and stamps with a computer system. this ncts system was gradually introduced and is now operational in all member states and helps customs services to detect in real time whether goods are illegally escaping customs supervision. so this is a huge breakthrough. all this does not, of course, exclude the possibility of false declarations still being made, and we therefore ask the commission to set up a system to prevent the false declaration of goods. we also ask that olaf have direct access to this computerised system. as i said, the committee of inquiry was mainly concerned with the problem of cigarettes. so cigarettes were being transported in transit across the territory from one customs post to another, but very often it was these goods, these cigarettes, that ended up on the black market. it was also very common for those goods to be smuggled back into the union. this caused immense problems, sometimes even violence. in italy, for example, two members of the guardia di finanza were even killed. the whole investigation also revealed that some tobacco manufacturers were colluding with the smugglers and on the basis of these indications the commission, together with a number of member states, brought a case against philip morris in the courts of new york. as time went on, it became clear that philip morris would have a particularly difficult time winning that lawsuit, and in 2004 a deal was struck with philip morris. in exchange for $1.25 billion, to be spread over a period of 12 years, the continuation of the trial in new york court was waived. but the second part of the philip morris agreement was actually perhaps even more important. it contained provisions on cooperation between philip morris and the european union in relation to the tracing and tracing of cigarettes. if shipments of cigarettes were found by the customs authorities and the provisions of this agreement were not followed, philip morris undertook to pay the customs duties evaded immediately. and that can go up to a million and a half euros per container of cigarettes. i repeat, this is a success story. it shows how parliament and the commission can work together. but there are also some criticisms. the commission and the member states will share the $1.25 billion. only 9.7% goes to the commission and the lion's share goes to the member states. i think that is unfair, especially since olaf and the commission are doing the lion's share of the work. a second point of criticism. in the spirit of the agreement, this money was to be used for the fight against fraud. the commission has done its job. she donated some of that money to the herculus ii program. the member states, however, simply let it circulate in their national treasuries and should actually use that money to equip their investigative services, for example the customs services, better and to fight this kind of fraud more vigorously. again, mr president, this is a success story that could perhaps be used for other products. i'm thinking about alcohol. i am thinking of some agricultural products. and i hope that with this report i have put a positive note on the important work that the commission, mr kallas and our parliament are doing in the fight against fraud.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's air transport sector is not left behind. the commission has also taken steps to ensure that the european union's air transport sector is not left behind. at the same time, we know that this is only an intermediate stage and that there is still a lot of work to be done to achieve the more or less integrated transatlantic aviation market we have in mind. some colleagues believe that we have already made too many concessions to the americans in this first phase. the spirit of negotiation would have disappeared, particularly since us airlines already have de facto the right to cabotage flights from one member state to another, something we cannot do on us territory. both parties are given what we call fifth freedom rights, which allows american airlines to fly through europe to africa or asia. the reverse is also possible, but less interesting because of the geographical location of the usa. in addition, from march next year the restrictions still in place on access to certain airports - think of heathrow - which today only two american airlines are allowed to use will be lifted. so there is no real balance in a sense, but on the other hand, commissioner, the agreement is clearly a step forward and an improvement on the present situation. first, the agreement puts an end to the legal uncertainty that arose following the judgments of the court of justice in 2002 which found that the existing bilateral open skies agreements contained illegal elements that needed to be amended. secondly, consumers will feel the difference. whereas until now only national or american airlines could fly to the united states from the member states, in future any european airline will be able to fly from any point in europe to any point in the united states and possibly also to fly through to mexico or south america, for example. now, this no doubt means more choice for the consumer and perhaps lower prices as well. thirdly, the establishment of the joint committee - to which you also referred, commissioner - with representatives of both sides creates an institution which will discuss issues relating to the agreement, but also matters relating to regulations in the social field, in the field of the environment, in the field of safety, etc. so it is a tool that can increase mutual trust and understanding. this is very important because, of course, in parallel with the opening up of the transatlantic market, it is essential that the regulations gradually become equivalent and harmonized and that this be understood at a high level. as the authorities get along better and the benefits of close cooperation become more apparent, the willingness on the american side to make new concessions on market access in the united states itself may also increase. there are, after all, a number of outstanding issues - the commission has already announced that it will be presenting a report on the implementation of the new strategy for the sustainable use of pesticides. given the major impact on the quality of food, public health, the environment and the viability of our agriculture - all of which are areas of concern - not only our rapporteurs and shadow rapporteurs, but also our whole group, have devoted a great deal of time to this package. i would like to make four points in particular. firstly, a further reduction in pesticide use is desirable; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that it is not able to meet the needs of the environment. we do want to reduce the actual impact, based on a scientific evaluation of the risk. secondly, our reasoning is analogous to that of the spray-free zones around watercourses. here too we are in favour of a risk-based approach and we believe that the member states are better placed than we are to make responsible choices, guided by criteria such as those laid down in the water framework directive. thirdly, i wonder why this parliament, which is in favour of an internal market with a high level of protection for consumers, health and the environment, would oppose a first step towards harmonization in this area. we prefer the idea of zones with internal mutual recognition proposed by the commission and would also like to see voluntary inter-zonal mutual recognition. finally, mr president, we oppose the generalized neighbour information proposed by the rapporteur, mr breyer. for a highly regulated activity, that information is not only unnecessary, but also leads to unnecessary fear and frustration. it can't be the intention to go into some kind of private enforcement. that is a society we do not want, that we do not support.

in writing. this afternoon, the european parliament delivered a broad message that responds to citizens' concerns about less dependence on pesticides, sustainable agriculture and the health of farmers and other users. a step forward: the health protection aspect, which was totally absent from the 1991 regulation, is now clearly affirmed through the recognition of the principle of substitution (aimed, as in reach, at encouraging less or no chemical alternatives), the priority given to the protection of the most vulnerable populations and the prohibition in principle of aerial spraying. it was essential to fill a legal gap concerning the use phase by professionals and individuals. this will be done with the framework directive which takes account of the new habits, the garden fashion: pesticides have entered our gardens and our homes! i am very sorry, however, that the klass report rejects the commission's new approach, the so-called integrated approach, which consists in allowing the use of all available agricultural techniques, giving priority to those which are environmentally friendly. i insist and i sign: integrated production is the common sense applied to agriculture in the 21st century!

vice-president of the commission. we are taking this very seriously, and there will definitely be proposals. we are discussing these issues. first of all, it must also be considered that all these administrative and control functions have also been some kind of consequence of development in the commission, where the managerial functions have been evaluated or assessed as a much more important function. also, their supportive systems have been enhanced. we are discussing at this moment how to proceed, and definitely before a new call for cuts in our posts comes we will be ready with an adequate project. we are currently discussing all these overheads and parallel functions, and inside the commission there are, of course, different views about this. it is no big surprise that some horizontal services want to have more rationalised services, while other services present arguments in favour of the existing system. so we are having a debate, but i can assure you that there will be a clear next step in this screening exercise.
vice-president of the commission. i am sorry that i cannot answer in finnish immediately, but we can have further discussions in finnish. i am a little afraid of not using the right terminology, which must be so exact in the european union. like the council, the commission has concluded an agreement with its staff trade unions, putting a certain amount of human resources at their disposal. in the case of the commission, on the basis of the resources agreement of 2001 and annual resources protocols, 12 so-called secondment posts are granted to the trade unions on a permanent basis out of a total of more than 23 000 posts at the commission. such secondments to trade unions, on the basis of their representativity, have been allowed since 1989. furthermore, a very limited number of posts are granted on a flexible basis, in particular in order to account for changing majorities after elections. the staff thus put at the disposal of the trade unions continue to be paid a salary from the eu budget as commission staff. in addition, the trade unions receive a budget which allows them to recruit up to nine contractual agents from function group ii (secretaries).
vice-president of the commission. i can certainly answer that on the basis of what i know. there are 12 seconded experts in the commission's trade union. in addition, there are 19 posts in the staff committees, which are separate bodies. they can hire nine secretaries. what you said about the strikes is partly true. it is true that the commission has reached a negotiated solution whereby during a strike it is possible to pay part of the salary but not the full salary. i've always thought it was a great thing that people who are on strike are paid half their salary.
vice-president of the commission. the budget for the trade unions is approximately eur 500 000 per year, which is given from the community budget.
member of the commission. since the beginning of its current term and up to the end of september 2007, the commission has adopted 21 decisions imposing fines on companies that have infringed the anti-trust rules. the total fines imposed during that period amount to just over eur 5.2 billion, 95% of which has been imposed on cartel participants. however, it should be borne in mind that such fines become part of the european budget only once they are final: that is, if no appeal has been lodged by the undertaking on which a fine has been imposed or when all possible appeals have been rejected by the european courts. as companies frequently challenge the validity of the commission decisions imposing fines, a large part of the amount mentioned above is not final, so to speak. the possible use of the proceeds of fines for additional activities, as suggested by the honourable member, is prevented by legal constraints. such use would run counter to the budgetary principle of universality as set out in articles 17 to 20 of the current financial regulation. according to that principle, total revenue covers total expenditure without any specific link between a given item of revenue and a given item of expenditure. the basic objective of anti-trust fines as laid out in the preamble to the 2006 guidelines on the method of setting fines is to ensure deterrence, in other words to discourage companies from behaving illegally. in time it is to be hoped that the levels of abuse, and hence the number and volume of fines imposed, will decrease as complying levels increase and we are still doing our job properly.
thank you, commissioner, for this very interesting information. what else i 'd like to know about you ? i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. am i getting this right? secondly, you tell me that - only if it is final - the money also belongs to the eu budget. can you give an estimate of how much of this large sum of five billion euros can be considered final?
member of the commission. we are not the only ones who have taken up the fight against cartels. the national competition authorities have too, and i am aware that all the 27 national competition authorities are involved in this struggle with us. we are discussing this within the european competition network. i am aware also that there is indeed a one-line policy in this case, so your example is absolutely a domestic issue and should be dealt with by the national competition authority. so there could be lots of reasons behind a rise in the level of food prices. it need not necessarily be a cartel, though you never can be sure that it is not. i therefore advise the member of parliament to take the initiative to address their national competition authority in this case.
member of the commission. the commission approved the merger of gaz de france (gdf) and the suez group in november 2006, subject to commitments by the parties, which allowed the commission to conclude that the merger would not significantly impede competition. the commitments entered into by the merging parties include, most notably, the divesture of suez shares in distrigas, the belgian gas incumbent, which has, as you are aware, also entered the french market, the divesture of gdf shares in the alternative belgian gas and electricity provider spe and the abandonment of any control, by law or de facto, suez has over the belgian gas transmission network operator fluxys. while gdf suez is allowed to remain a shareholder of fluxys, specific arrangements will guarantee that the latter is managed in an independent way, and this outcome is entirely consistent with the policy objectives pursued by the commission regarding the liberalisation of the energy markets in europe, in particular the package of proposals of september 2007. the commission decision on the merger provides that the parties must proceed with the divesture of their ownership, including a partial divesture of participation in fluxys, and with the other commitments, including those relating to fluxys governance, within a certain divesture period. the parties have to fully comply with the commitments in order to legally implement the merger. in the mean time, the parties have to respect certain obligations, which the commission will oversee with the assistance of the monitoring trustees.
member of the commission. the answer to both questions is "yes".
member of the commission. the commission will analyse the planned subsidy to volvo cars in ghent directly on the basis of the ec treaty, since the matter exceeds the eur 1 million ceiling laid down in the specific block exemption regulation on training aid. article 87 of the treaty provides that aid to facilitate the development of certain economic activities, and which does not unduly distort competition, may be considered compatible with the common market. training aid can be permitted when it creates incentives for activities which will increase the pool of skilled workers in the european union and which would not otherwise be undertaken. that is the case where the training goes beyond what is normally done in the sector concerned; for instance, because more workers are trained or because the training is more extensive. that was the situation, for instance, in the recent fiat case. on the other hand, aid cannot be authorised for training that is an accessory for the good operation of the firm concerned and that would therefore be carried out even without aid. for instance, when a car assembly plant wants to produce a new model, workers have to be trained on the new techniques and new working methods to be adopted. subsidising this type of training simply relieves the company from a cost it would normally bear. accordingly, the commission has in the recent past partially prohibited aid in favour of several assembly plants and you are certainly aware of general motors in belgium and ford genk. the proposed eight measures for volvo cars ghent relate to the introduction of a new production platform. the commission cannot, at this stage, exclude that the training concerned would take place in any case and therefore would not contribute to increasing the pool of skilled workers in the eu. therefore, the commission has decided to open a formal investigation that is without prejudice to the final decision in the case. we need more time to find out what it is all about. the belgian authorities, the beneficiary, as well as third parties, will have the opportunity to comment and to submit information before a final decision is taken.
thank you, commissioner, for your answer. and by the way, congratulations on your victory over microsoft. what i was going to say. i understand that the case is still pending. i am right in thinking that even in the new interpretation of the state aid rules for training there will still be sufficient scope for the social relevance of training which ensures that, if training gives workers more opportunities on the global labour market, it is training aid which is not seen as an unheard-of interference in competition.
member of the commission. mr president, first of all i would like to begin by congratulating and thanking the rapporteur, mr florenz. i think he, and other colleagues here, remember from the very first day of my hearing in the european parliament, which he chaired by the way, that i have made a very firm commitment: i would like to see europe free of tobacco. i think that, slowly and gradually, we are getting there. i would like to thank mr florenz for the work and his dedication and the commitment he has to this cause. also, on a personal note, mr president, if you will allow me, i would like to wish mr florenz a happy birthday. i understand that yesterday was his birthday. i will not mention the age as i do not want to create an interinstitutional problem, but i would like to wish him many happy returns. i would like to say that we see a trend and significant improvement in the area of smoke-free environments in the european union, as compared to a situation three years ago where we only had ireland and, i think, italy which had just introduced these bans. now we have a much larger number of states that have adopted these more comprehensive bans, even some with a few exceptions: i shall come to that in a minute. but i believe that we need a joint effort to achieve this – the commission, member states and, most importantly, the european parliament. i shall say that parliament’s contribution to the tobacco directive was of vital importance and i would like to interpret today’s report as support for smoke-free environment policies. i am also very happy that the report takes a very positive constructive approach to the commission’s green paper, in particular on supporting the commission’s view that only a full smoking ban can adequately protect the health of citizens and workers. we have to be frank about that. first of all, a comprehensive smoking ban protects from passive smoking, it is a disincentive for people, especially young people, to begin smoking, it is an incentive for many people to quit. it creates, and this is an important factor, a level playing field among the various operators, especially in the entertainment industry, because the exceptions, in order to be effective, have to provide for separate rooms, sealed, negative pressure, separate ventilation systems – all this creates a certain cost that not all operators can meet. it does create a better level playing field and, also, it is easier to enforce when there are no exceptions. we have evidence from the european union, but also from other jurisdictions which have introduced these bans before us, that there has been no negative effect on the hospitality sector. actually, in many cases the revenues went up. now, for me, what is important is a result. that is what we are trying to achieve. the procedure through which we achieve it is not very important for me, whether it is european legislation, national legislation or regional legislation in some cases, what we need to do is achieve the target. i am ready, and i would be willing to accept a step-by-step approach, provided that at the end the target, and not far away, should be an introduction of a comprehensive ban. where are we today? i would like to mention that. we already have total bans in ireland and the united kingdom – the whole of the united kingdom. we have sweden, italy, malta, lithuania, estonia and finland which have already introduced quite thorough smoking bans even with exceptions on the special sealed-off rooms as i said earlier, with separate ventilation systems. france followed this two-step approach, so as of next year will have a similar situation. then we have the same restrictions, but with more exceptions, when it comes to hospitality venues for countries like belgium, spain and the netherlands. we have also proceeded in countries like germany and austria, where initiatives are under way already to strengthen existing smoke-free regulations. we have had a green paper as you know. we had a consultation, a debate in the council and now with the contribution from the european parliament i am planning to take all these and then decide what will be the next step at european level and what action should be taken. we also have commitments under the international obligation of the framework convention on tobacco control – all these will be brought together to decide what the next steps should be. i will go to another point which is included in the report, the tobacco products directive, and i think that is a very important issue. we will produce a second report on the implementation of this directive later this year and it will analyse the state of implementation of the directive and identify areas that warrant future amendments. it will discuss and will be inspired by the report. several issues which feature in today’s report will be included in ours, for example those on the regulation of tobacco ingredients, labelling requirements for tobacco products and products and manufacturers’ liability. i would like to mention two points. one is the issue of the pictorial warnings. as you know, we have created a databank with different photographs and pictures that can be used as pictorial warnings, but i have to admit that it is very disappointing that very few member states have actually taken advantage of that. i really regret that. to give you an example, i am just going through my notes, we have now only belgium which has introduced them. romania will do it from next year and then we have the united kingdom also later next year. i have commitments from the finnish and latvian governments that they plan to introduce these in the near future and that was after many calls and urging member states to introduce them. we have many countries outside the european union, like switzerland and new zealand, which signed licence agreements with us so that they can use our pictures and our suggestions so, for me, the situation in the european union is very disappointing and therefore we are very seriously contemplating actually amending the relevant directive and creating a mandatory obligation to use the pictorial warnings. i also find very interesting the parliament proposal to have these on both sides of the package. this is something that we will study. finally, the application of the implementation of the tobacco advertising ban is now everywhere. we had problems with certain member states. we managed to sort them out, some through legal actions, others through persuasion, but now that applies equally everywhere. one specific aspect i would like to mention is the initiative we took recently to convince non-eu countries to stop allowing the sponsorship of formula one events by the tobacco industry. as you know, we do not have the legal extraterritorial jurisdiction; on the other hand, as these events are broadcast back to europe they are viewed by the european citizens, they affect young people in europe, they undermine european legislation and european policies in this area, so i have contacted both those from the private sector involved in this and the respective countries which still allow – there are only three – tobacco sponsorship and host formula one events. hopefully soon we may have some positive results but i will pursue this issue. once again, i would like to thank the rapporteur, but also the members of parliament who are very committed in this effort. for me, and i have said that from the very first day, it is very frustrating that, in the european union, with the highest levels of education and standards of living, people still die of preventable diseases. more than 600 000 people die of tobacco-related diseases. i believe that this is a lifestyle where we can encourage change.
i am glad that the commission has taken the initiative to introduce a directive on the protection of workers from the risks of smoking in public places. i feel, ladies and gentlemen, that we are now, with the resolution we are voting on tomorrow, playing a leading role in this field. the green paper initiative and the excellent report by mr florenz have certainly shown us the way. anyone who reads the resolution can only come to one conclusion: the european parliament is now voting for a zero tolerance policy on smoking. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission has also proposed an unlimited ban on smoking in public places, on transport and in closed workplaces, including in restaurants. but here my group thinks that we should take account of the decisions recently taken in a number of member states which have called for high, unpaid investments in a number of sectors. we must take this into account, but our ultimate aim must be to achieve an indefinite ban on smoking in the long term, including in these places. anyway, we've got the wind in our sails for this. if we are to believe the eurobarometer and other surveys, the people are fully behind us. and for those who still doubt the need to intervene, i would point out that since we arrived here in strasbourg on monday afternoon, there have been perhaps some 480 deaths in europe from the effects of secondhand smoke. colleagues, we must do something about this. my group too wants to make the maximum effort to achieve this and also wants to support all the supporting measures to stop smoking.
i am not going to repeat myself here: four minutes of speaking time is too precious. except to say that i welcome the broad lines of this motion for a resolution, as do most speakers. we can only applaud the information programmes and the preventive measures which will reduce the number of young people who smoke in the years ahead. that is true of tobacco deaths, and in another area it is true of alcohol deaths and road deaths. until then, i want to join the consensus. but we also have to be reasonable. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i am thinking, for example, of the green health apostles in my country who are vehemently opposed to tobacco but who, a few years ago, legalized soft drugs. that is why, ladies and gentlemen, i have a few reservations about this resolution. in principle, there should be no need for supranational regulation in this area. the fact that europe is finally respecting the principle of subsidiarity means that it wants to reconcile itself with its citizens. let me give you an example. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the tobacco sector is not left behind. all good and all that, but then suddenly the bureaucracy comes around the corner. the label must cover 30% of the package and 35% if bilingual. the text must be in black letters with a black border of at least 3 mm and at most 4 mm, the font must be in italics, and so on and so forth. europe wants to dictate everything and no one who understands in retrospect with our national states: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of tobacco in the community. but billions a year in excise taxes could then be used to finance the health costs of fighting lung cancer. forgive my cynicism. but what are we gonna do? the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. these are also questions that must be answered if one is to indulge in high-sounding lyricism. otherwise, the citizens will be fooled. unfortunately, i cannot find answers to these questions. finally, ladies and gentlemen, let us not exaggerate, because the use of such language sometimes gives me a very bad feeling. i'm shy of the big right. a smoke-free europe is a reality. what big words. what a puritan. the sinners will be conditions. in california, some want to ban smoking in private homes by 2009. can you imagine that? how are they gonna do that? with commandos and snitches? with mandatory smoke detectors in every home? is this the example we want to reflect? we're going after them? are we going to ban simenon because commissioner maigret with his pipe would be a bad example for the youth? let's be reasonable about this. in many european countries - i am thinking of italy, france, the scandinavian countries and my own country - smoking has long been banned from the workplace and restaurants. and you better be. europe was not needed for that. but i also say, "live the freedom". let us, if the restaurant owners so wish, provide a small space for those people who want to enjoy a good cigar at their coffee or their pousse-café from time to time. but who knows, perhaps in the europe of tomorrow coffee will also be banned and exiled, because caffeine is also addictive. perhaps the commission can then do better by putting people on bread and water.
on behalf of the alde group the who decision of 30 august 2003, which provided for a temporary derogation from the trips agreement, has now been converted into a permanent derogation by a new decision of 6 december 2005. in his report, mr susta makes a number of very pertinent comments on the feasibility of compulsory licensing. i recognise many elements of my 2005 report, which translated the temporary derogation into a european regulation. i fully endorse the recommendations of the council and the commission, which mr susta links to the avis conforme. the mechanism created is indeed only a limited solution to the problem of poor countries' lack of access to medicines. the eu must support developing countries in finding a long-term solution to obtain the most essential medicines at a reasonable price and encourage investment that provides local manufacturing facilities. in this respect, of course, the transfer of technology, research and technical assistance is essential. i refer to recent reports that the market share of generic medicines is set to fall again. the reason for this is allegedly the inexpensive production by pharmaceutical companies of medicines whose patent protection has expired. this should include possibilities for poor countries where compulsory licensing could then provide a solution for medicines that are still patented, in accordance with the who decision. but if we are not to give vain hope to the millions of people who still lack the most essential medicines, we will need more, much more than a mechanism whose feasibility is and remains very doubtful.

president. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in the debate on the florence motion for a resolution yesterday it was rightly noted that a large majority of the population is in favour of strict measures to ban smoking in the workplace and in the catering industry. i had hoped that this resolution would also take account of the majority of the vast majority of the population, namely that turkey cannot become a member of the european union. but i guess i've been living under a false illusion. when it comes to crucial issues, such as the constitution or turkey's accession to the european union, the opinion of the people suddenly no longer counts. turkey, a friendly nation, is not a european country. that's it. amen and out. that should close the debate. but not only is this parliament once again refusing to take responsibility, it is once again turning a blind eye to certain recent developments which again clearly show that turkey does not belong in the european union, and that the decision to open accession negotiations was a mistake. why does this resolution not mention the akp's opposition to a constitutional provision which makes conversion to another religion no longer a criminal offence? why is there such a lack of clarity on the armenian question and on cyprus? this is another example of european realpolitik, and we are in the right.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. mr president, you said in your opening remarks that relations with russia are good, at least better than what the international press is writing about. minister, are you living on mars? do you live on venus? do you dare to tell the russians what is at stake, or is the european union the end of the road for you? money and flat trade, is that more important to you than democracy and human rights? let's just say it like it is. russia is sliding towards a full-blown dictatorship with a strong leader. a strong leader who will not tolerate dissent and, where he deems it necessary, will deploy secret police forces from the fsb. a leader who does not want to relinquish power under any circumstances and uses all sorts of tricks to keep the reins in his hands after the parliamentary elections in december and the presidential elections in march. russia, minister, is evolving into a closed society where the ruling regime prefers not to tolerate peeping toms. i do not want to confine myself to theoretical considerations. the reality is that human rights in russia are under constant pressure and the state of democracy is deplorable. the reality is that free speech and freedom of the press are sliding into strict self-censorship. the recently passed law on extremism can easily be used to silence independent journalists and political opponents. the reality is that a strong and independent civil society is not desirable to the russian regime and that the position of ngos is under severe pressure from very restrictive legislation. the reality is, minister, that free elections are a utopia. only those tolerated by the regime are allowed to participate. the coalition 'another russia', united around gary kasparov, will be prevented from taking part in the parliamentary elections. finally, mr president, the situation in chechnya may no longer be politically topical, but the reality is still very worrying. there is a constant killing, people are illegally arrested and extorted, kidnappings are still daily, and torture is a common practice. that is the reality in russia, minister, and i hope you will take that into account when you speak to mr putin at the end of this week.
commissioner, president, ladies and gentlemen, the whole of this debate and the statements by the council and the commission make it abundantly clear how difficult it is to combine the necessary respect for russia, for that great and important country and that great and important people, with the promotion of our fundamental principles of the rule of law, a functioning democracy, a free media and respect for human rights. the balance is difficult to strike, especially since president putin has the ability to exploit any differences of opinion, even nuanced ones, between the member states. it has been said that there will soon be elections in russia - and we know that they will be held in a few weeks' time. in this connection i would like to stress that the conditions imposed on parties which are not represented in the duma are absolutely unworthy of a democracy. whether it is the amount of the deposit, the number of signatures required, the control of them - think of what happened a few months ago in st petersburg - there is nothing that can be said to be in line with our and, indeed, the world's requirements for fair and free elections. in other words, it does not look good at all. in addition, we are receiving disturbing reports of attempts to monitor the contacts of students or participants in visitor programmes. in other words - and i conclude, mr president - there is more need than ever for unanimity and vigilance among the member states to make cooperation possible.
in writing. i voted in favour of the richard corbett report on the amendment of rule 173 on the report in extenso, although i regret the adoption of a whole series of amendments calling for the translation of the reports in extenso into all the official languages. to the hardcore supporters of multilingualism, i would say, to use a euphemism, that it is a falsehood to say that without this extension of translation, the european parliament would be the only parliamentary assembly in the world not to have all its acts and debates translated into all the languages on paper. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the adverse effects of ionising radiation. that's the main thing, i think! in conclusion, i regret that our european parliament has not wanted to take part in a decidedly contemporary approach to access to documents: i say yes, a thousand times yes to multilingualism. but i will continue to oppose the so-called defence of linguistic diversity when it is used as an alibi for the defenders of the status quo and guilty immobility.
in writing. i voted against the proposal in the corbett report for the same reasons that i voted against the budget for the translation of the verbatim report of our plenary sessions being cut. in a parliament, the spoken word is sacred. what we say is not just a piece of the day's news, with any luck, it is part of a democratic legislative process. it is not a luxury to make this properly available in the official european languages. translation is politically necessary for proper archiving, which is also necessary for the discovery of information. we must bear the consequences of our fundamental choice of multilingualism and not let it be undone. a parliament with self-respect does not abandon its classical archiving. if we have to cut back, we'll choose better budget lines. i also maintain that a vigorous multilingualism is a form of respect for the diversity of cultures and languages in the eu and a blessing for democracy.
in writing. i supported amendment no 20 by the greens to delete paragraph 17 of the wagenknecht report on the contribution of taxation to the lisbon strategy. in order to ensure the proper functioning of our internal market, i am in favour of any contribution to tax harmonization at european level. and although taxation still remains an area of the sovereignty of the member states, it has quickly become necessary to ensure a minimum of tax coordination between the member states. in the field of indirect taxation, for example, the commission has gradually introduced a minimum rate system for excise duties in order to reduce distortions of competition. but in the report we are voting on today, paragraph 17 calls this system into question and calls for a code of conduct to replace it! i do not think that the 'encouragement' of the member states in the area of indirect taxation coordination is sufficient. i am even less convinced of the effectiveness of a code of conduct on excise duties: it would only increase the temptation to break up the community acquis, leading to the sad effects of unfair competition in this area.
i voted against the oomen-ruijten resolution because i believe that the european parliament should play a more active and ambitious role in monitoring the negotiation process with turkey. now it seems that we should not step on the sore toes of mr erdoğan and mr gül. it is becoming increasingly clear that turkey is not just another candidate country. turkey does not seem to have to be so strict about the copenhagen criteria, despite all the promises from the council, the commission and parliament that it should have been. it is therefore not surprising that more and more citizens in the european union are turning their backs on the european union.
the new vote on turkey's accession, mr president, is yet another illustration of the fact that europe is not only a far-from-my-bed-show for most of our citizens, but that the european institutions themselves are increasingly alienating themselves from the european citizens. for the eurocracy, europe is no longer europe, because the preparations for the accession of a country which is not european at all, not historically european, not euro-european, not culturally european, not religiously european, and not even geographically european, are being carried on with a sense of humour. moreover, the whole thing is being pushed down our throats in a fundamentally undemocratic way, because the vast majority of european citizens are indeed against turkish accession, but they are not allowed to speak. in the turkish dossier, as in the dossier on the new constitution which cannot be called a new constitution, the citizens are not allowed to speak. is the eurocracy afraid of democracy, afraid of referendums? this europe is only functioning undemocratically and antidemocratically, and that will all end very badly.
in writing. i have been keen to support amendments to the text being voted on today on relations between the european union and turkey, in order to recall the conclusions of the brussels european council in december 2006, which made explicit the principle that the union, in the field of enlargement, naturally required each candidate country to fulfil the copenhagen criteria in full, but also that any enlargement was conditional on the union's capacity for integration. many of you have known for some time my doubts, or rather my worries, about the ability of the european union to continue to function properly if turkey were to become a member of the union. of course, turkey is a 'friend' country which, geostrategically, represents a major partner for the union. i am therefore in favour of the european union having a privileged partnership with it. but i am strongly opposed to our union welcoming him into it. i think that the difficulties of turkey's integration as one of our potential member states will become increasingly clear as the accession negotiations progress.
in writing. i voted in favour of the resolution on turkey encouraging it to speed up its reforms. we must ask turkey to make additional efforts. for effective control of the army by the civilian population. for a zero tolerance policy against torture. for the protection of women and minorities. for the recognition of the armenian genocide. the management of the kurdish question will also have to be used to evaluate reforms. the report we voted for urges the turkish government to launch a political initiative to promote a long-term settlement of the kurdish question. and condemns the violation of iraqi territory - while denouncing, of course, the violence perpetrated by the pkk. it is not a question of targeting turkey as such, but of recalling that there will be no double standards, that we will not sell the values on which we are founded. it would also be disastrous to continue to ignore the opinion of the people, which was also expressed in the poll conducted by "our europe" before the lisbon summit. europeans are concerned about possible future unprepared accessions and the eu's absorption capacity after the large waves of accessions in 2004 and 2007.
mr president, the kacin report deals with serbia and not kosovo, and not with what will happen after december. and it is true that it does not link serbia's reaction to kosovo to the promise of membership of the european union. mr lagendijk regrets this, and my group welcomes it, and considers that the european parliament is refusing to engage in any bargaining or parallel diplomacy. it is true that there is one issue that has been raised constantly, namely the icc and serbia's cooperation with the court. so, mrs carla del ponte has just launched - i will not say a cry of alarm, but still - she recalls the intransigence and uses the european union in relation to everything that concerns human rights, conditionality, and i think she is not wrong. i do not think, as mr lang does, that it is simply a matter of giving out good and bad points. i think that the issue of nationalism, that a culture of impunity, that four criminals - not the least - are still roaming freely in serbia, is unbearable. and i will say that we know from experience that a country has every advantage in clarifying its past and bringing justice to its crimes. we asked croatia to make a tremendous effort, we asked bosnia to make a tremendous effort, and it was these same efforts, moreover, which enabled us, after the second world war, to build europe. they are really at the heart of our values, it's not about points here, it's the heart of our values and it's the key to reconciliation in the balkans. i would add, though we are not talking about kosovo, that deputy prime minister djelic, who came to the european parliament a few weeks ago, reiterated to us that whatever happens in kosovo, his country has no intention of resorting to arms. i would like to remind you of this and i hope, without linking this to the kacin report, that this appeal has been heard even among his own people.
on behalf of the pse group. madam president, mr minister, mr commissioner, the socialists have always strongly supported the principle of the ownership of development strategies by the populations concerned, so that their priorities are really taken into account. in this report we have therefore naturally placed great emphasis on the involvement of national parliaments and civil society in the new europe-africa strategy. in the current phase of preparation of this new strategy, which is intended to open up a new field of partnership, this participation has been largely lacking. it is essential and urgent to redress the situation. in this connection, we can welcome the successful initiative of the portuguese presidency in inviting delegations from the european parliaments and the pan-african parliament to the december summit in lisbon. let's hope this isn't just a symbolic gesture. we have also stressed - and mrs martens has just mentioned this - that there must be real coherence between the various european policies. this means that measures taken in the context of our trade, agricultural, fisheries and immigration policies, in particular, must be particularly attentive to their impact on the development of the countries of the south and africa in particular. we also recalled that the european union had promised to do everything possible to achieve the millennium development goals and to combat poverty as effectively as possible. in this context, we ask that the new europe-africa strategy clearly recalls its commitments and specifies the concrete measures to honour them as far as africa is concerned. as regards the economic partnership agreements, our position is very clear: we are in no way opposed, for any reason, ideological or otherwise, to the signing of agreements which would lay down the terms of a relationship which would be beneficial both to the europeans and to the peoples of the acp countries. we will, however, firmly reject any agreement which, once signed, would place the peoples of these countries, particularly those in africa, in a less favourable position than they are in today. that is the subject of the amendment we are proposing on this subject. finally, i would like to stress that at the moment we do not yet know the text that will be proposed at the next lisbon summit. you told us it was being drafted. we will therefore be very attentive to the way in which the recommendations contained in our report have been taken into account in the drafting of this text and of course we reserve the right to react to them as soon as we become aware of them.
the report by mrs martens contains a number of interesting recommendations, although i must admit that my group has some difficulty with the somewhat negative tone and the lack of a coherent, clear vision of the future in the report. seven years after the first summit in cairo, the portuguese presidency is trying to draw up a new eu-africa strategy together with the commission in what i believe is a fair attempt to get rid of the old model of give and take. it is of the utmost importance that this summit takes place. not just from a negative motivation, not just from a constricted reaction to china's growing influence. on the contrary, the growing awareness on both sides that europe and africa are no longer each other's exclusive preferential partners offers a unique opportunity to forge a whole new, much more balanced relationship. at first glance, the water between europe and africa does not seem too deep. however, for africa, any new partnership must essentially move away from the traditional aid dependence and the culture of charity and conditionality. there is a growing call for more industrialisation, for more private sector development and for more investment in the knowledge economy. fortunately, there is also a growing awareness in africa that it is primarily up to the individual to solve his or her own problems. europe must now make clear how it intends to support these promising developments without falling into the trap of paternalism, meddling and interference. any future partnership between the eu and africa will inevitably have to be based on the principle of mutual accountability. in this sense, unconditional abolition of agricultural export subsidies would, more than any other gesture, greatly enhance the eu's credibility with our african friends.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) on the conclusion of the protocol establishing an association between the european communities and their member states, of the one part, and the republic of south africa, of the other part (com (97) 0406 - c4-0173/97). but while i greatly appreciate the rapporteur's expertise in the field of development policy, i still feel that this report does not adequately address the root cause of africa's problems, nor does it address some very fundamental challenges. that may not be politically correct to say, but it is. contrary to what this report claims, africa and the european union today do not share the same view on greater democracy, good governance and human rights. the main cause of the poverty, hunger, insecurity and socioeconomic problems which plague the continent - and which are rightly listed in this report - are precisely the very bad and corrupt regimes which make no effort to respect good governance, democracy and human rights. for example, no one is disputing that mugabe is a criminal who is destroying his country and terrorizing his own people. but what do the countries of the southern african development community say? that the elections were conducted correctly and that the western countries should now mind their own business. allow me to question the promises of those same leaders about good governance. the report also rightly addresses peace and security. in africa, with its myriad undemocratic regimes, arms spending still far outstrips the massive development aid that goes to it, oxfam says. so all problems often come down to the same root cause. finally, i must say that i cannot agree with the paragraph on immigration. for anyone who thinks that the concept of circular migration can stop the brain drain from africa and the pressure of immigration on europe is, i fear, deluding himself in this respect.
