author. madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, after losing ten of his men in the attack on haskanita, general agwai, who heads the african union mission in the sudanese province, sounded a real alarm, saying that his troops were under-equipped, too few in number and likely to be defeated very quickly. so far, this situation has remained unchanged and attacks similar to the one on 29 september are still possible. the international community, and the european union in particular, must hear his cry, and the european union could make a very strong appeal for the good cooperation of all the member states involved in the support promised by the european union. in two days' time, important talks are due to take place in libya between the government in khartoum and various rebel forces. it is still unclear whether abdel an-nour, the founder of the sudan liberation army, now in exile in france, will participate in the talks. we do not know anything more about the other representatives of the various rebel forces, since their numbers change every day. in any case, these talks must be given priority, since they could lead to a ceasefire. we are also asking the european union authorities to communicate very widely the importance of this meeting and the european union's intention to support any positive progress that may result, both militarily and in terms of improving the working conditions of humanitarian personnel who are facing a particularly difficult situation. i would also like to remind you that, so far, the khartoum government bears a huge responsibility for the dramatic situation we are experiencing today, and not only because of its support for the arab janjawid militias which are spreading death and terror among the black african population. it is also the one which has been refusing, for months, to accept the presence of a western force in sudan. it is also the one which prevents foreign journalists and observers from accessing the most troubled areas, whereas the work they could do there would be of great help in continuing to mobilise international public opinion. we know that this is a necessary mobilization to help us to make everyone in europe take responsibility for what we must call by its proper name, namely genocide. it should also be noted that the future european force, which is to be deployed in eastern chad and northern central african republic, will make it more difficult for the rebels to move freely across the borders and to recruit fighters, willingly or by force, in the camps for refugees and displaced persons. in the face of this blockade, some armed groups are already turning to the other side of darfur, in the neighbouring kordofan region, of which haskanita is one of the gateways. here again, the support that the european union has pledged to provide could therefore prove very useful but also, very quickly, very insufficient. it is therefore our responsibility to continue to do everything we can to ensure that the political conditions for effective european intervention are met. we have, i think, seen enough violence and despair in this part of the world; the fact that the darfur conflict has already claimed hundreds of thousands of lives and has also destroyed the social fabric of the region makes the task of reconstruction more difficult.
the death of ten african union peacekeepers is, of course, another regrettable fact in darfur. this parliament has been rightly concerned about the region for years. but i regret that another opportunity to highlight china's responsibility and role in this conflict is being missed. because it needs to be said again: china's quest for influence in africa is playing a pernicious role in conflict-torn sudan; the european union must not allow itself to be drawn into a situation where it is not able to take action, and this must be done on the backs of the victims who are dying in darfur, and now indirectly on the blood of the soldiers who are trying to play a stabilizing role. the chinese, with their dubious investments, have made a huge contribution to the war effort of the regime there. europe must finally realize that as the chinese gain power in africa, our western message of democracy, freedom and good governance in africa is becoming increasingly muted. but yes, china is too important a trading partner for europe, and when realpolitik comes around the corner, the discourse on human rights and democracy is all too often put on the back burner.
president, i congratulate mr schmitt on this excellent report, and i congratulate commissioner figeľ on the white paper on sport. let me say that again clearly, because we are taking a very important step forward, especially with regard to the sports clause in the treaty on european union. the european union's involvement in sport, ladies and gentlemen, is not obvious to everyone. but some aspects of sport, whether we like it or not, must be seen in a european light. i 'll take one element out of it: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's sporting activities are not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. that can be perfectly integrated into the open-air policy, as we call it, also at professional sports clubs. sport, ladies and gentlemen, is an excellent instrument for giving socially disadvantaged groups of young people a chance to return to school and training. i think that aspect of sport also deserves maximum support. then, of course, there's the obesity problem. that call for an urgent approach. more and more young people, we read in mr schmitt's report, are being affected. in the eu of 27, one in four children is already overweight, not because these children eat too much, or because they eat the wrong way, but mainly because they don't move enough. the report therefore calls on the member states and the regions to devote a minimum of three hours a week to sport and to include it in the curricula of primary and secondary schools. that call came in no time. we cannot impose that from europe. we in europe do not want that either, of course, but we can brand the member states and regions that do not do so as the worst pupils in the class. it is also an economic necessity, because, as you know, childhood obesity is one of the causes of cardiovascular disease, also in later life. so we have every interest in investing more time and more energy in school sports facilities.
president. - mr president, i should like to begin by congratulating our rapporteur, mrs gutiérrez-cortines, and thanking her for her enormous efforts to strike a balance between those in favour of this directive and those against it in this very difficult matter. i myself am very convinced that a european soil protection directive is necessary for a number of reasons. i hope i can convince mrs mcguinness to do the same. some colleagues reject the framework directive because their country already has far-reaching legislation on land. my region, flanders, has also been pursuing a progressive soil policy for many years. for example, we have long had a compulsory soil test for the transfer of property, something that is still unthinkable in many eu countries today. i would therefore like to reverse the reasoning of my colleagues. this flexible directive not only provides a framework for member states which do not yet have a soil policy, but it can also help to avoid undermining the competitiveness of countries and regions which are already making efforts today. for this reason it is very important that all member states take the measures proposed in the report. cross-border impact is certainly there in many places, erosion in flanders could for example cause mud in the netherlands or vice versa. the directive also provides a coherent framework for existing legislation relating to soil protection. i have a number of other reasons, but for the sake of time i would like to conclude by saying that the report we are discussing today provides for all the needs mentioned much more than the original commission proposal. i myself have tabled a number of amendments and i am very pleased with the result. the proposal provides for a wide margin of manoeuvre for the member states, avoids additional administrative burdens or duplication of work and explicitly recognises the role of regional authorities. i therefore hope to have the support of my colleagues in the vote tomorrow.
for the greens/efa group mr president, mr president of the commission, commissioners, you have announced 26 strategic initiatives and 61 priority initiatives. i'm not going to comment on all of them in four minutes. in this type of debate, it must be kept in mind that the commission has a responsibility, a very special role in the community decision-making process. you have the monopoly of the legislative initiative and you have the power to filter through amendments, both to parliament and to the council. you have a great responsibility in the kind of results we get in terms of community legislation. i can therefore assure you that, as every year, the green group is in fact probably the most united group in support of this community method, because i think the commission has the formidable task of having to deal with the national interests and the sensitivities of the various national governments. to get to the heart of the matter, there are three points i would like to make. first, the energy policy. 2007 is a big year. the nobel peace prize has been awarded and the european council in march adopted objectives which we would have liked to have been even more ambitious, but which we supported. 2008 is, as you have indicated, now a year in which we are going to have to put things into practice. here we need to be very vigilant. we were disappointed with the results on car emissions. we believe that your approach to biofuels ignores the negative environmental impacts of this type of technology. we believe that in the second phase of emission allowances, we must move to an auction system and not give away free of charge what is in fact new financial assets. we are a little surprised that you are only announcing studies of a methodological nature on the internalisation of transport costs. it seems to me that this is a debate which has been going on among economists for at least fifteen years and that the commission could draw on good national experience. we also think, and this is the message that needs to be conveyed throughout this whole energy policy area, that you have to resist the idea, to please different lobbies or to meet different governmental demands, of using too many soft instruments. we need to use binding instruments, because the example of the car shows us that voluntary agreements do not really work. in the field of renewable energy, we do not think it is a good idea to allow flexibility between member states either. we all have to do our jobs. the second subject i would like to discuss is immigration policy. you have a very great responsibility in this regard. i remember kofi annan's speech to the european parliament a few years ago. it would be wonderful if the european union were to be awarded the nobel peace prize for a successful immigration policy. we are counting on you to ensure that this immigration policy, which you announced in your work programme and which has already been launched, does not end up with the adoption of a biometric passport common to all the member states. i think it would also be - beyond the disastrous situation - that it would represent for people an absence of credibility of the european union vis-à-vis the rest of the world. finally, the last point i would like to mention is the absence in your programme - which also surprises me, mr barroso - of the question of international financial markets. if you want citizens, or a large majority of them, to have a more positive attitude towards the issues of globalisation, the european union, and the commission in particular, cannot ignore the situation of the financial markets, the continued existence of tax havens, the inability of us to bring to the table the issue of exchange rates between the euro, the chinese yuan and the dollar. i think that we are waiting for ambitious initiatives from the european union to ensure that these financial markets work in the service of the economy and society, and not the other way around. i remember the debates we had on this in this parliament at the beginning of this parliamentary term. all of that seems to have disappeared from the concerns of both sides. in this connection, it seems to me important that you should relaunch an initiative with the support of parliament.
the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. the commission, for example, seems to be taking over, in full accordance with earlier initiatives by commissioner frattini, the responsibility for legal immigration for work, which in my view is essentially a matter for the member states. the commission is thus imposing on the european citizens its response to the demographic challenges, which i consider particularly questionable, namely a new, very massive import of labour from non-european countries without the slightest democratic support or agreement from our own european citizens. let me be very clear about that: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. and this at a time - i want to emphasize this - when in europe itself there are already many millions and millions of former immigrants of non-european origin, some of them already of the third and even fourth generation, who are still unemployed and are developing into a welfare proletariat which is hardly integrated, let alone assimilated. it is also very striking that the commission feels it must intervene in the educational policies of the member states. the communication on communicating europe in partnership to which this work programme refers literally states that the commission will examine how best schools in the european member states can teach core skills to their students and how best school communities in the member states can prepare young people to become responsible citizens in line with fundamental european values. i ask myself: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. is big brother europe that close? the commission claims to put the european citizens first, but in reality it is very often acting like the emperor's tavern. often finished with the finger-pointing and the inquisitiveness of a frustrated teacher. the commission president barroso himself told the dutch people in their own country at the beginning of this year that they had had enough, that they were not bothered by the referendum and that it was time to accept the principles of the european constitution. it is the same great european democrats who are doing everything possible to ensure that our citizens have no say in this new pseudo-constitution or in the possible accession of turkey. and as far as my own country is concerned, barroso is allowing himself to be taught, along with other european mandarins, about the problems between flanders and wallonia, which are not hindered by any knowledge of the case file. in conclusion, and i am afraid i am not saying this for the first time in this chamber, and i am afraid i am not saying it for the last time either, this official europe does not seem to tolerate democracy, does not tolerate subsidiarity, does not tolerate a correct and clear and sharp division of powers between the member states and europe. it is therefore not surprising to me that the so-called reform treaty, apart from some cosmetic changes, is identical to the former constitution and is therefore only a disguised version of it, a totally illegible, obscure and veiled document which is intended to put the normal functioning of a democracy controlled by citizens out of the question.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. transport and mobility are of course also a major theme in the 2008 programme. the challenges are, of course, accordingly clear. between economic efficiency, the impact on the environment and, last but not least, the rights and safety of the users, we must also set priorities for 2008, which we can only briefly discuss here. security checks at airports, for example, were then done under the pressure of a certain urgency. we in the epp group believe that this directive could be reviewed. passenger rights: the subject could be completed by looking at passenger rights in international bus transport in more detail. this would complete the whole issue of passenger rights in europe, both in air, rail and road transport. transeuropean networks: we have discussed this a great deal in the last few months. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we believe that clear decisions must be taken on the 2008 transeuropean networks and that crossborder lines must be given absolute priority in order to eliminate the so-called bottlenecks and, accordingly, to ensure that the so-called multimodal transport principle can be applied effectively. a rapid decision should and could be taken by taking account of those countries which have already provided for co-financing and where the projects have technically been successful.
i am sorry to say that i find the chapter in your legislative and work programme on europe as a global player to be extremely disappointing. there is, of course, little legislation in the field of foreign policy, as we all know, but there is so much more work to be done and i think that what is written about it, with a few exceptions, is actually very lacking. there is also apparently a problem in understanding the fundamental problem facing the european union when it comes to foreign policy. it is clear from the text that europe is the world leader in the fight against poverty, the promotion of sustainable development, human rights and governance and so on and so forth. all of that may be true, but it is not perceived as such by the rest of the world, and certainly not by the member states of the union, and i am particularly sorry that it is not. what is a fact is that the european union is the main provider of all those things that are listed. but to turn that into providing that into being seen as a global leader, as an entity that exercises global leadership, that's something else entirely. i find the proposals for programmes relating to africa to be a step in the right direction. i think, for example, that the proposal to launch a trilateral dialogue between the union, africa and china is very hopeful and positive. i also think that the whole point of working harder on economic development with africa is equally important. so, good motives, but still too little effect of a just ambition.
i have reservations about the schmitt report on sport in education. i would just like to say for the record that this is of course not because i am against sports in education, nor because i am against schools of all kinds of classes offering physical education in their curriculum. - no, it's the opposite. i naturally also agree that sports teachers should be treated on an equal footing with all other teachers. i have abstained because i believe that sport and education, and sport education in general, should absolutely remain the exclusive competence of the member states and that europe should not necessarily be putting its finger up everywhere. what is equally disturbing to me about this report is that it once again over-emphasizes the multicultural society and the so-called gender issue, and that this does fit in with the political correctness of the report. the european union, for god's sake, should remain within its powers.
in writing. the need to make young europeans love sport has certainly never been felt more than today. the reason is simple: in the face of the sedentary lifestyle that our children are exposed to, it is more than urgent to offer an alternative to snacking and gaming consoles! i therefore fully share the message conveyed by mr pál schmitt in his own-initiative report for more compulsory physical education, starting from primary school. a message that sounds like a double-slit reminder. first, school is the natural place to instill important social values such as honesty, solidarity, teamwork, tolerance and fair play from an early age. values to distill and share with the family too and above all! secondly, in the face of the dual threat, that of public health with the surge in obesity among our young people, and that of society with a school system that is made to measure, the european union and the 27 member states must react and rethink the relationship between sport and education. and the battle is far from won. the average child watches two hours of television a day - far more than the 109 minutes a week spent in physical education classes at school!
mr president, following the example of the european ministers of the interior, this parliament is giving the green light to the extension of schengen to the member states which joined in 2004. it will be a relief to many. but let me be a little less euphoric about schengen itself, because it is a fact: the european policy of open borders has also significantly strengthened the power of international organized crime. this created a schengen spillover effect. the system of open borders has always required a further transfer of powers, an ever closer police cooperation which will lead to a fully fledged european police and prosecutorial and criminal justice system, with its positive but also its negative sides. but the open-border policy also leaves the individual member states powerless to deal with the waves of regularisation of illegal immigrants that have been decided in certain countries - i am thinking in particular of spain and italy - waves of regularisation which in turn attract more and more illegal immigrants to the insufficiently protected european borders. the so-called european solidarity is completely lacking here. we must have the courage to say that too.
vice-president of the commission. mr. deputy, i'm afraid i can't agree with you entirely. as you know, we had the great european tourism conference in portugal just a few days ago, where all these questions were discussed at length. there were 500 of the leading experts from all over europe, and i did not get the impression at this tourism conference that the european tourism industry or the european member states were worried that europe might be less attractive as a tourism destination. the figures available to us do not support what you have said, mr president, that we are facing a particularly difficult situation here. i repeat: fluctuations in exchange rates have an effect on prices, but what europe offers to visitors from outside europe is not primarily determined by price. the decision to travel to europe is not a decision primarily influenced by price, but by the quality of what is offered in europe. however, i agree with you that we must pay close attention to the burdens on the european tourism industry and that we must ensure that the framework conditions are set in such a way that this industry can really take advantage of the enormous potential for growth and employment that it has.
member of the commission. thank you very much for your question, which gives me the opportunity to highlight the situation in the maldives. as president maumoon abdul gayoom pointed out at the recent un summit, the maldives, which is only 1.5 metres above sea level, is in danger of disappearing due to the constant rise in sea levels as a result of global warming. this has forced more than 300,000 refugees to emigrate. therefore, as the honourable member has very rightly pointed out, inviting the president of the maldives is an opportunity to highlight the danger facing the maldivian people. the maldives has also always actively contributed to international conferences on the least developed countries and small island developing states. this commitment was confirmed when the maldives participated in the third united nations conference on the least developed countries convened by the european parliament in 2001, and even more so when the maldives assumed the important role of chairing the panel of experts at the united nations framework convention on climate change. as regards the political situation in the maldives, in accordance with the statement by the presidency of the council of the european union, the commission welcomes the recent political developments in the maldives and expresses its satisfaction with the reforms implemented so far. the publication of the roadmap for the reform programme in 2006 was indeed good news. next year, in 2008, the elections will be an important step towards more democracy. the european commission is ready to support the reform process, including by offering special assistance for the 2008 elections. we are of course very vigilant when it comes to human rights. the commission was satisfied with the recent improvements and, inter alia, the release last year of two prominent activists: jennifer latheef and mohammed nasheed.

on behalf of the pse group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking mr cornillet for the work he has done on this report and for the intense debate he has had in our committee on development. i would also like to thank the commissioner for the relevant analysis he has made in his introduction to this debate. the report on humanitarian aid rightly focuses on the protection of humanitarian personnel and access to victims, which are indeed the two major challenges that humanitarian aid is facing and will increasingly face. in this connection, i would like to recall and emphasize, as the commissioner has done, that changes in the way the armed forces intervene can contribute to the tarnishing of the image of humanitarian workers. the confusion caused by the use of military personnel, sometimes to accompany humanitarian workers, inevitably leads to a loss of the immunity which ngos and their teams on the ground have enjoyed until now. furthermore, in order to be able to reach out to victims and claim a certain freedom of expression, it is of paramount importance that the organization does not appear to be a party to these conflicts or to be dependent on the politics of the organization's home nation. however, recent developments in conflict areas have made the perception of the position of ngos more complex. in darfur, in particular, several international ngos, including médecins du monde, are reporting a steady decline in their ability to reach victims outside displacement camps and an increase in violent actions against the population and humanitarian personnel. their room for manoeuvre is based both on the ngos' reaffirmation of their independence, both vis-à-vis the armed forces engaged on the ground and vis-à-vis any political position. it should also be noted that an increasing share of the financial resources of humanitarian ngos comes from institutional and, in particular, european funding. this situation can also contribute to a form of distrust of these ngos, which are not immune from being confused with the geostrategic positions of the donor countries or the union. for this reason, i would stress here that the increase in public intervention, which we welcome and which is an excellent thing, cannot, however, lead to the instrumentalisation of these ngos. in the last ten years, more than a thousand humanitarians have been killed in more than five hundred attacks on relief operations for civilian populations. if sufficient care is not taken to guarantee the independence of ngos, there will undoubtedly be a decline in their capacity to intervene and, consequently, a vast reduction in the areas of application of international humanitarian law. the media logic is such that the whole world is now focusing on the ark of zoé, an amateur association which is quite marginal in relation to the vast majority of humanitarian operations which are carried out by recognized structures, staffed by personnel whose courage and human and professional quality must be emphasized. this isolated case cannot therefore have the effect of discrediting all those involved in humanitarian aid. on the contrary, it reminds us that it is absolutely essential that humanitarian operations be conducted within the strict framework of international humanitarian law and un security council resolutions. we therefore welcome the fact that this european consensus, which will be adopted at the next session, confirms our request to set up within the council a formal structure specifically responsible for humanitarian issues and to review annually the consensus and the accompanying action plan.

president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is good that the commission is paying attention to the external dimension as a new element in the lisbon strategy, but above all let us not forget that globalisation also has consequences for our own internal european policy. it is true that the lisbon strategy has contributed to economic growth and jobs, but it is also true that not everyone has benefited from it. globalisation has greatly increased inequality and the gap between the educated and the uneducated in europe. i am therefore pleased that the commission and the employment council want to pay more attention to the social dimension in the future, because too many people - low-skilled, disabled, older workers, migrants - still do not have access to a good education and good job opportunities. six million young people leave school without a diploma, 72 million people live on the margins of society in poverty and europe has 14 million working poor. economic prosperity, ladies and gentlemen, must be for the benefit of all. that's why i want to put three extra points in the spotlight. first of all: it is clear that the new generation of policy instruments for lisbon must focus much more strongly on social inclusion, equal opportunities, the fight against poverty and good social protection. the social dimension must be re-established in the integrated guidelines. secondly: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the eu's employment and training policy. quality work is not an automatic consequence of economic growth, and we need a clear commitment from the member states. thirdly: there is much more to be done in partnership. a good strategy for growth, jobs and social inclusion also requires input from national parliaments, local and regional authorities, social partners and civil society organisations. that is why my group believes that it cannot be business as usual for the next generation of lisbon instruments. there are reasons, mr vice-president of the commission, for updating the lisbon package on key points.

mr president, i am glad to hear that there is a european consensus on humanitarian aid and that no sensible person is opposed to aid to countries which really need it. but let us also be frank about the fact that there seems to be no european consensus on a code of conduct for dealing with dictators. the british boycott of the eu-africa summit in response to mugabe's participation is simply ignored by other member states and humanitarian tragedies very often arise - everyone knows that - from wars or from criminal governance, as in zimbabwe. the reality is and remains that africa is the scene of bloody conflicts and that africans spend more on arms than they receive in development aid. it is equally true that states with a democratic system, where the rulers and the administration are not above the law, almost never go to war with each other. if dictators like mugabe can attend a european summit, europe will not be credible on human rights and democracy. it is because of this ambiguity that i have abstained from voting on the cornillet report.
in writing. i welcome the adoption by the plenary of the report by cristina gutiérrez-cortines, which aims to establish a unified and coherent european policy on soil protection. protecting soils has become an eu priority because of the increasing degradation of european soils. the reason is rapid urbanization, the proliferation of contaminated sites (now numbering over 2 million), and intensive agriculture, which has used pesticides and nitrate fertilizers for the past 50 years. i think that the 225 members who voted against this directive were either nationalist zealists or at least they were not concerned with the concerns of the citizens! europe has a real added value to bring to soil protection where only nine member states have legislation. the directive leaves a wide margin of choice to the member states with two specific objectives to be achieved in the distant future: five and seven years after the transposition of the text, respectively, the member states will have to draw up an inventory of contaminated sites and adopt a national strategy for remediation. i think that is called respecting the principles of flexibility and subsidiarity!

on behalf of the pse group. madam president, commissioner špidla, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by welcoming this communication, which is indeed, as you said, commissioner, marked by real pragmatism on the part of the commission and which today allows us to debate a problem which is of concern not only to the citizens of the union's southern partners but also to our fellow citizens who often legitimately ask themselves the question of the effectiveness of the european union's development aid. in this particular case, the key message of our resolution is one sentence and should be accepted by all, regardless of political divisions. it is a matter of proposing the establishment of a simplified cooperation system which will enable the commission and the member states to provide more rapidly than at present the aid they have promised to partner countries which are experiencing a particularly serious crisis - i will not dwell on this since our colleague mr deva has just explained this at length - aid without which the populations of these states are seeing their chance of survival diminish further every day. once this aid has been approved, which is no small matter, the complexity and length of the current procedures for the delivery of european aid can lead to even more dramatic situations that directly harm the beneficiary populations. take the example of burundi, a small country to which i chaired the parliamentary observation mission for the last elections two years ago and to which i shall have the pleasure of returning in a few days. the european union has done a very important and remarkable job in supporting the electoral process and the democratization of the institutions, after more than ten years of bloody conflicts. once the institutions and the new democratically elected government were in place, they were quickly confronted with the reality and the needs to be met, first and foremost, by the people, whether in health, education or agriculture. there is no shortage of concrete projects in these different sectors, but european aid is absolutely crucial for their implementation. it is not only crucial for meeting the urgent needs of the people, but also for enabling the still fragile public authorities to begin a process of reconstruction without which there is a great risk that the country will quickly sink back into chaos. two years later, commissioner, the budgetary aid announced by the union has still not arrived. what is true of the great lakes region is also true of other regions of the world. that is why, madam president, subject to a clear definition of the conditions they must meet in order to qualify for it, all the states in the world which are in a situation of serious fragility and with which the european union cooperates should be able to rely on an exceptional system which guarantees rapid and effective intervention.
the report, mr president, with its rather cryptic title 'balance of social reality', was adopted almost unanimously in the committee on social affairs, with only one vote against, and that should not just a catalogue of good intentions, that is fine, but the european institutions have been trying for years to take over the social policy field, and that is not a good thing.
i have not approved the coelho report, mr president, mainly because i want to address the many pain points of the open borders policy of the schengen agreement. the schengen system is, or falls, in the form of strict, watertight controls at the external borders, which clearly do not exist, and a strict, coordinated approach to crime in all member states, which is only too limited. finally, all schengen countries must also have a strict immigration policy, which is completely contrary to the massive waves of regularization that we have had in recent years in quite a few member states. in these circumstances, i cannot, as a human being and as a politician, be happy, because i am no longer checked at internal borders, since criminals and illegal immigrants are no longer checked, and this is bad for our society.
in writing. i would like to express my vote on the report on the recovery plan for bluefin tuna in the eastern atlantic and the mediterranean. given the really critical state of bluefin tuna stocks, the eu is responding by means of this regulation: gradual reduction of catch quotas, limitation of fishing periods, or even reinforcement of controls to prevent illegal fishing. i support these proposals. i am a little pessimistic, though. firstly, because scientists are already saying that the provisions of the regulation are not sufficient to ensure the replenishment of stocks. they argued that quotas should be set well above the annual catch rates set by the international commission for the conservation of atlantic tunas (icsta). i tend to believe them, and that is why i supported amendment no 13 tabled by the greens. secondly, given the presence in the mediterranean of fleets from third countries such as libya, turkey, tunisia, japan and china, which are not members of the iccat, it is clear that the regulation under consideration will only have its effect there if these countries adhere to the provisions it lays down, which is not guaranteed.
in writing. europe will not be able to tackle the growing poverty and global warming - the great challenges of globalisation - if it continues to prioritise liberalisation. it is free trade that is creating these problems. globalisation creates the illusion that the general level of prosperity in the world is gradually increasing, but at the same time i see that the gap between the rich and the poor in the member states is steadily widening. liberalisation is also the cause of the great environmental disaster we are heading for if we do not take decisive and enforceable measures against global warming soon. it is irresponsible and criminal not to pursue a more vigorous policy in this area. investing in an energy-efficient economy and creating jobs in that direction is a promising story, however. the polluter pays principle is too often ignored. as a green mep, i miss this move. if europe is to be a single market where people, goods, services and capital move freely, then europe must also set high social and environmental standards which protect and set an example for the rest of the world. the proposed text remains vague and superficial and once again typifies the commission's policy.

mr president, no one can deny that citizens in europe must be armed with fundamental rights and freedoms, both vis-à-vis their own state and vis-à-vis the european union. a europe without rights and freedoms would no longer be europe. but that's not the issue here today. the citizens are already sufficiently protected against their national authorities by their own national constitutions and by the echr. the individual european can also assert fundamental freedoms and rights vis-à-vis the european institutions, in accordance with the settled case law of the court of justice. the point is that the signing of this charter marks a new step towards a federal europe. as in the federal states, they wanted a european bill of rights. the difference between the two is that this charter goes far beyond a list of the classical rights and freedoms. sometimes it seems like a list of all kinds of socioeconomic promises. so the flag does not cover the cargo at all.
the european union is not a political entity, but a political entity, and it is not a political entity, but a political entity. the european institutions are increasingly - and rightly so in my view - mistrusted by our citizens who do not really accept that many important decisions which directly affect their lives are taken in cenakels which are no longer controlled by anything or anyone. i am not saying that the european parliament should be the only one to have the power to decide on this. i'll give you two examples. first example: the text of the new european treaties to be signed in lisbon. everyone knows that this is a slightly modified version of the european constitution. mr zapatero himself said: not one essential element has changed. the text worked in democratic referendums, in france and in the netherlands, it was voted down and now it is all laughed at. in the best case, we might be able to vote again here and there until the eurocrats are satisfied, but in reality democratic decisions are simply thrown out of the referendum. i am afraid that in this way europe will gradually become more and more a very small club, which will govern like a superstate, which will not tolerate participation and therefore cannot be called a democracy. the same applies to the way in which turkey's potential accession to the european union is being handled. it is not what our citizens want, on the contrary, because turkey is not a european country - not culturally, not geographically, not religiously, not in any way - and yet the commission and the council give a damn about the opinion of the majority of our citizens on this. instead of a debate on the future of the union, we should actually be having a debate on the restoration of democracy in the institutions of this european union.
mr president, the fact that we are welcoming the spanish prime minister to our house today may give the wrong impression that we are still in the period of reflection on the constitution, because that is what this series of prime ministers was designed to do. but fortunately that period is over and there is now agreement on a good reform treaty. of course you are welcome here, mr prime minister, because even now it is still worthwhile to talk about the future of europe. more than ever, because the treaty is not an end point, but a new beginning. it is not an end in itself either; it is a tool that we must use effectively and that offers us a perspective for better governance, for more democracy, for the realisation of values such as freedom, security, prosperity and the further development of our social market economy in an open globalised world. and believe me, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, these are the same objectives pursued by many people in belgium in their desire for state reform. it is also purely instrumental, it is also aimed at achieving the objectives which we share with many others here.
the european parliament, the council and the commission have been working together for a long time to ensure that the budgetary authority is able to take account of the needs of the citizens of the union. this is only 1% of the gross national product of the 27 member states, but it is still a lot of money. it is money that is being hoisted by you and me and by all taxpayers and therefore also deserves serious scrutiny. that is one of the key tasks of the european parliament. for the 13th time in a row, i note that the court of auditors refuses to guarantee the absolute legality and regularity of this expenditure. this is not a factual situation. there's a place for some indignation here. it is a serious warning to the commission, but also to the governments of the member states, to act here. the belgian member of the court of auditors, karel pinxten, wrote in the newspapers de tijd and echo de la bourse, and i quote him: "such a reservation by a corporate auditor or an external auditor for a publicly traded company with the size of the european union budget would send shockwaves through the financial markets". he's got a lot of sense, and we can't let that go to waste. what are the bottlenecks? agriculture, still the most important budget line, at over eur 50 billion. the positive developments there are undeniable, especially as a result of the application of the integrated administration and control system. but, ladies and gentlemen, let us also be clear: some member states, and greece in particular, refuse to join the system. so this money is managed by the administrations of the member states and i think we should show a clear red card to greece here. we must reiterate our demand that payments to greece in the form of agricultural payments be suspended until greece finally gets on track. the second problem in agriculture is the payment - mr weber has pointed this out, mr kallas has referred back to it - of agricultural funds to golf clubs, railway companies, horse breeding companies, also to landowners, who apparently are not farmers and who are going to be running on money from the agricultural budget. so these are usually people who have a noble or a royal title. there's a little bit of a "what if" game going on here. mr weber says it is true, mr kallas says it is not true. there has been a hearing in the committee on budgetary control of the european parliament, mrs fischer boel, and the commissioner for agriculture says that this is all exaggerated, she is minimizing the whole thing. i would like to hear mr kallas' reply, mr weber's reply, and the state of play. can the court of auditors make this hard? is she sticking with that charge? the second major budget item is still the structural funds. the court of auditors' report says that 12% of this money should not have been paid. this is not possible. we should be there. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. 80% of european money is managed by them. i therefore repeat the demand of our parliament, of the plenary, that they sign a declaration stating that european money has been spent correctly and thus also accept their political responsibility. denmark, the netherlands, the united kingdom have done so. what about the other governments of the member states? where is belgium, where is france, where is germany? should they not finally take political responsibility too?
the leinen report, mr president, is yet another manifestation of the cancer of financial self-management which this european union is becoming for all political parties. it is particularly characteristic in my view that even european election campaigns are now being financed by taxpayers, which of course means the campaigns of eurocratic parties, because as we know, the bar is set high and it is virtually impossible for eurocritics to meet the conditions for financing. this is yet another theft of taxpayers' money being sold in this house under the guise of democratization. the same people who are using today, the same parties that are using today from the pot of community money, are the same people who simply ignored democratic referendums in france and the netherlands and then in denmark and still ignore them today. these are the same people who say that our citizens should not have a say in the prospect of turkish accession. so that they will for god's sake keep their mouths shut about democratization and not disguise their financial wolf-lust as service to society.
i voted against the roure report for a number of fundamental reasons. one is that legal protection against racist acts can be perfectly achieved by the member states, in other words, action by the european union in this area is fundamentally contrary to the principle of subsidiarity. the framework directive is also an attack on freedom of expression. while it is acceptable to combat racism - and i mean racism based on specific incitement or violence - it is unacceptable to confuse this concept with a legitimate public discourse, such as opposition to mass immigration, opposition to islamization or defence of national identity.
i voted against the leinen report on the financing of european political parties because it is unacceptable that european funds should flow to existing european political associations and that this should discriminate against movements which do not feel the need to organise themselves at european level. it is also very cynical to propose that european funding of european electoral campaigns and of all kinds of european political foundations is a strengthening of european democracy. because, as my colleague has already said, it is the same european political parties that have always called for the french and dutch votes against the european constitution to be wiped out and for the ratification process to continue. i have never heard the same parties advocating a referendum on turkish membership. but that would be a strengthening of european democracy. but funding seems to be available only to those parties which want to be politically correct in europe. that is why we must vote against this funding.
mr president, since i have already said all the bad things i think about the first leinen report, i would like to add my support to what my colleague philip claeys said earlier, which was also a reply to the strange intervention of mr bourlanges. there is a complete confusion arising between the concepts of racism on the one hand and legitimate public discourse on the other. this is also evident from what the european observatory against racism said when it claimed that islamophobia is a new form of discrimination and that the danish cartoon riots should be tackled with a law against blasphemy. all this is very dangerous, now that we are finding that normal public discourse in favour of islam and islamization can be interpreted as inciting hatred against muslims. for all these different reasons, we consider this whole discourse unacceptable and have rejected both the leinen and the roure reports.
i voted against the leinen report because it expands the system of financing european political parties, which i consider unacceptable in itself. in addition to numerical criteria for eligibility, there are also ideological criteria which mean that parties who go against the grain do not stand a chance. the established parties and political groups decide who gets money. there is no possibility of appeal against the decisions taken. what we also have to establish is the stealthy decision-making, the typical salamite technique used here. the principle of european party financing was launched cautiously a few years ago. it was then explicitly stated that the money could not be used to finance electoral campaigns. what's going on now? election campaigns will be funded. on top of that, there will be money going to all kinds of political foundations. in such circumstances, mr president, you will understand that i voted against this self-service shop.
i do not want to create any misunderstanding. my vote against the leinen report on fundamental rights should not be taken to mean that i am opposed to the granting of very fundamental rights and freedoms to european citizens. i have, by the way, intervened many times in this hemisphere and beyond to protect free speech and to fight the mules. the point is that this report is not about fundamental freedoms. fortunately, the european citizen is already armed with very fundamental rights and very fundamental freedoms, including against abuse in his own country and abuse at the level of the european union. the reason why i voted against this is because making this charter binding is actually a very big step forward towards a federal europe. i am in favour of a confederal europe of free nations and therefore voted against this report on fundamental rights, without, of course, being against fundamental rights.
member of the commission. mr president, this will be a very encouraging start for me. i am glad to announce that the commission is ready to accept the majority of parliament’s amendments. as this debate has shown, there are currently negotiations under way that will address your concerns. a compromise could open the door to future development. the commission supports the debate that is still ongoing and sincerely hopes that a solution will be found that is acceptable to a broad majority within this house, as well as to the member states represented in the council. however, it should not be forgotten that the inclusion of new topics in a census takes in-depth and long-haul preparation. i am confident that the correct balance will be found between the very important reporting on the eu population and housing, and the cost and effort of collecting the data. i would like to pay special attention to the question by ms schroedter. the main point was that this legislation does not sufficiently assure data confidentiality. my answer is that data confidentiality is being taken most seriously by eurostat and all national statistical offices. there is framework legislation on data confidentiality on the national as well as on the european level. the proposal on censuses does not violate or modify any of this legislation. in contrast, it fully respects all provisions, be they national or european. there is no need to repeat established framework legislation on data confidentiality within this specific proposal on censuses. no data transmitted to eurostat allows insight into the data of any particular individual person. it is anonymous data. moreover, the legislation in this current draft allows only the transmission of aggregated data, not of microdata.
member of the commission. mr president, i would like to thank parliament for its continued interest in product safety issues, and for this opportunity to give you a short presentation on the first results of the product safety stocktaking, released on 22 november 2007. i attended the imco committee meeting on 26 november and i am now pleased to address the european parliament meeting in plenary. my three starting points and principles are still valid and can be confirmed: there can be no compromise on consumer safety; we believe in open markets and fair competition; and we want to work in international partnership with the chinese and the americans. these stocktaking results indicate that the regulatory framework is fit for the purpose, if and when it is properly applied. in particular, the rapid alert system rapex is working efficiently and ensuring that dangerous products are recalled from the market throughout the european union. the stocktaking report nonetheless identifies certain scope for improvements, in preventive actions and international cooperation as well as in enhanced enforcement. some envisaged improvements are already at an advanced stage towards becoming concrete actions, as is notably the case of the revision of the toy safety directive. i intend to propose to my colleagues in the commission that we adopt a temporary measure to require that warnings be given on magnetic toys, pending revision of the standard, to address the risks that these toys could pose. we have this right under article 13 of the general product safety directive. i hope that this measure will become effective soon, but i am also aware that it is just a partial stop-gap solution until the relevant standard addresses the risk associated with magnets comprehensively. the commission is assisting the member states’ market surveillance authorities to identify and share best practices with a view to improved market surveillance. in october 2007, member states reported on initiatives for better cooperation with economic operators and on specific surveillance campaigns on toys. the commission intends to publish comparative enforcement capacity data on the consumer scoreboard in the first quarter of 2008. the commission also continues concretely to reinforce the market surveillance capacity of the member states by participating in the financing of well-designed joint market surveillance projects (which in 2007 will receive eur 1.3 million in community funding). the protection of consumers against dangerous products requires a consistent level of protection not only within the community, but also at the eu external borders. in addition to actions to improve protection within the european union, various actions are under way to strengthen protection at borders. recent major changes to eu customs legislation will help in identifying high-risk consignments for control. secure customs exchange mechanisms will also enable rapid action to be taken when information becomes available on new types of dangerous products. cooperation between these two networks, in practical terms, is being stepped up through regular exchanges of alerts between the rapex system and the customs authorities’ ‘risk information form’ system. the latter draws border inspectors’ attention to specific, potentially dangerous, cargo. the commission has highlighted traceability, which is a very important area for further improvement. statistics show that products of unknown origin notified through rapex were down for the first time in october 2007, standing at 3% as compared to 17% in 2006. the commission is currently assessing, with the help of the member states, how to ensure that this improvement is not just temporary. the commission has already included, in the ‘internal market for goods’ legislation package, a provision requiring economic operators to have available the identity of their supplier, which should be helpful for market surveillance interventions once this legislation enters into force. the commission has also asked what china could do about traceability, and welcomes the initiatives in china to require the bar-coding, at factory level, of certain categories of high-risk product. the commission also consistently highlights the responsibility of economic operators for product safety. i welcome the commitment from the industry to work on measures to build consumer confidence. we have agreed on what i call a ‘safety pact’. i will also send experts to carry out a study of business safety measures in the supply chain, and report further on this in the first quarter of 2008, because in the era of globalisation we are only as strong as the weakest part of our chain. i also find it important to communicate with the citizens of europe on product safety issues, and to listen to their concerns. this is essential because both you and i want to respond ever better to their queries and needs. i found my recent web chat under the topic ‘have a safe christmas’ (which i very much enjoyed) most enlightening and engaging, and answered nearly 50 interesting questions. finally, our product safety concerns were communicated to the chinese leaders at the eu-china summit on 28 november 2007. the leaders on both sides shared the view that a high level of product safety is key to consumer confidence and reciprocal trade. to this end, the competent authorities have established a comprehensive cooperative relationship. both sides are willing to continue and deepen the dialogue, regularly exchange information and aim at measurable, continuous improvements. likewise, the commission has agreed with the united states government, in the context of the trans-atlantic economic council, to enhance our cooperation on import safety issues. i believe this report to you has shown that i and all my colleagues in the commission have taken the necessary steps to enhance consumer product safety, in association with all the relevant actors, in the most effective, proportionate and committed manner. i would like once again to thank parliament for our many inspiring debates on this issue.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe-de group. president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is of course no coincidence that we in the ppe-de group have called for this topical debate just before christmas. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we want to make it clear that we want a high level of consumer protection and that we want to keep the attention paid to the smallest, the most vulnerable consumer - the child - high on the agenda. commissioner, i am glad to hear your statements here. we note that you have already done a great deal of work in the commission and we would naturally like to see the member states encouraged to do their part. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the market is not distorted. we also know, commissioner, that you personally make every effort to protect the consumer. we are very grateful for this effort and we agree with you that we should not invent yet another new approach to toy safety, but that we should close the gaps in the existing system. a few things i'd like to have clarified: you have said - and you have now referred to it - that the chinese government is also working very hard. you announced some time ago that they were going to set up a kind of chinese rapex system. perhaps you could clarify that a little more. secondly, i wonder whether there are any real agreements with the toy industry or whether each member state has its own way of doing this. i would also like to know why we are still waiting for the new toy directive or the commission's proposal to amend the existing toy directive? we were expecting it around this time. apparently there's a delay. perhaps you could explain to us how that is? you 're absolutely right about the other thing . the single market is also for consumers. if consumers do not have confidence, they will not make sufficient use of that market. this is a very, very important file.

the debate on the protection of designs for visible replacement parts has been going on for almost a whole generation of members of parliament. it has been discussed for 17 years, but so far we have not been able to reach a final agreement on this important economic legislation and to bring the laws of the member states into sufficient harmony. we are now three years into the launch of the current commission proposal and finally we are going to vote on the first reading tomorrow. the report by mr lehne points the way in this respect. it respects the intellectual property of the design of the assembled product as a whole and at the same time it gives us room for the proper functioning of the internal market for replacement parts. the current fragmentation of the internal market is no longer tenable. it deprives the producer of the components - the independent producer - of the opportunity to benefit from the economies of scale of the internal market. it deprives independent repairers of the choice of supplier and consumers of the opportunity or benefit of the downward pressure on prices which is normally the result of effective competition. and now that we have removed the last obstacle, namely safety, with the new directive - malcolm harbour also mentioned it earlier, the new directive on vehicle typeapproval - it is time to make a final decision. i do not even need the five-year transitional period, but since i too have learned during all these years - 16 of the 17 years of debate - that politics is the art of the feasible, i can live with those five years, with that compromise. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community. let us opt for the single market. this is an aspect of the internal market which allows us to demonstrate very clearly that it is also in the interests of consumers. let's make a choice for the consumer.

madam president, the resumption of fighting between rebel and government forces in chad has left hundreds of people dead and wounded and has led to renewed tensions in the border region of sudan and out as quickly as possible. i would also like to stress that no peacekeeping mission in eastern chad and northern central african republic will be effective without a comprehensive reconciliation process involving the whole region. it is already known that the presence of eufor at the gates of sudan will make it more difficult for the rebels to move freely across the borders and will therefore cause some attacks to be relocated. the urgent and indispensable support that the european union has committed itself to provide could therefore also prove to be insufficient very quickly. it is therefore also essential that the union should make every effort now to promote the resumption of peace talks at regional level, in partnership with all the parties to the conflict and the various representatives of the international community. in conclusion, i would like to stress that we consider the neutrality of the european force to be particularly important and strategic in this respect. we know that rebels have already threatened to target it if it is merely a disguised french mission, so we ask the other member states to participate massively in the composition of this force so that it is clear to everyone that it is indeed a european force.
president. - the european and african summit took place in lisbon last weekend and was intended to lay the foundations for a new partnership between europe and africa. a partnership which, according to the joint declaration of the european parliament and the pan-african parliament, was to be based on shared values and principles, such as democracy, human rights and good governance. but unfortunately, the attitude of europe towards figures like mugabe in zimbabwe and gaddafi in libya - to name just two - both of whom were greeted with the red carpet - proves that there is still a gap between declarations of principle and reality. the situation in chad today also shows abundantly that a large part of africa is far from ready to be part of this so-called community of values. so if we as europeans are to maintain the credibility of our discourse on human rights and not be guided solely by realpolitik, intervention must indeed send a clear signal that lawlessness and arbitrariness must be replaced as soon as possible by the most basic democratic principles. unfortunately, the assessment of chad applies to many other regimes in africa. chad's not the only one sick in this bed.
madam president, just to correct what you said before the last speech: the socialist group spoke through me, i spoke as the author and on behalf of the socialist group. i would not like to give the impression that we are not interested in this question.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, the discussion we are having here today on saudi arabia, and in particular the deplorable legal situation of women, should really be extended to a large part of the islamic world. because only a few, like the dutch ayaan hirsi ali, dare to say it out loud. the discrimination and subjugation of women is ingrained in the sacred texts of islam itself. discrimination against women in saudi arabia and almost the entire muslim world finds its basis and justification in the koran. that is the first fundamental truth we must dare to face in this debate. we have seen this recently in sudan, where a 54-year-old teacher was sentenced to 15 days in prison for mentioning a teddy bear as mohammed, before being pardoned under pressure from international diplomacy. in saudi arabia, a 20-year-old woman is gang-raped, but is herself convicted. it couldn't be more symbolic. but of course the saudis are allies of the us and they're allowed a little more. because, like china, saudi arabia is a rich country and we dare to be a little less principled when the great principles we have talked about so often this week are at stake. europe should therefore also ask washington to put its full weight behind the improvement of the situation in saudi arabia.
